### [1] [A GPU-Accelerated Hybrid Method for a Class of Multi-Depot Vehicle Routing Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05208)
*Zhenyu Lei,Jin-Kao Hao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a hybrid algorithm for multi-depot vehicle routing problems that combines a learning-driven crossover, a multi-penalty feasible-infeasible search framework, dedicated depot local search operators, and a GPU-accelerated tensor-based implementation. The algorithm achieves state-of-the-art performance on large-scale instances across three MDVRP variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MDVRPs are prevalent in practice but computationally challenging due to their inherent complexity, motivating the need for more effective and scalable solution methods.

Method: A hybrid algorithm integrating: (1) a learning-driven, diversity-controlled route-exchange crossover; (2) a multi-depot-supported feasible-and-infeasible search framework guided by a multi-penalty evaluation function; (3) two dedicated depot-related local search operators; and (4) an enhanced version using tensor-based GPU acceleration with a multi-move update strategy.

Result: The proposed algorithms are highly competitive with state-of-the-art methods on benchmark instances of three MDVRP variants, especially for large-scale instances.

Conclusion: The hybrid algorithm effectively addresses the complexity of MDVRPs, achieving superior scalability and solution quality, particularly for large-scale problems.

Abstract: Multi-depot vehicle routing problems (MDVRPs) are prevalent in a variety of practical applications. However, they are computationally challenging to solve due to their inherent complexity. This paper proposes an effective hybrid algorithm for a class of MDVRPs. The algorithm integrates a learning-driven, diversity-controlled route-exchange crossover and a multi-depot-supported feasible-and-infeasible search framework guided by a multi-penalty evaluation function. Two dedicated depot-related local search operators are incorporated to further strengthen the search capability in multi-depot settings. To improve computational efficiency and scalability, an enhanced version of the algorithm is developed that uses a tensor-based GPU acceleration combined with a novel multi-move update strategy. Extensive computational experiments on benchmark instances of three MDVRP variants show that the proposed algorithms are highly competitive with state-of-the-art methods, especially for large-scale instances.

</details>


### [2] [Topology-Driven Anti-Entanglement Control for Soft Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05236)
*Haoyang Le,Shengxuan Wang,Mohan Chen,Shuo Feng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a topology-driven multi-agent reinforcement learning (TD-MARL) framework to coordinate multiple soft robots for anti-winding control in constrained environments. It uses centralized learning with shared topological states, distributed execution to eliminate communication needs, and a topological security layer to assess entanglement risks. Simulations show superior convergence and anti-winding performance compared to existing DRL methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing distributed training frameworks struggle with observability challenges in high-density barrier and unstable environments, leading to poor learning outcomes for coordinating multiple soft robots in anti-winding operations within complex constrained environments.

Method: Proposes a TD-MARL framework with: (1) centralized critic network learning where each agent perceives others' strategies via shared topological states to reduce training instability; (2) distributed execution eliminating inter-robot communication to improve reliability; (3) an integrated topological security layer using topological invariants to assess and mitigate entanglement risks and avoid local optima.

Result: Full simulation experiments in a realistic environment demonstrate that TD-MARL outperforms current state-of-the-art deep reinforcement learning methods in both convergence speed and anti-winding effectiveness.

Conclusion: The topology-driven multi-agent RL framework effectively addresses the observability and instability challenges in coordinating soft robots for anti-winding tasks in constrained environments, achieving better performance without requiring inter-robot communication.

Abstract: In the field of precision manufacturing in complex constrained environments, the role of soft robots is increasingly prominent, and the realization of anti-winding control based on multi-intelligent body reinforcement learning has become a research hotspot. One of the core problems at present is to coordinate multiple robots to complete the unwinding operation in a highly constrained environment. The existing distributed training framework faces some observability challenges in high-density barrier and unstable environments, resulting in poor learning results. This paper proposes a topology-driven Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (TD-MARL) framework to coordinate multi-robot systems to avoid entanglement. Specifically, the critical network adopts centralized learning, so that each intelligent body can perceive the strategies of other intelligent bodies by sharing the topological state, thus alleviating the training instability caused by complex interactions; eliminating the demand for communication resources between robots through distributed execution, Upgrade system reliability; the integrated topological security layer uses topological invariants to accurately assess and mitigate the risk of entanglement to avoid the strategy from falling into local difficulties. Finally, the full simulation experiments carried out in the real simulation environment show that the method is better than the current advanced deep reinforcement learning (DRL) method in terms of convergence and anti-winding effect.

</details>


### [3] [DexSim2Real: Foundation Model-Guided Sim-to-Real Transfer for Generalizable Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05241)
*Zijian Zeng,Fei Ding,Huiming Yang,Xianwei Li,Yuhao Liao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DexSim2Real is a framework that uses vision-language foundation models (FM-DR for visual domain randomization, TVCAP for visuo-tactile policy, PSC for curriculum learning) to bridge the sim-to-real gap for dexterous manipulation, achieving 78.2% real-world success rate with only 8.3% sim-to-real performance gap across six tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sim-to-real transfer for dexterous manipulation policies is a critical bottleneck; existing methods like manual domain randomization or task-specific adaptation lack generalizability across diverse scenarios.

Method: Three components: (1) FM-DR uses a VLM as a visual realism critic to optimize simulation parameters via closed-loop CMA-ES; (2) TVCAP adapts cross-attention visuo-tactile fusion for zero-shot sim-to-real RL; (3) PSC combines LLM-based task decomposition with a difficulty scheduler for contact-rich dexterous tasks.

Result: 78.2% average real-world success rate across six challenging manipulation tasks, outperforming DrEureka and DeXtreme, with only 8.3% sim-to-real performance gap.

Conclusion: DexSim2Real effectively bridges the sim-to-real gap for dexterous manipulation by leveraging vision-language foundation models for domain randomization, tactile-visual policy design, and curriculum learning, significantly reducing the sim-to-real performance drop.

Abstract: Sim-to-real transfer remains a critical bottleneck for deploying dexterous manipulation policies learned in simulation to real-world robots. Existing approaches rely on manually designed domain randomization or task-specific adaptation, limiting their generalizability across diverse manipulation scenarios. We present DexSim2Real, an integrated framework that leverages vision-language foundation models to bridge the sim-to-real gap for dexterous manipulation. Our system combines three components: (1) Foundation Model-Guided Domain Randomization (FM-DR), which uses a vision-language model as a visual realism critic to optimize simulation parameters via closed-loop CMA-ES, complementing text-based approaches like DrEureka with direct visual feedback; (2) a Tactile-Visual Cross-Attention Policy (TVCAP) that adapts cross-attention visuo-tactile fusion to zero-shot sim-to-real RL; and (3) a Progressive Skill Curriculum (PSC) that builds on LLM-based task decomposition with a difficulty scheduler tailored to contact-rich dexterous tasks. Extensive experiments on six challenging manipulation tasks with blinded evaluation demonstrate that DexSim2Real achieves a 78.2% average real-world success rate, outperforming DrEureka and DeXtreme while reducing the sim-to-real performance gap to only 8.3%.

</details>


### [4] [Track A*: Fast Visibility-Aware Trajectory Planning for Active Target Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05338)
*Hanxuan Chen,Kangli Wang,Ji Pei*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TA* is an offline search-based trajectory planner for active target tracking that uses a layered DAG search with beam pruning, BVH-based obstacle distance caching, and multi-ray visibility evaluation to efficiently find high-quality tracking trajectories on a 4D spatio-temporal grid, achieving 23x speedup and 100% convergence vs. A* baseline with negligible visibility loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Offline reference trajectories are needed for building multi-modal tracking datasets and benchmarking online tracking planners under repeatable conditions, but existing methods lack scalability for practical use.

Method: TA* combines a layered Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) search with three optimizations: cross-time obstacle distance caching against a Bounding Volume Hierarchy (BVH), per-layer beam pruning, and a configurable multi-ray visibility evaluator, operating on a discretized 4D (x, y, z, t) spatio-temporal grid.

Result: On 1000-scenario CARLA test: 100% convergence in 45s (32 workers). On 248-scenario comparison: 23.0x mean / 11.8x worst-case speedup over A*, convergence from 56.9% to 100%, with only -0.15 pp average visibility change (no scenario exceeding 5 pp drop).

Conclusion: TA* is a practical offline reference planner for active target tracking, trading strict optimality for scalability, with limitations in dense vegetation environments like Town07.

Abstract: Offline reference trajectories for active target tracking are needed both for building multi-modal tracking datasets and for benchmarking online tracking planners under repeatable conditions. We present Track A star (TA star), an offline search-based trajectory planner that targets the visibility-aware target tracking objective on a discretized four-dimensional spatio-temporal grid (x, y, z, t). TA star combines a layered Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) search with three engineering optimizations: cross-time obstacle distance caching against a Bounding Volume Hierarchy (BVH), per-layer beam pruning, and a configurable multi-ray visibility evaluator. TA star employs a beam-pruned heuristic search on this discrete graph to efficiently find high-quality tracking trajectories. While it trades strict theoretical optimality for practical scalability, our empirical results demonstrate robust, near-baseline visibility performance at a fraction of the computational cost. On a 1000-scenario stress test across eight CARLA Optimized maps, TA star converges on all scenarios and completes in 45 s using 32 workers; on a 248-scenario controlled comparison against an unoptimized priority-queue A star baseline (BinaryHeap implementation) under identical scenario inputs and a 5 x 10^6 expansion cap, TA star reduces mean planning time by 23.0x and worst-case planning time by 11.8x, while raising convergence from 56.9% to 100%. On the n=141 baseline-converged subset, TA star changes average visibility by only -0.15 percentage points (pp), with no scenario exceeding a 5 pp drop. We position TA star as a practical offline reference planner under these specific conditions, with limitations and failure cases discussed for environments such as Town07 dense vegetation.

</details>


### [5] [Passive Fault Tolerance through Tension-to-Thrust Feed-Forward: Hybrid Input-to-State Stability for Decentralized Multi-UAV Slung-Load Transport under Abrupt Cable Severance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05339)
*Hadi Hajieghrary,Paul Schmitt*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A passive decentralized controller for multi-UAV slung-load transport that uses direct cable tension feed-forward into altitude thrust, with a conditional hybrid practical ISS certificate enabling certified recovery from abrupt cable severance without peer-state exchange.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing controllers rely on coordinated force allocation, peer-state exchange, or fixed cable topology and lack a certified decentralized recovery mechanism for unannounced cable severance in multi-UAV slung-load transport.

Method: The architecture routes each vehicle's measured cable tension directly into altitude thrust command (T_i^ff = T_i) with a surrounding PD, anti-swing, and projection cascade. A conditional hybrid practical input-to-state-stability certificate provides recovery envelope under actuator, slack, and dwell assumptions.

Result: In Drake multibody simulation (5 vehicles, 10 kg payload, Kelvin-Voigt cables, Dryden wind), achieved 0.312-0.328 m RMSE, 76.1-95.2 mm peak sag, recovery within one payload-pendulum period. Disabling tension feed-forward inflates cruise error by 34-39% and peak sag by 3.6x-4.0x.

Conclusion: Local tension feed-forward is the dominant passive recovery mechanism in the tested decentralized cascade, enabling certified fault recovery without inter-vehicle communication.

Abstract: Abrupt cable severance in multi-UAV slung-load transport redistributes load and changes the active constraint set, leaving limited time for fault diagnosis and reconfiguration. Existing controllers rely on coordinated force allocation, peer-state exchange, or fixed cable topology, and therefore lack a certified decentralized recovery mechanism for unannounced severance. We present a passive architecture that routes each vehicle's measured cable tension directly into its altitude thrust command, $T_i^{\mathrm{ff}}=T_i$, while a surrounding proportional-derivative, anti-swing, and projection cascade preserves local tracking feasibility. The main contribution is a conditional hybrid practical input-to-state-stability certificate that composes a slack-excursion-bounded taut-cable reduction, bounded post-severance Lyapunov jumps, inter-fault decay, and per-fault-cycle contraction $ρ\in (0,1)$ into an explicit recovery envelope under stated actuator, slack, and dwell assumptions. We validate the controller in Drake multibody simulation with five vehicles, a 10 kg payload, Kelvin-Voigt cables, Dryden wind, and single- and dual-severance schedules: the closed loop attains 0.312-0.328 m RMSE, 76.1-95.2 mm peak sag, and recovery within one payload-pendulum period. Disabling the identity inflates cruise error by 34-39% and peak sag by 3.6x-4.0x, identifying local tension feed-forward as the dominant passive recovery mechanism in the tested decentralized cascade.

</details>


### [6] [Creative Robot Tool Use by Counterfactual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05411)
*M. Tuluhan Akbulut,Varun Satheesh,Ahmed Jaafar,Alper Ahmetoglu,Shane Parr,Aditya Ganeshan,Shivam Vats,George Konidaris*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A causal reasoning framework for creative robot tool use that discovers causal relationships between tool properties and task success via VLM-based feature suggestion, counterfactual tool generation, causal feature classification, and keypoint-based skill transfer grounded in physics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable robots to identify and use novel objects as tools beyond their primary functions by grounding tool use in causal physical reasoning rather than superficial feature matching.

Method: (1) Causal discovery via simulated experiments in a dynamics model, decoupled into VLM-based feature suggestion and counterfactual tool generation through geometric/physical perturbations; (2) Causal feature classification for novel objects; (3) Skill transfer via keypoint matching conditioned on identified causal features.

Result: The approach demonstrated reliable tool selection and stronger skill keypoint transfer across three tasks: reaching distant objects with sticks, scooping candies with diverse items, and using boxes/crates as stepping platforms.

Conclusion: Identifying causal features and grounding them in physical tool properties leads to more reliable tool selection and more effective skill transfer compared to baseline methods.

Abstract: We propose a causal reasoning framework for creative robot tool use where a suitable tool for a task is correctly identified for use beyond its primary objectives. The proposed framework first discovers the causal relationships between the tool and the task by conducting simulated experiments in a dynamics model. We decouple the causal discovery problem into two complementary components: VLM-based feature suggestion and counterfactual tool generation via targeted geometric and physical feature perturbations. Then, novel objects are classified based on identified causal features, and the tool use skill is transferred via keypoint matching conditioned on the identified causal features. By reconstructing the task in a dynamics model, our approach grounds tool use in the physics of the problem. We illustrate our approach in reaching a distant object with different sticks, scooping candies from a bowl using diverse items, and using different boxes or crates as stepping platforms to retrieve an object from a high shelf. Our baseline comparisons show that identifying causal features and grounding them in physical tool properties leads to more reliable tool selection and stronger skill keypoint transfer.

</details>


### [7] [Contact-Free Grasp Stability Prediction with In-Hand Time-of-Flight Sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05461)
*Kyle DuFrene,Cindy Grimm*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a contact-free grasp stability predictor using multi-zone time-of-flight sensors on a gripper's distal links. Unlike prior tactile-based methods, it predicts stability before grasping, achieving 86% accuracy at 15 Hz on real-world objects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing grasp planning degrades with noisy sensors, and tactile-based stability classifiers require grasping the object first, which is slow and failure-prone. A contact-free, pre-grasp predictor is needed to speed up and improve stability assessment.

Method: Multi-zone time-of-flight (ToF) sensors mounted on the distal links of a gripper capture pre-contact depth information. A classifier trained on over 2,500 real-world grasps across 15 objects predicts grasp stability without requiring physical contact or grasping.

Result: The classifier achieved 85.5% accuracy on validation objects and 86.0% accuracy on unseen test objects, with the system cycling at 15 Hz for rapid stability classification.

Conclusion: Contact-free pre-grasp stability prediction using multi-zone ToF sensors is feasible and efficient, outperforming tactile-based methods in speed while maintaining competitive accuracy, enabling faster and safer robotic manipulation.

Abstract: Current approaches to grasp planning for robotics demonstrate high success rates, but degrade with noisy sensors and other factors. Previous works have proposed tactile-based grasp stability classifiers to detect failures, but these approaches rely on making contact and grasping the object to do so. We propose a contact-free grasp stability predictor using multi-zone time-of-flight sensors mounted in the distal links of a gripper. Our method, as it does not require grasping the object to make a prediction, significantly speeds up the stability classification process, cycling at 15 Hz. We collected over 2,500 real-world grasps across 15 objects to train a classifier. Additionally, we conducted grasp attempts over six additional unseen objects, three for validation and model selection, and three for model testing. Our approach demonstrated strong classification performance, with an accuracy of 85.5% on validation and 86.0% on test objects.

</details>


### [8] [Real-world Latency Analysis of Vehicular Visible Light Communication with Multiple LED Transmitters and an Event-Based Camera](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05541)
*Ryota Soga,Tsukasa Shimizu,Shintaro Shiba,Quan Kong,Shan Lu,Takaya Yamazato*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents an event-camera-based visible light communication (VLC) system for V2X applications, addressing bandwidth saturation, multi-transmitter reception, and latency characterization. Using a positive-event-only mode with a custom protocol, the system suppresses event generation, supports simultaneous reception from up to three LEDs, and meets cooperative perception latency requirements, demonstrating feasibility as a complement to RF-based V2X technologies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Event cameras have high temporal resolution, low latency, and wide dynamic range, making them promising for VLC in V2X. However, existing systems face key challenges: bandwidth saturation, multi-transmitter reception, and latency characterization need to be addressed.

Method: The system uses a positive-event-only mode and a custom-designed protocol to suppress event generation while maintaining communication distance and wide field of view. A method to identify multiple transmitters is proposed, enabling simultaneous reception from up to three LEDs.

Result: The system demonstrates simultaneous reception from up to three LEDs. End-to-end latency evaluation in real vehicular scenarios shows the system meets cooperative perception requirements.

Conclusion: Event-camera-based VLC is a feasible complement to existing V2X technologies such as RF.

Abstract: Event cameras offer high temporal resolution, low latency, and wide dynamic range, making them promising receivers for visible light communication (VLC) in vehicle-to-everything (V2X) applications. This work presents an event-camera-based VLC system addressing three key challenges: bandwidth saturation, multi-transmitter reception, and latency characterization.
  We adopt a positive-event-only mode and design a protocol that suppresses event generation while maintaining communication distance and a wide field of view. We also propose a method to identify multiple transmitters and demonstrate simultaneous reception from up to three LEDs. Finally, we evaluate end-to-end latency in real vehicular scenarios and show that the system meets cooperative perception requirements. These results demonstrate that event-camera-based VLC is a feasible complement to existing V2X technologies (e.g., RF).

</details>


### [9] [On the Emergence of Pendular Structure in Multi-Contact Locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05707)
*Lingxue Lyu,Zihui Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This note links LIPM to an optimal control problem that penalizes angular momentum rate, showing that the optimum converges to a pendular force pattern, with bounds from foot-span geometry and friction cones, and tests these findings on a quadruped and the Unitree Go1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To make explicit the connection between LIPM (a common modeling choice in legged locomotion control) and what an optimal controller actually prefers, bridging a gap between modeling assumptions and cost-driven behavior.

Method: The authors formulate a small centroidal OCP that penalizes the rate of angular momentum, analyze its optimum via SVD of the moment Jacobian, examine N=2 stance (trot) with friction cone constraints, and validate with open-loop QP and closed-loop torque control on a point-mass quadruped and Unitree Go1 in MuJoCo.

Result: (1) With full-rank stance, optimum drifts toward pendular force pattern, with constant set by foot-span geometry matching experiments within 16%. (2) With N=2 stance, friction cone introduces a lower bound on angular momentum rate that weight tuning cannot fix, plus a non-smooth feasibility kink at a closed-form critical horizontal acceleration. (3) Adding a task term for nonzero angular momentum rate predictably moves the optimum away from the pendular set.

Conclusion: The findings align closely with classical ZMP/DCM theory, but the asymptotic analysis diverges from actual closed-loop behavior in certain regimes, indicating limits of the simplified model.

Abstract: LIPM is everywhere in legged-locomotion control, but almost always as a modeling choice rather than as something the
  controller's cost actually prefers. This note tries to make that link more explicit. Working from a small centroidal
  OCP that penalizes the rate of angular momentum, we look at what its optimum tends to look like. Three things come
  out. With full-rank stance, the optimum drifts toward a pendular force pattern at a rate determined by the SVD of the
  moment Jacobian; the constant is set by foot-span geometry and matches the experiments to within 16%. With N=2 stance,
  as in trot, the friction cone introduces a lower bound on $\|\dot{H}_G\|$ that no amount of weight tuning fixes; we
  also see a non-smooth feasibility kink at a critical horizontal acceleration that we can write in closed form. Adding
  a task term that asks for a nonzero $\dot{H}_G$ moves the optimum off the pendular set in a predictable way. None of
  this is far from the classical ZMP/DCM picture. We test these claims on a point-mass quadruped and on the Unitree Go1
  in MuJoCo (open-loop QP and a torque-level closed-loop controller), and we note where the asymptotic story stops being
  a good description of what the closed loop actually does.

</details>


### [10] [MaMi-HOI: Harmonizing Global Kinematics and Local Geometry for Human-Object Interaction Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05756)
*Hao Wang,Shiqi Wang,Qi Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MaMi-HOI is a hierarchical framework that addresses "Geometric Forgetting" in diffusion models for 3D Human-Object Interaction generation by using a Geometry-Aware Proximity Adapter (GAPA) for precise contact and a Kinematic Harmony Adapter (KHA) for natural motion, achieving both realism and physical accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing HOI generation methods excel at semantic alignment but struggle with precise object contact due to "Geometric Forgetting"—where semantic features overshadow object geometry features as diffusion model depth increases, causing the model to lose perception of object geometry.

Method: MaMi-HOI is a hierarchical framework with two key components: (1) Geometry-Aware Proximity Adapter (GAPA) that explicitly re-injects dense object geometry details for residual snapping corrections to achieve precise contact; (2) Kinematic Harmony Adapter (KHA) that aligns whole-body posture with spatial objectives to prevent robotic stiffness caused by local enforcement.

Result: The method simultaneously achieves natural motion and precise contact, and extends generation capabilities to long-term tasks with complex trajectories, bridging global navigation and high-fidelity manipulation in 3D scenes.

Conclusion: MaMi-HOI effectively reconciles macro-level kinematic fluidity with micro-level spatial precision for realistic 3D HOI generation, overcoming the geometric forgetting problem in diffusion models.

Abstract: Generating realistic 3D Human-Object Interactions (HOI) is a fundamental task for applications ranging from embodied AI to virtual content creation, which requires harmonizing high-level semantic intent with strict low-level physical constraints. Existing methods excel at semantic alignment, however, they struggle to maintain precise object contact. We reveal a key finding termed \textit{Geometric Forgetting}: as diffusion model depth increases, semantic feature tend to overshadow object geometry feature, causing the model to lose its perception to object geometry. To address this, we propose MaMi-HOI, a hierarchical framework reconciling \textbf{Ma}cro-level kinematic fluidity with \textbf{Mi}cro-level spatial precision. First, to counteract geometric forgetting, we introduce the Geometry-Aware Proximity Adapter (GAPA), which explicitly re-injects dense object details to perform residual snapping corrections for precise contact. Nevertheless, such aggressive local enforcement can disrupt global dynamics, leading to robotic stiffness. In response, we introduce the Kinematic Harmony Adapter (KHA), which proactively aligns whole-body posture with spatial objectives, ensuring the skeleton actively accommodates constraints without compromising naturalness. Extensive experiments validate that MaMi-HOI simultaneously achieves natural motion and precise contact. Crucially, it extends generation capabilities to long-term tasks with complex trajectories, effectively bridging the gap between global navigation and high-fidelity manipulation in 3D scenes. Code is available at https://github.com/DON738110198/MaMi-HOI.git

</details>


### [11] [Resource-Constrained Robotic Planning in the face of Mixed Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05797)
*Yihao Yin,Pian Yu,Andrea Turrini,Zhiming Chi,Yong Li,Lijun Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper introduces CMDPST, a unified framework for modeling robotic systems under quantifiable and unquantifiable uncertainty with resource constraints, and proposes two methods (direct unrolling and optimized pruning) to synthesize strategies maximizing LTLf task satisfaction probability while avoiding resource exhaustion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots operate under significant uncertainty (both quantifiable and unquantifiable) and must respect strict operational constraints like limited resources, yet existing models fail to unify these aspects for robust strategy synthesis.

Method: The authors model the system as a Consumption Markov Decision Process with Set-valued Transitions (CMDPST), combine it with LTLf task specifications, and solve the resource-constrained optimal robust strategy synthesis problem using two techniques: a direct unrolling-based method and an optimized approach with state-space pruning.

Result: Experiments on a warehouse transportation network demonstrate the effectiveness of both proposed solutions, with the optimized approach achieving better performance through state-space pruning.

Conclusion: The CMDPST framework effectively unifies nondeterminism, quantifiable/unquantifiable uncertainty, and resource consumption for robust strategy synthesis under resource constraints, with the pruning-based optimization offering improved efficiency.

Abstract: Robots operate under significant uncertainty, from quantifiable noise to unquantifiable unknowns, and must account for strict operational constraints, such as limited resources. In this paper, we consider the problem of synthesizing robust strategies to guide a robot's actions in fulfilling a given task, while ensuring the system never exhausts its resources. To solve this problem, we first model the robotic system as a Consumption Markov Decision Process with Set-valued Transitions(CMDPST), a unified framework modelling nondeterministic actions, quantifiable and unquantifiable uncertainty, and resource consumption. Then, we combine the CMDPST with the task specification, expressed as a Linear Temporal Logic over finite traces (LTLf ) formula. Lastly, we address the resource constrained optimal robust strategy synthesis problem, which aims to synthesize a strategy that maximizes the probability of satisfying the LTLf objective without resource exhaustion. Our solution involves two techniques: a direct unrolling-based method and a more efficient, optimized approach that leverages state-space pruning for better performance. Experiments on a warehouse transportation network show the effectiveness of the proposed solutions.

</details>


### [12] [A Comparative Study of INDI and NDI with Nonlinear Disturbance Observer for Aerial Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05825)
*Benedetta Rota,Mirko Mizzoni,Amr Afifi,Pieter van Goor,Antonio Franchi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper compares Incremental Nonlinear Dynamic Inversion (INDI) and Nonlinear Dynamic Inversion with nonlinear disturbance observer (NDI+NDO) for fully actuated aerial robots via simulation. INDI shows stronger robustness under model mismatch and combined stress, while NDI+NDO matches nominal performance but is more sensitive to non-ideal conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a systematic comparative robustness analysis between INDI and NDI+NDO control strategies for fully actuated aerial robots, offering practical guidance on their relative strengths and limitations.

Method: Simulation-based comparative analysis across representative operating scenarios, evaluating tracking performance, robustness, and control effort under parametric variations, external disturbances, and measurement noise.

Result: INDI exhibits stronger robustness in several model-mismatch and combined-stress cases; NDI+NDO matches nominal performance but shows greater sensitivity under various non-ideal conditions.

Conclusion: INDI is more robust under challenging conditions, while NDI+NDO performs well nominally but is less robust; findings offer practical guidance for selecting inversion-based control strategies in aerial robotics.

Abstract: This work presents a simulation-based comparative robustness analysis of Incremental Nonlinear Dynamic Inversion (INDI) and Nonlinear Dynamic Inversion augmented with a nonlinear disturbance observer (NDI+NDO) for fully actuated aerial robots. A systematic simulation campaign across representative operating scenarios is conducted, where we compare tracking performance, robustness, control effort, under parametric variations, external disturbances, and measurement noise. Results show that INDI demonstrates stronger robustness in several model-mismatch and combined-stress cases, while NDI+NDO primarily matches nominal performance but exhibits greater sensitivity under several non-ideal conditions. These findings provide practical guidance on the relative strengths and limitations of incremental and observer-based inversion strategies for aerial robotic applications.

</details>


### [13] [Cycle-resolved Cephalopod-Inspired Pulsed-Jet Robot With High-Volume Expulsion and Drag-Reduced Gliding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05875)
*Yiyuan Zhang,Anye Zhong,Junkai Chen,Wenci Xin,Cecilia Laschi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A cephalopod-inspired pulsed-jet robot with a rigid-soft hybrid origami mantle achieves 75% cavity-volume reduction and 75.7% drag-area reduction, reaching peak speed of 0.5 m/s (3.8 BL/s) and average speed >0.2 m/s (1.5 BL/s). A cycle-resolved framework analyzes how expulsion volume, glide duration, and refill pathway affect whole-cycle locomotion performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the gap in understanding cycle-resolved pulsed-jet locomotion (expulsion, gliding, refilling) by developing a robotic platform that mimics cephalopod active body deformation for improved jet propulsion efficiency.

Method: A rigid-soft hybrid origami mantle combining rigid folding panels with compliant silicone framework enables large actively driven geometry-guided deformation. A cycle-resolved experimental framework separately investigates expelled volume, glide duration, and refill pathway effects on locomotion performance.

Result: Peak speed ~0.5 m/s (3.8 BL/s), average speed >0.2 m/s (1.5 BL/s) in first jetting cycle. High expelled-volume-ratio boosts speed; reduced-drag-area gliding improves efficiency; mantle-aperture-inspired passive inlet valves assist refill.

Conclusion: This work provides a robotic implementation of actively deformable cephalopod-like jet propulsion and a unified experimental platform for studying expulsion-gliding-refilling dynamics in pulsed-jet locomotion.

Abstract: Cephalopod pulsed-jet locomotion is not a single isolated expulsion event, but a coordinated cycle involving jet expulsion, passive gliding, and mantle refilling. Inspired by this cycle-resolved biological strategy, this paper presents a cephalopod-inspired pulsed-jet robot with a rigid-soft hybrid origami mantle that enables large, actively driven, and geometry-guided body deformation. The proposed mantle integrates rigid folding panels with a compliant silicone framework, allowing a 75% effective cavity-volume reduction during expulsion and reducing the projected cross-sectional drag area by approximately 75.7% in the contracted gliding configuration. Using this platform, we formulate a cycle-resolved framework to separately investigate how expelled volume, glide duration, and refill pathway influence whole-cycle locomotion performance. Experiments show that the robot reaches a peak speed of approximately 0.5 m/s (3.8 BL/s) and an average speed exceeding 0.2 m/s (1.5 BL/s) within the first jetting cycle. The results further demonstrate the roles of high expelled-volume-ratio contraction in speed generation, reduced-drag-area gliding under different glide durations, and mantle-aperture-inspired passive inlet valves in assisting refill. This work provides both a robotic implementation of actively deformable cephalopod-like jet propulsion and a unified experimental platform for studying expulsion-gliding-refilling dynamics in pulsed-jet locomotion.

</details>


### [14] [Generating Roadside LiDAR Datasets from Vehicle-Side Datasets via Novel View Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05897)
*Yuhan Xia,Runxin Zhao,Hanyang Zhuang,Chunxiang Wang,Ming Yang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VRS is a data synthesis framework that generates labeled roadside LiDAR datasets from vehicle-side data via novel view synthesis, using point cloud completion and occupancy-based visibility constraints to bridge the vehicle-to-roadside domain gap, improving roadside 3D object detection performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large-scale annotated roadside LiDAR datasets are scarce, which limits the training of high-performance roadside perception models for Intelligent Transportation Systems.

Method: VRS synthesizes labeled roadside LiDAR data from vehicle-side datasets using LiDAR novel view synthesis. It employs vehicle point cloud completion to recover missing geometry and an occupancy-based visibility constraint to handle large viewpoint changes during cross-view rendering.

Result: Synthesized data effectively complements real roadside data, mitigates limitations of limited real-world data, and improves generalization to unseen roadside viewpoints in 3D object detection tasks.

Conclusion: VRS enables flexible multi-view rendering for scalable roadside data generation, offering a practical solution to the data scarcity problem in roadside perception.

Abstract: Intelligent Transportation Systems (ITS) require reliable environmental perception to support safe and efficient transportation. With the rapid development of Vehicle-to-everything (V2X), roadside perception has become an effective means to extend sensing coverage and improve traffic safety. However, the scarcity of large-scale annotated roadside LiDAR datasets poses a major challenge for training high-performance roadside perception models. In this paper, we introduce Vehicle-to-Roadside LiDAR Synthesis (VRS), a data synthesis framework that generates labeled roadside LiDAR datasets from vehicle-side datasets via LiDAR novel view synthesis. To mitigate the vehicle-to-roadside domain gap, VRS employs vehicle point cloud completion to compensate for missing geometry in vehicle-side observations, and introduces an occupancy-based visibility constraint to handle large viewpoint changes during cross-view rendering. The proposed framework enables flexible multi-view rendering for scalable roadside data generation. Extensive experiments on roadside 3D object detection demonstrate that the synthesized data effectively complements real roadside data, mitigates the limitations of limited real-world roadside data, and improves generalization to unseen roadside viewpoints.

</details>


### [15] [DexSynRefine: Synthesizing and Refining Human-Object Interaction Motion for Physically Feasible Dexterous Robot Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05925)
*Hyesung Lee,Hyunwoo Jung,Si-Hwan Heo,Sungwook Yang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DexSynRefine is a framework for learning dexterous manipulation from human-object interaction data, combining motion manifold synthesis, residual RL policy, and contact-dynamics adaptation to outperform baselines in sim-to-real transfer across five tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning dexterous manipulation from human-object interaction data is more scalable than teleoperation, but HOI demonstrations are sparse, kinematic-only, and suffer from embodiment mismatch and contact-rich dynamics issues, motivating a framework that can synthesize and physically ground such data.

Method: Three components: (1) HOI-MMFP — task- and object-state-conditioned motion manifold primitives for coordinated hand-object trajectory synthesis from sparse HOI data; (2) task-space residual RL policy that physically grounds the reference while preserving its kinematic structure; (3) contact-and-dynamics adaptation module for sim-to-real transfer using proprioceptive history.

Result: Across five dexterous manipulation tasks (pick-and-place, tool use, object reorientation), the task-space residual policy outperforms prior action-representation baselines in simulation and transfers to a real robot on all five tasks, improving over kinematic retargeting by 50-70 percentage points.

Conclusion: DexSynRefine effectively bridges the gap between sparse kinematic HOI demonstrations and physically executable robot dexterous manipulation, achieving strong sim-to-real transfer by combining motion manifold synthesis with residual RL and contact adaptation.

Abstract: Learning dexterous manipulation from human-object interaction (HOI) data is a scalable alternative to teleoperation, but HOI demonstrations are sparse and provide only kinematic motion that is not directly executable under embodiment mismatch and contact-rich dynamics. We present DexSynRefine, a framework with three coupled components: HOI-MMFP, a task- and object-initial-state-conditioned extension of motion manifold primitives that synthesizes coordinated hand-object trajectories from sparse HOI demonstrations; a task-space residual RL policy that physically grounds the synthesized reference while inheriting its kinematic structure; and a contact-and-dynamics adaptation module that enables sim-to-real transfer from proprioceptive history. Across five dexterous manipulation tasks spanning pick-and-place, tool use, and object reorientation, our task-space residual policy outperforms prior action-representation baselines in simulations and transfers to a real robot on all five tasks, improving over kinematic retargeting by 50-70 percentage points.

</details>


### [16] [Plug-and-Play Label Map Diffusion for Universal Goal-Oriented Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05960)
*Zhixuan Shen,Yijie Zeng,Shengxiang Luo,Tianrui Li,Haonan Luo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PLMD is a plug-and-play label map diffusion model that uses DDPM to complete obstacle and semantic labels for unobserved regions, enabling goal localization in partially explored environments and achieving SOTA on three GON tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing map-based GON methods rely on self-centered semantic maps and suffer from challenges such as reliance on complete maps or inconsistent semantic association when localizing unobserved goals.

Method: Proposes Plug-and-Play Label Map Diffusion (PLMD), a diffusion-based map completion model that uses DDPM to generate obstacle and semantic labels for unobserved regions. It leverages structural consistency between known and unknown obstacle layouts and integrates obstacle priors into the semantic denoising process.

Result: PLMD effectively expands the region of unknown maps, integrates seamlessly into existing navigation strategies, and achieves state-of-the-art performance on three GON tasks.

Conclusion: By substituting predicted labels for unobserved regions, robots can accurately localize specified objects in partially observed environments, and PLMD can be plugged into existing semantic-map-based navigation strategies.

Abstract: In embodied vision, Goal-Oriented Navigation (GON) requires robots to locate a specific goal within an unexplored environment. The primary challenge of GON arises from the need to construct a Bird's-Eye-View (BEV) map to understand the environment while simultaneously localizing an unobserved goal. Existing map-based methods typically employ self-centered semantic maps, often facing challenges such as reliance on complete maps or inconsistent semantic association. To this end, we propose Plug-and-Play Label Map Diffusion (PLMD), which defines a novel map completion diffusion model based on Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models (DDPM). PLMD generates obstacle and semantic labels for unobserved regions through a diffusion-based completion process, thereby enabling goal localization even in partially observed environments. Moreover, it mitigates inconsistent semantic association by leveraging structural consistency between known and unknown obstacle layouts and integrating obstacle priors into the semantic denoising process. By substituting predicted labels for unobserved regions, robots can accurately localize the specified objects. Extensive experiments demonstrate that PLMD \textbf{(I)} effectively expands the region of unknown maps, \textbf{(II)} integrates seamlessly into existing navigation strategies that rely on semantic maps, \textbf{(III)} achieves state-of-the-art performance on three GON tasks.

</details>


### [17] [Accurate Trajectory Tracking with MPCC for Flapping-Wing MAVs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06042)
*Charbel Toumieh,Jack Zeng,Niel Mistry,Dario Floreano*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper presents a Model Predictive Contouring Control (MPCC) approach for bird-scale ornithopters that tracks arc-length-parameterized trajectories and optimizes progress online, using a compact, continuously differentiable dynamical model that captures dominant aerodynamic couplings. The method achieves 6.5–9 cm mean tracking deviation at up to 3 m/s—a ~10× improvement over prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Precise autonomous control of bird-scale ornithopters is challenging due to tight coupling between lift, airspeed, and turning authority with few control inputs. Conventional cascaded controllers produce persistent tracking errors, and time-parameterized trajectory tracking cannot robustly handle these coupled dynamics without predefined speed profiles.

Method: The authors propose Model Predictive Contouring Control (MPCC) that tracks arc-length-parameterized trajectories while optimizing progress online, eliminating predefined timing. A compact, continuously differentiable dynamical model captures dominant couplings of bird-scale ornithopters, enabling real-time nonlinear optimization.

Result: Validated with the XFly ornithopter on circular and 3D racing trajectories: mean deviation of 6.5–9 cm at speeds up to 3 m/s, representing an almost 10-fold improvement over prior ornithopter control methods.

Conclusion: The MPCC approach with the proposed compact model effectively addresses coupled dynamics and tracking limitations in bird-scale ornithopter control, achieving substantial precision improvements.

Abstract: Flapping-wing micro aerial vehicles offer quieter and safer operation than rotary-wing drones, yet achieving precise autonomous control of bird-scale ornithopters remains challenging: lift, airspeed, and turning authority are tightly coupled and governed by only a few control inputs. Conventional cascaded controllers treat altitude, speed, and heading independently, producing persistent tracking errors during complex maneuvers, while time-parameterized trajectory tracking requires predefined speed profiles that existing methods cannot robustly produce for these coupled dynamics. We address both limitations simultaneously with a Model Predictive Contouring Control (MPCC) approach that tracks arc-length-parameterized trajectories while optimizing progress online, eliminating the need for predefined timing. However, MPCC requires a dynamical model that captures the coupled aerodynamics without exceeding the computational budget of real-time nonlinear optimization. Here, we propose a compact, continuously differentiable model that captures the dominant couplings of bird-scale ornithopters, enabling real-time predictive control. We validated the method with the XFly ornithopter flying along circular and three-dimensional racing trajectories and achieved a mean deviation from the reference trajectory between 6.5 and 9 cm at speeds up to 3 m/s, which represents an almost 10-fold improvement over prior ornithopter control methods.

</details>


### [18] [Monitoring autonomous persistent surveillance missions using invariance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06062)
*Vladislav Nenchev,Prodromos Sotiriadis*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper presents a compositional runtime monitoring approach for persistent surveillance by autonomous robots with black-box autonomy stacks, using decentralized invariant computation for large environments, validated on a real robot in a labyrinth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Persistent surveillance by autonomous robots with black-box autonomy stacks lacks formal guarantees, especially for large environments where computing a global invariant offline is intractable.

Method: Models the closed-loop system as a state-dependent hybrid system with linear parameter varying dynamics; designs a monitor based on an offline-computed invariant; for scalability, proposes a compositional monitor via decentralized computation of low-dimensional invariant sets for each uncertainty region, checked online via conjunction.

Result: Under common independence assumptions, the compositional monitor is sound and complete with respect to the full-system invariant. Validated in a case study with a real robot persistently monitoring a labyrinth.

Conclusion: The compositional invariant approach enables scalable runtime monitoring for persistent surveillance in large environments, and is practically applicable on real robotic systems.

Abstract: This paper studies runtime monitoring for persistent surveillance by autonomous robots when the autonomy stack is a black box. The environment is partitioned into finitely many parts, each carrying an uncertainty state that decreases when observed and increases otherwise. We model the closed loop as a state-dependent hybrid system with linear parameter varying dynamics and design a monitor based on an invariant computed offline. As this invariant is typically hard to obtain for large to-be-surveyed spaces, we propose a compositional monitor obtained by decentralized computation of low-dimensional invariant sets for each uncertainty region, and checking their conjunction online. Under common independence assumptions, the compositional monitor is sound and complete with respect to the full-system invariant. The approach is applied in a case study with a real robot persistently monitoring a labyrinth, emphasizing its applicability in practice.

</details>


### [19] [VLA-GSE: Boosting Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning in VLA with Generalized and Specialized Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06175)
*Yuhua Jiang,Junjie Lu,Xinyao Qin,Xiaoyu Chen,Kaixin Wang,Feifei Gao,Li Zhao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VLA-GSE is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning framework for vision-language-action models that uses spectral decomposition to create generalized (shared) and specialized (routed) experts, achieving strong robot control performance with only 2.51% parameter updates while preserving pre-trained capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full fine-tuning of VLA models overfits on downstream robotic data and causes catastrophic forgetting of pre-trained vision-language knowledge. Existing PEFT methods struggle to adapt effectively to robot control tasks.

Method: VLA-GSE initializes experts by spectrally decomposing the frozen backbone: assigning leading singular components to generalized experts (shared) and disjoint residual components to specialized experts (routed), enabling better adaptation under a fixed trainable-parameter budget.

Result: Updates only 2.51% of parameters, achieves 81.2% average zero-shot success on LIBERO-Plus, preserves VLM capability comparably to LoRA, and improves real-world manipulation under multiple distribution shifts.

Conclusion: VLA-GSE outperforms both FFT and strong PEFT baselines in robotic control adaptation while retaining knowledge preservation, demonstrating effective parameter-efficient fine-tuning for VLA models.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models inherit rich visual-semantic priors from pre-trained vision-language backbones, but adapting them to robotic control remains challenging. Full fine-tuning (FFT) is prone to overfitting on downstream robotic data and catastrophic forgetting of pretrained vision-language capabilities. Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) better preserves pre-trained knowledge, yet existing PEFT methods still struggle to adapt effectively to robot control tasks. To address this gap, we propose VLA-GSE, a parameter-efficient VLA fine-tuning framework that improves control adaptation while retaining PEFT's knowledge preservation advantage. Specifically, VLA-GSE (Generalized and Specialized Experts) is initialized by spectrally decomposing the frozen backbone, assigning leading singular components to generalized experts (shared experts) and disjoint residual components to specialized experts (routed experts). This decomposition improves adaptation capacity under a fixed trainable-parameter budget. Under a comparable parameter budget, VLA-GSE updates only 2.51% of the full model parameters and consistently outperforms strong FFT and PEFT baselines. It achieves 81.2% average zero-shot success on LIBERO-Plus, preserves pre-trained VLM capability comparably to LoRA on multimodal understanding benchmarks, and improves real-world manipulation success under multiple distribution shifts. Code is available at: https://github.com/YuhuaJiang2002/VLA-GSE

</details>


### [20] [When to Trust Imagination: Adaptive Action Execution for World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06222)
*Rui Wang,Yue Zhang,Jiehong Lin,Kuncheng Luo,Jianan Wang,Zhongrui Wang,Xiaojuan Qi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes FFDC, a lightweight verifier for adaptive execution of World Action Models, which determines whether predicted actions remain reliable by reasoning over predicted actions, visual dynamics, real observations, and language instructions. This enables dynamic action chunk sizes, improving efficiency and robustness. Experiments show 69.10% fewer forward passes, 34.02% less execution time, and improved success rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current World Action Models execute a fixed number of predicted actions after each inference, leaving the robot unaware of whether the imagined future remains consistent with actual physical rollout. This work formulates adaptive WAM execution as a future-reality verification problem.

Method: Proposes Future Forward Dynamics Causal Attention (FFDC), a lightweight verifier that jointly reasons over predicted future actions, predicted visual dynamics, real observations, and language instructions to estimate whether the remaining action rollout can still be trusted. Also introduces Mixture-of-Horizon Training to improve long-horizon trajectory coverage.

Result: On RoboTwin benchmark: 69.10% reduction in WAM forward passes, 34.02% reduction in execution time, 2.54% improvement in success rate over short-chunk baseline. In real-world experiments: 35% improvement in success rate.

Conclusion: FFDC enables adaptive action chunk sizes as an emergent consequence of prediction-observation consistency, preserving long-horizon execution efficiency while restoring responsiveness in contact-rich or difficult phases. The method achieves a strong robustness-efficiency trade-off.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) have recently emerged as a promising paradigm for robotic manipulation by jointly predicting future visual observations and future actions. However, current WAMs typically execute a fixed number of predicted actions after each model inference, leaving the robot blind to whether the imagined future remains consistent with the actual physical rollout. In this work, we formulate adaptive WAM execution as a future-reality verification problem: the robot should execute longer when the WAM-predicted future remains reliable, and replan earlier when reality deviates from imagination. To this end, we propose Future Forward Dynamics Causal Attention (FFDC), a lightweight verifier that jointly reasons over predicted future actions, predicted visual dynamics, real observations, and language instructions to estimate whether the remaining action rollout can still be trusted. FFDC enables adaptive action chunk sizes as an emergent consequence of prediction-observation consistency, preserving the efficiency of long-horizon execution while restoring responsiveness in contact-rich or difficult phases. We further introduce Mixture-of-Horizon Training to improve long-horizon trajectory coverage for adaptive execution. Experiments on the RoboTwin benchmark and in the real world demonstrate that our method achieves a strong robustness-efficiency trade-off: on RoboTwin, it reduces WAM forward passes by 69.10% and execution time by 34.02%, while improving success rate by 2.54% over the short-chunk baseline; in real-world experiments, it improves success rate by 35%.

</details>


### [21] [RobotEQ: Transitioning from Passive Intelligence to Active Intelligence in Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06234)
*Kuofei Fang,Xinyi Che,Haomin Ouyang,Shufan Zhang,Xuehao Wang,Qi Liu,Liyi Liu,Chenqi Zhang,Wenxi Cai,Wenyu Dai,Jinyang Wu,Fan Zhang,Haoyu Chen,Bin He,Zheng Lian*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RobotEQ is the first benchmark for active intelligence in embodied AI, assessing whether models can understand and adhere to social norms without explicit user instructions. It includes a dataset of 1,900 egocentric images with 5,353 action judgment and 1,286 spatial grounding questions, and a benchmark showing current models fall short, though RAG-based external knowledge can improve performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current embodied AI research focuses on passive intelligence (following explicit user instructions), but robots must also understand permissible vs. prohibited actions without explicit commands to integrate into society. The authors introduce RobotEQ to fill the gap in evaluating active intelligence and social norm compliance.

Method: The authors construct RobotEQ-Data with 1,900 egocentric images across 10 categories and 56 subcategories, manually annotated with 5,353 action judgment and 1,286 spatial grounding questions. They establish RobotEQ-Bench to evaluate state-of-the-art models, including experiments with RAG techniques incorporating external social norm knowledge bases.

Result: Current models fail to achieve reliable active intelligence, especially in spatial grounding. Leveraging RAG techniques with external social norm knowledge bases generally improves performance.

Conclusion: RobotEQ facilitates the transition of robotics from user-guided passive manipulation to active social compliance, highlighting the need for better active intelligence in embodied AI.

Abstract: Embodied AI is a prominent research topic in both academia and industry. Current research centers on completing tasks based on explicit user instructions. However, for robots to integrate into human society, they must understand which actions are permissible and which are prohibited, even without explicit commands. We refer to the user-guided AI as passive intelligence and the unguided AI as active intelligence. This paper introduces RobotEQ, the first benchmark for active intelligence, aiming to assess whether existing models can comprehend and adhere to social norms in embodied scenarios. First, we construct RobotEQ-Data, a dataset consisting of 1,900 egocentric images, spanning 10 representative embodied categories and 56 subcategories. Through extensive manual annotation, we provide 5,353 action judgment questions and 1,286 spatial grounding questions, specifying appropriate robot actions across diverse scenarios. Furthermore, we establish RobotEQ-Bench to evaluate the performance of state-of-the-art models on this task. Experimental results show that current models still fall short in achieving reliable active intelligence, particularly in spatial grounding. Meanwhile, we observe that leveraging RAG techniques to incorporate external social norm knowledge bases can generally enhance performance. This work can facilitate the transition of robotics from user-guided passive manipulation to active social compliance.

</details>


### [22] [CKT-WAM: Parameter-Efficient Context Knowledge Transfer Between World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06247)
*Yuhua Jiang,Yijun Guo,Hongbing Yang,Guojun Lei,Nuo Chen,Yinuo Zhang,Shaoqiang Yan,Bo Lin,Feifei Gao,Biqing Qi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CKT-WAM is a parameter-efficient framework for transferring knowledge across heterogeneous world action models by injecting compact context information into the text embedding space, achieving 86.1% success rate on LIBERO-Plus with only 1.17% trainable parameters and 83.3% on real-world long-horizon tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transferring knowledge across heterogeneous World Action Models (WAMs) is challenging due to mismatched latent interfaces, high adaptation costs, and rigidity of conventional distillation objectives.

Method: CKT-WAM extracts intermediate teacher hidden states, reduces token count via learnable-query cross attention (LQCA) compressors, transforms them through an always-on generalized adapter, a lightweight router, and sparsely activated specialized adapters, then appends the resulting context to the student's conditioning textual embeddings.

Result: CKT-WAM achieves 86.1% total success rate on LIBERO-Plus with only 1.17% trainable parameters, approaching full fine-tuning performance, and achieves 83.3% average success rate on real-world long-horizon manipulation tasks.

Conclusion: CKT-WAM consistently improves zero-shot generalization and achieves best overall performance both in simulation and real-world settings, demonstrating strong long-horizon manipulation ability with minimal architectural modification.

Abstract: World action models (WAMs) provide a powerful generative framework for embodied control, yet transferring knowledge across heterogeneous WAMs remains challenging due to mismatched latent interfaces, high adaptation cost, and the rigidity of conventional distillation objectives. We propose \textbf{CKT-WAM}, a parameter-efficient \textbf{C}ontext \textbf{K}nowledge \textbf{T}ransfer framework that transfers teacher WAM's knowledge into a student WAM through a compact context in the text embedding space, rather than output imitation or dense hidden-state matching. Specifically, CKT-WAM extracts intermediate teacher hidden states, reduces the number of tokens via compressors' learnable-query cross attention (LQCA), and transforms them through an always-on generalized adapter, a lightweight router, and sparsely activated specialized adapters. The resulting context is then appended to the student's conditioning textual embeddings, thereby injecting the transferred knowledge into the student with minimal architectural modification. Experiments show that CKT-WAM consistently improves zero-shot generalization and achieves the best overall performance on LIBERO-Plus, reaching 86.1\% total success rate with only 1.17\% trainable parameters, while approaching full fine-tuning performance. Beyond simulation, CKT-WAM also demonstrates strong real-world long-horizon manipulation ability, achieving the best average success rate of 83.3\% across four multi-step and long-horizon tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/YuhuaJiang2002/CKT-WAM.

</details>


### [23] [Toward Visually Realistic Simulation: A Benchmark for Evaluating Robot Manipulation in Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06311)
*Yixin Zhu,Zixiong Wang,Jian Yang,Jin Xie,Jingyi Yu,Jiayuan Gu,Beibei Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VISER is a visually realistic simulation benchmark for robot manipulation evaluation, featuring over 1,000 high-fidelity 3D assets with PBR materials. It uses MLLMs for material-aware part segmentation and retrieval, and achieves a 0.92 Pearson correlation with real-world performance across various VLA policies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing simulation benchmarks lack visual realism (lighting and material fidelity), creating a large sim-to-real domain gap that undermines the reliability of simulation-based evaluation in predicting real-world robot manipulation performance.

Method: 1) Systematic analysis isolating effects of lighting and material on geometric reasoning and spatial grounding. 2) Construction of VISER benchmark with over 1,000 high-fidelity 3D assets using PBR materials. 3) Automated pipeline using MLLMs for material-aware part segmentation and material retrieval to generate physically plausible assets. 4) Diverse evaluation tasks (grasping, placing, long-horizon) for assessing VLA models.

Result: VISER achieves a strong correlation between simulation and real-world performance, with an average Pearson correlation coefficient of 0.92 across different policies.

Conclusion: Lighting and material factors are critical for geometric reasoning and spatial grounding in robot manipulation evaluation. VISER demonstrates that high visual fidelity in simulation is essential for reliable real-world performance prediction, and the proposed automated asset generation pipeline enables scalable benchmark construction.

Abstract: Reliable simulation evaluation of robot manipulation policies serves as a high-fidelity proxy for real-world performance. Although existing benchmarks cover a wide range of task categories, they lack visual realism, creating a large domain gap between simulation and reality. This undermines the reliability of simulation-based evaluation in predicting real-world performance. To mitigate the sim-to-real visual gap, we conduct a systematic analysis to isolate the effects of lighting and material. Our results show that these factors play a critical role in geometric reasoning and spatial grounding, yet are largely overlooked in existing benchmarks. Motivated by the analysis, we propose VISER, a visually realistic benchmark for evaluating robot manipulation in simulation. VISER features a high-fidelity dataset of over 1,000 3D assets with physically-based rendering (PBR) materials, along with 3D scenes created from these assets through curated layouts or generation. To this end, we propose an automated pipeline leveraging Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) for material-aware part segmentation and material retrieval, enabling scalable generation of physically plausible assets. Building on the high-fidelity 3D asset dataset, we construct diverse evaluation tasks, such as grasping, placing, and long-horizon tasks, enabling scalable and reproducible assessment of Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models. Our benchmark shows a strong correlation between simulation and real-world performance, achieving an average Pearson correlation coefficient of 0.92 across different policies.

</details>


### [24] [AssistDLO: Assistive Teleoperation for Deformable Linear Object Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06323)
*Berk Guler,Simon Manschitz,Kay Pompetzki,Jan Peters*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AssistDLO is an assistive teleoperation framework for deformable linear object manipulation that combines multi-view state estimation, visual assistance, and a geometry-aware shared-autonomy controller (SA-CBF). A user study (N=22) on bimanual knot-untangling reveals that SA-CBF acts as a skill equalizer for naive users (71%→88% success), while experts prefer visual assistance, and effectiveness depends on rope properties (rigidity, length). The results highlight the need for adaptive, user-aware, and material-aware shared autonomy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manipulating Deformable Linear Objects (DLOs) in teleoperation is challenging due to infinite-dimensional configuration space, complex nonlinear dynamics, and depth uncertainty that hinders state perception and reaction. Existing shared autonomy methods rely on simple geometric attractors that fail to preserve DLO geometry.

Method: The AssistDLO framework combines real-time multi-view state estimation, visual assistance (VA), and a geometry-aware shared-autonomy controller based on Control Barrier Functions (SA-CBF). SA-CBF acts as a geometry-aware funnel for precise grasping while preserving operator authority.

Result: In a bimanual knot-untangling study (N=22) with varying rope length/rigidity: SA-CBF increased naive user success from 71% to 88% (skill equalizer), was more effective for stiffer ropes. Expert users preferred VA. Highly compliant, long ropes benefited more from visual support than localized action assistance.

Conclusion: Effective DLO teleoperation cannot rely on a fixed assistance strategy; adaptive, user-aware, and material-aware shared autonomy is critical for success.

Abstract: Manipulating Deformable Linear Objects (DLOs) is challenging in robotics due to their infinite-dimensional configuration space and complex nonlinear dynamics. In teleoperation, depth uncertainty hinders state perception and reaction. AssistDLO addresses this challenge as an assistive teleoperation framework for DLO manipulation that combines real-time multi-view state estimation, visual assistance (VA), and a geometry-aware shared-autonomy controller based on Control Barrier Functions (SA-CBF). While traditional shared autonomy methods often rely on simple geometric attractors and may fail to preserve DLO geometry, SA-CBF acts as a geometry-aware funnel, facilitating precise grasping while preserving the operator's high-level authority. The framework is evaluated in a bimanual knot-untangling user study (N = 22) using ropes with varying length and rigidity. Results show that the effectiveness of the assistance depends strongly on operator expertise and DLO properties. SA-CBF provides the strongest gains for naive users, acting as a skill equalizer that increases task success from 71% to 88%, and is effective for stiffer ropes. Conversely, expert users prefer VA, and highly compliant, long ropes benefit more from visual support than localized action assistance. Ultimately, these findings demonstrate that effective DLO teleoperation cannot rely on a fixed strategy, highlighting the critical need for adaptive, user-aware, and material-aware shared autonomy.

</details>


### [25] [TouchDrive: Electronics-Free Tactile Sensing Interface for Assistive Grasping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06432)
*Jing Xu,Xuezhi Niu,Didem Gurdur Broo,Klas Hjort*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TouchDrive is an electronics-free, pneumatic tactile sensing interface for assistive robotic grasping that converts contact forces into haptic feedback via valve-mediated switching, enabling delicate object manipulation without electronic components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing assistive grasping systems rely on electronic sensing and multi-stage processing pipelines, increasing complexity and reducing accessibility. A simpler, electronics-free solution is needed.

Method: A passive mechanical loop using a pneumatic normally closed valve, compressed air tank, sensing element, and haptic feedback actuator. Contact forces directly trigger pneumatic feedback without electronics.

Result: Validated across diverse robotic platforms, demonstrating reliable performance in handling compliant/fragile objects (up to 20 items including fruits and everyday objects).

Conclusion: TouchDrive provides a cost-effective, accessible alternative to electronic sensing for assistive grasping, enabling users to modulate grasp forces through direct tactile feedback.

Abstract: Assistive robotic grasping plays an important role in enabling safe and adaptive manipulation of diverse objects. However, existing systems often rely on electronic sensing and multi-stage processing pipelines, increasing system complexity and reducing accessibility. To address these limitations, we present TouchDrive, a cost-effective, electronics-free tactile sensing interface for assistive grasping. TouchDrive directly converts contact forces into pneumatic feedback through valve-mediated switching, integrating sensing, signal generation, and feedback within a single passive mechanical loop. The system can be employed using a pneumatic normally closed valve, a compressed air tank, sensing element, and haptic feedback actuator without electronics. By delivering tactile cues, TouchDrive empowers users to modulate grasp forces, enabling precise and robust delicate manipulation of compliant and fragile objects. The interface has been validated across diverse robotic platforms, consistently demonstrating reliable performance and practical applicability in assistive grasping tasks, such as handling fruits and everyday items (up to 20 objects).

</details>


### [26] [GA3T: A Ground-Aerial Terrain Traversability Dataset for Heterogeneous Robot Teams in Unstructured Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06478)
*Siwei Cai,Knut Peterson,Quan Tran,Christian Ricks,Dhanush Parthasarathy,Amir Kaidarov,Neil Deshpande,Sukaina Najm,David Han,Lifeng Zhou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GA3T is a real-world multi-robot collaborative perception dataset featuring a Husky UGV and EVO II UAV operating in unstructured off-road environments, providing over 13,000 synchronized frames with LiDAR, stereo, thermal, RGB, and GPS data, plus SAM-based and manual annotations, designed to advance cross-view perception and terrain traversability research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The lack of real-world datasets with overlapping multi-modal observations from heterogeneous air-ground robot platforms in unstructured terrain limits progress in multi-robot collaborative perception.

Method: Collected data using a Clearpath Husky UGV (3D LiDAR, stereo camera, IMU, GPS) and Autel EVO II UAV (RGB, thermal/infrared, GPS) across 4 environments (forests, rocky paths, mud, snow, grass) with over 13,000 synchronized frames, SAM 3-based zero-shot segmentation, and 8,000+ manual labels.

Result: A dataset covering ~29 minutes of operation with cross-modal, cross-view, occlusion-aware observations, collected during early-spring when sparse canopies enable partial aerial views of ground robot through trees.

Conclusion: GA3T is specifically designed to support research on cross-view perception, air-ground viewpoint fusion, traversability estimation, and collaborative scene understanding in real off-road environments, addressing gaps left by prior SLAM-focused or simulated multi-robot datasets.

Abstract: Heterogeneous air-ground robot teams combine complementary sensing modalities, mobility characteristics, and spatial viewpoints that can significantly enhance perception in complex outdoor environments. However, progress in multi-robot collaborative perception has been constrained by the lack of real-world datasets featuring overlapping multi-modal observations from platforms operating in unstructured terrain. We present GA3T (Ground-Aerial Team for Terrain Traversal), a real-world multi-robot collaborative perception dataset collected using a Clearpath Husky UGV and an Autel EVO~II UAV across diverse unstructured environments, including forest trails, rocky paths, muddy terrain, snow piles, and grass-covered fields. The ground platform provides 3D LiDAR, stereo camera, IMU, and GPS data, while the aerial platform contributes RGB imagery, thermal/infrared observations, and GPS from a complementary overhead viewpoint, allowing for rich cross-modal and cross-view perception. The dataset is collected in 4 unique environments, with over 13,000 synchronized frames across approximately 29 minutes of operation, and includes both SAM~3-based zero-shot segmentation and over 8,000 manually labeled images. A unique aspect of the dataset is its early-spring collection period, during which sparse tree canopies allow the aerial robot to partially observe the ground robot and terrain through the trees, allowing for occlusion-aware collaborative perception. Unlike prior multi-robot datasets that focus on SLAM or simulated cooperative driving, GA3T is specifically designed to support research on cross-view perception, air-ground viewpoint fusion, traversability estimation, and collaborative scene understanding in real off-road environments.

</details>


### [27] [OA-WAM: Object-Addressable World Action Model for Robust Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06481)
*Yushan Liu,Peibo Sun,Shoujie Li,Yifan Xie,Lingfeng Zhang,Xintao Chao,Shiyuan Dong,Fang Chen,Xiao-Ping Zhang,Wenbo Ding*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: OA-WAM is an Object-Addressable World Action Model that decomposes visual scenes into persistent address vectors for each object, enabling robust robot manipulation under scene shifts by separating object identity from content, achieving state-of-the-art results on LIBERO (97.8%) and SimplerEnv (79.3%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing World Action Models represent predicted scenes as holistic images, video tokens, or global latents, which are difficult for action decoders to handle when instructions refer to specific objects, especially under scene shifts where object identity is entangled with context.

Method: OA-WAM decomposes each frame into N+1 slot states (one robot slot, N object slots) with persistent address vectors and time-varying content vectors. It uses block-causal sequence fusion with text, image, proprioception, and past-action tokens. A world head predicts next-frame slots, and a flow-matching action head decodes 16-step continuous action chunks. Addressability is enforced via address-only key routing in cross-slot attention and address slice resetting at every transformer layer.

Result: OA-WAM achieves 97.8% on LIBERO and 79.3% on SimplerEnv, reaches SOTA on LIBERO-Plus geometric axes, and obtains a swap-binding cosine of 0.87 in causal slot-intervention tests vs. at most 0.09 for holistic baselines.
