Abstract: Domain adaptation aims to transfer knowledge from a labeled source domain to an unlabeled target domain with different distributions. In real-world scenarios, the label spaces of the two domains often have an inclusion relationship, where some classes exist only in one domain but not the other. These non-overlapping classes are referred to as private classes. Identifying private class samples and mitigating their adverse effects is critical in the literature. Existing methods rely on the assumption that shifts in private classes are large enough to be considered outliers. However, the variance within a single shared class can be significantly larger than the difference between a private class and another shared class, challenging this assumption. Consequently, private classes substantially increase the difficulty of cross-domain classification. To address these issues, based on local transportation and metric properties of optimal transport (OT), a locality-aware private class identification approach is proposed in the form of a score function on transport mass. The effectiveness of the proposed approach is theoretically proven, highlighting the score function's strong ability to distinguish between shared and private class samples. Building on this, we introduce a reliable OT-based method (ReOT) for domain adaptation under severe label shift. ReOT minimizes classification risk while learning the separated cluster structure between the identified shared classes and private classes, effectively avoiding mismatch between shared-private sample pairs, thus ensuring that important knowledge is reliably transported intra-class to mitigate class-conditional discrepancy. Furthermore, a generalization upper bound of the target risk is provided for extreme label shift scenarios, which can be minimized by ReOT. Extensive experiments on benchmarks validate the effectiveness of ReOT.

</details>


### [54] [AlphaCrafter: A Full-Stack Multi-Agent Framework for Cross-Sectional Quantitative Trading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05580)
*Yishuo Yuan,Jiayi Sheng,Sirui Zeng,Jiaqi Wang,Jiaheng Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AlphaCrafter is a full-stack multi-agent framework that unifies factor discovery, regime-adaptive selection, and risk-constrained execution into a continuously adaptive pipeline, outperforming baselines on CSI 300 and S&P 500.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing quantitative trading systems optimize factor mining, regime selection, and execution under static or isolated assumptions, lacking a fully automated, rationality-driven unified pipeline that adapts to non-stationary financial markets.

Method: AlphaCrafter employs three specialized agents: a Miner (LLM-guided search for continuous factor expansion), a Screener (assesses market conditions to build regime-conditioned factor ensembles), and a Trader (translates ensembles into risk-constrained quantitative strategies), forming a closed-loop cross-sectional trading system.

Result: Experiments on CSI 300 and S&P 500 show AlphaCrafter consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in risk-adjusted returns with the lowest cross-trial variance.

Conclusion: An integrated and adaptive factor-to-execution design yields robust trading performance, confirming that coupling continuous factor discovery, regime adaptation, and risk-constrained execution is superior to static or isolated approaches.

Abstract: Financial markets are inherently non-stationary, driven by complex interactions among macroeconomic regimes, microstructural frictions, and behavioral dynamics. Building quantitative strategies that remain profitable demands the continuous coupling of factor discovery, regime-adaptive selection, and risk-constrained execution. Prevailing approaches, however, optimize these components under static or isolated assumptions. Factor mining frameworks typically treat alpha discovery as a one-time search process, implicitly assuming that factor efficacy persists across market regimes. Execution-oriented systems often adopt role-playing agent architectures that simulate anthropomorphic trading committees, introducing behavioral noise rather than systematic rationality. Consequently, a fully automated, rationality-driven framework unifying a coherent quantitative pipeline remains absent. We introduce AlphaCrafter, a full-stack multi-agent framework that closes this gap through a continuously adaptive factor-to-execution pipeline, designed to track and respond to evolving market conditions without manual intervention. AlphaCrafter operates via three specialized agents: a Miner that continuously expands the factor pool via LLM-guided search, a Screener that assesses prevailing market conditions to construct regime-conditioned factor ensembles, and a Trader that translates these ensembles into quantitative strategies under explicit risk constraints. Together, these three agents form a closed-loop cross-sectional trading system that adapts holistically to evolving market dynamics. Extensive experiments on CSI 300 and S&P 500 demonstrate that AlphaCrafter consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in risk-adjusted returns while exhibiting the lowest cross-trial variance, confirming that integrated and adaptive factor-to-execution design yields robust trading performance.

</details>


### [55] [Belief Memory: Agent Memory Under Partial Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05583)
*Junfeng Liao,Qizhou Wang,Jianing Zhu,Bo Du,Rui Yan,Xiuying Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BeliefMem is a probabilistic memory framework for LLM agents that preserves uncertainty by storing multiple candidate conclusions with probabilities, updated via Noisy-OR rules, outperforming deterministic memory methods on LoCoMo and ALFWorld benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing external memory methods for LLM agents commit to a single deterministic conclusion per observation, discarding inherent uncertainty and causing self-reinforcing errors that are never revisited.

Method: BeliefMem stores multiple candidate conclusions as separate memory entries with probabilities, updates probabilities via Noisy-OR rules as new observations arrive, and retrieves all candidates with their probabilities at inference time.

Result: Achieves best average performance on LoCoMo and ALFWorld benchmarks, outperforming well-known baselines even with limited data.

Conclusion: Probabilistic memory that preserves uncertainty is a promising new direction for agent memory in partially observable environments, substantially improving over deterministic memory paradigms.

Abstract: LLM agents that operate over long context depend on external memory to accumulate knowledge over time. However, existing methods typically store each observation as a single deterministic conclusion (e.g., inferring "API~X failed" from temporary errors), even though such observations are inherently partial and potentially ambiguous. By committing to one conclusion and discarding uncertainty, these methods introduce self-reinforcing error: the agent acts on the stored conclusion, never revisits alternatives, and reinforces the conclusion over time. To address this issue, we propose BeliefMem, which shifts the memory paradigm from committing to a single conclusion per observation to retaining multiple candidate conclusions with their probabilities. Concretely, BeliefMem stores the candidate conclusions as separate memory entries, each carrying a probability that is updated via Noisy-OR rules as new observations arrive. At retrieval, all candidates surface together with their probabilities, keeping alternatives visible to the agent. Since each conclusion in memory retains its probability, BeliefMem preserves the uncertainty that the deterministic paradigm discards, enabling the agent to act with high confidence on well-evidenced knowledge while retaining the capacity to update its confidence when new evidence arrives. Empirical evaluations on LoCoMo and ALFWorld benchmarks show that, even with limited data, BeliefMem achieves the best average performance, remarkably outperforming well-known baselines. More broadly, such probabilistic memory produces substantial gains and explores a new direction for agent memory in partially observable environments.

</details>


### [56] [Causal Probing for Internal Visual Representations in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05593)
*Zehao Deng,Tianjie Ju,Zheng Wu,Liangbo He,Jun Lan,Huijia Zhu,Weiqiang Wang,Zhuosheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a causal framework using activation steering to probe and manipulate internal visual representations in MLLMs. It finds that entities are localized while abstract concepts are globally distributed, motivating scaling laws; reveals a compensatory mechanism between perception and generation via reverse steering; and exposes a disconnect between visual perception and abstract reasoning in MLLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite MLLMs' success, the internal mechanisms governing how they encode and ground distinct visual concepts remain poorly understood. The authors aim to bridge this gap by proposing a causal framework to actively probe and manipulate internal visual representations.

Method: The authors propose a causal framework based on activation steering to systematically intervene across four visual concept categories, probing how entities vs. abstract concepts are encoded. They also employ reverse steering and extend analysis to visual reasoning tasks.

Result: (1) Entities exhibit localized memorization while abstract concepts are globally distributed; (2) increasing model depth is indispensable for encoding abstract concepts, but entity localization is invariant to scale; (3) reverse steering reveals a compensatory mechanism between perception and generation; (4) MLLMs treat geometric relations as static visual features rather than triggering procedural reasoning.

Conclusion: The study uncovers fundamental mechanistic differences in how MLLMs encode concrete vs. abstract visual concepts, reveals a perception-generation compensation mechanism, and demonstrates a disconnection between visual perception and abstract reasoning in current MLLMs.

Abstract: Despite the remarkable success of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) across diverse tasks, the internal mechanisms governing how they encode and ground distinct visual concepts remain poorly understood. To bridge this gap, we propose a causal framework based on activation steering to actively probe and manipulate internal visual representations. Through systematic intervention across four visual concept categories, our results reveal a divergence in concept encoding: entities exhibit distinct localized memorization, whereas abstract concepts are globally distributed across the network. Critically, this divergence uncovers a mechanistic driver of scaling laws: increasing model depth is indispensable for encoding distributed and complex abstract concepts, whereas entity localization remains remarkably invariant to scale. Furthermore, reverse steering uncovers that blocking explicit output triggers a surge in latent activations, exposing a compensatory mechanism between perception and generation. Finally, extending our analysis to visual reasoning, we expose a disconnect between perception and reasoning although MLLMs successfully recognize geometric relations, they treat them merely as static visual features, failing to trigger the procedural execution necessary for abstract problem-solving.

</details>


### [57] [Text-Graph Synergy: A Bidirectional Verification and Completion Framework for RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05643)
*Jiarui Zhong,Hong Cai Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TGS-RAG is a unified Text-Graph Synergistic framework that introduces bidirectional enhancement between unstructured text and structured graphs for RAG, using a Global Voting strategy for Graph-to-Text refinement and a Memory-based Orphan Entity Bridging algorithm for Text-to-Graph path restoration, achieving superior multi-hop reasoning performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RAG approaches suffer from the "Information Island" problem caused by asymmetric reasoning flows between unstructured text (prone to pseudo-evidence) and structured graphs (prone to search-time pruning), and hybrid methods only use simple concatenation or unidirectional enhancement that fails to address this fundamental disconnect.

Method: TGS-RAG proposes a bidirectional synergistic mechanism: (1) Graph-to-Text channel using Global Voting from visited graph nodes to re-rank and refine textual evidence for noise filtering; (2) Text-to-Graph channel using Memory-based Orphan Entity Bridging algorithm that leverages textual cues to resurrect valid but pruned reasoning paths from search history without extra database overhead.

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on multiple multi-hop reasoning benchmarks, achieving superior balance between retrieval precision and computational efficiency.

Conclusion: The bidirectional text-graph synergy effectively resolves the Information Island problem, demonstrating that complementary interaction between unstructured and structured knowledge sources significantly improves multi-hop reasoning quality over unidirectional or concatenation-based hybrid approaches.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has become a core paradigm for enhancing factual grounding and multi-hop reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs). Traditional text-based RAG often retrieves logically irrelevant pseudo-evidence, while graph-based RAG is frequently hindered by search-time pruning, which may discard potentially valid reasoning paths. Existing hybrid approaches primarily adopt simple evidence concatenation or unidirectional enhancement, which fails to address the fundamental "Information Island" problem caused by asymmetric reasoning flows between unstructured text and structured graphs. We propose \textbf{TGS-RAG}, a unified framework for \textbf{T}ext-\textbf{G}raph \textbf{S}ynergistic enhancement. TGS-RAG introduces a bidirectional mechanism: (i) a \textbf{Graph-to-Text} channel that employs a Global Voting strategy from visited graph nodes to re-rank and refine textual evidence, filtering out semantic noise; and (ii) a \textbf{Text-to-Graph} channel that utilizes the \textbf{Memory-based Orphan Entity Bridging} algorithm. This algorithm utilizes textual cues to proactively resurrect valid but previously pruned reasoning paths from the search history without additional database overhead. Experimental results on multiple multi-hop reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that TGS-RAG significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving a superior balance between retrieval precision and computational efficiency.

</details>


### [58] [Proactive Instance Navigation with Comparative Judgment for Ambiguous User Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06223)
*Junhyuk Kwon,Seungjoon Lee,Hyejin Park,Kyle Min,Jungseul Ok*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ProCompNav is a two-stage framework for natural-language instance navigation that constructs a candidate pool then asks binary yes/no questions to distinguish the target from distractors, improving success rate while reducing user response length compared to existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing navigation agents often fail when the initial user request is ambiguous—they either stop at the first plausible candidate without exploring alternatives or ask poor questions derived from individual candidates rather than questions designed to distinguish the entire candidate pool, leading to premature decisions and lengthy user responses.

Method: ProCompNav uses a two-stage framework: (1) construct a candidate pool of possible target instances, then (2) identify the target through comparative judgment—at each round, extract an attribute-value pair that splits the current pool, ask a binary yes/no question, and prune all inconsistent candidates simultaneously.

Result: On CoIN-Bench, ProCompNav improves Success Rate over both interactive baselines with the same minimal input and non-interactive baselines with detailed descriptions, while substantially reducing Response Length. It also achieves state-of-the-art Success Rate on TextNav.

Conclusion: Comparative judgment—pool-level discriminative questioning rather than open-ended target description—is broadly useful for instance-level navigation among similar distractors.

Abstract: Natural-language instance navigation becomes challenging when the initial user request does not uniquely specify the target instance. A practical agent should reduce the user's burden by actively asking only the information needed to distinguish the target from similar distractors, rather than requiring a detailed description upfront. Existing approaches often fall short of this goal: they may stop at the first plausible candidate before sufficiently exploring alternatives, or, even after collecting multiple candidates, ask about the target's attributes derived from individual candidates rather than questions selected to distinguish candidates in the pool. As a result, despite the dialogue, the agent may still fail to distinguish the target from distractors, leading to premature decisions and lengthy user responses. We propose Proactive Instance Navigation with Comparative Judgment (ProCompNav), a two-stage framework that first constructs a candidate pool and then identifies the target through comparative judgment. At each round, ProCompNav extracts an attribute-value pair that splits the current pool, asks a binary yes/no question, and prunes all inconsistent candidates at once. This reframes disambiguation from open-ended target description to pool-level discriminative questioning, where each question is chosen to narrow the candidate set. On CoIN-Bench, ProCompNav improves Success Rate over interactive baselines with the same minimal input and non-interactive baselines with detailed descriptions, while substantially reducing Response Length. ProCompNav also achieves state-of-the-art Success Rate on TextNav, suggesting that comparative judgment is broadly useful for instance-level navigation among similar distractors.

</details>


### [59] [Retrieval-Conditioned Topology Selection with Provable Budget Conservation for Multi-Agent Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05657)
*Abhijit Talluri,Pujith Anne,Bhagavan Choudary Pendiyala,Raghavendra Chilukuri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RGAO is a multi-agent code generation system that dynamically selects orchestration topology based on code complexity, using a hierarchical code index and formal budget contracts, reducing misrouting from 30.1% to 8.2% with provable budget conservation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent LLM systems for code generation use fixed orchestration topologies regardless of code structure, leading to suboptimal routing. The paper aims to close this gap by making topology selection conditional on code complexity.

Method: RGAO extracts a structural complexity vector from a hierarchical code index, uses it to select orchestration topology dynamically, and governs sub-agents via formal contracts with six-dimensional budget vectors. It composes complexity-conditioned LLM routing with formal resource algebras.

Result: Reduces proxy-measured misrouting from 30.1% to 8.2%; achieves sub-millisecond DAG construction and linear tree-index scalability; proves budget conservation under retrieval-conditioned dynamic topology selection.

Conclusion: RGAO demonstrates that combining complexity-conditioned routing with formal budget algebras yields provable resource guarantees while significantly improving routing accuracy in multi-agent code generation systems.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM systems for code generation face a fundamental routing problem: the optimal orchestration topology depends on the structural complexity of the code under modification, yet existing systems select topologies without consulting the codebase. We present Retrieval-Guided Adaptive Orchestration (RGAO), an architecture that closes this loop by extracting a structural complexity vector from a hierarchical code index before selecting the orchestration topology. RGAO operates within Code-Agent, a multi-agent framework whose sub-agents are governed by formal contracts with six-dimensional budget vectors. Our headline contribution is the composition of two previously separate lines of work -- complexity-conditioned LLM routing and formal resource algebras -- yielding a property neither admits alone: provable budget conservation under retrieval-conditioned dynamic topology selection. Concretely we contribute: (1) a complexity-conditioned topology router that reduces proxy-measured misrouting from 30.1% to 8.2%; (2) a budget algebra with a structural-induction conservation theorem; and (3) a hierarchical code retrieval engine. Empirical evaluation demonstrates sub-millisecond DAG construction and linear tree-index scalability.

</details>


### [60] [Large Vision-Language Models Get Lost in Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05668)
*Gongli Xi,Ye Tian,Mengyu Yang,Huahui Yi,Liang Lin,Xiaoshuai Hao,Kun Wang,Wendong Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a unified information-theoretic and geometric framework to analyze residual updates in LVLM decoders, revealing that attention preserves subspaces while FFNs expand them. It further shows that replacing learned attention weights with random noise achieves comparable or better performance, indicating significant redundancy in current attention mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing statistical attribution methods for understanding LVLM internal modules lack a unified theoretical basis; the authors aim to bridge this gap by grounding analysis in information theory and geometry.

Method: The authors develop a unified framework using information theory and geometry to quantify the geometric and entropic nature of residual updates in decoder Transformer layers, then compare learned attention against predefined (e.g., Gaussian noise) attention weights.

Result: Attention acts as a subspace-preserving operator for reconfiguration, while FFNs are subspace-expanding operators for semantic innovation. Replacing learned attention with random noise yields comparable or superior performance across most datasets.

Conclusion: Current state-of-the-art LVLMs suffer from severe attention misallocation and redundancy—they "get lost in attention" rather than efficiently leveraging visual context, suggesting room for architectural optimization.

Abstract: Despite the rapid evolution of training paradigms, the decoder backbone of large vision--language models (LVLMs) remains fundamentally rooted in the residual-connection Transformer architecture. Therefore, deciphering the distinct roles of internal modules is critical for understanding model mechanics and guiding architectural optimization. While prior statistical approaches have provided valuable attribution-based insights, they often lack a unified theoretical basis. To bridge this gap, we propose a unified framework grounded in information theory and geometry to quantify the geometric and entropic nature of residual updates. Applying this unified framework reveals a fundamental functional decoupling: Attention acts as a subspace-preserving operator focused on reconfiguration, whereas FFNs serve as subspace-expanding operators driving semantic innovation. Strikingly, further experiments demonstrate that replacing learned attention weights with predefined values (e.g., Gaussian noise) yields comparable or even superior performance across a majority of datasets relative to vanilla models. These results expose severe misallocation and redundancy in current mechanisms, suggesting that state-of-the-art LVLMs effectively ``get lost in attention'' rather than efficiently leveraging visual context.

</details>


### [61] [Attractor Geometry of Transformer Memory: From Conflict Arbitration to Confident Hallucination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05686)
*Qiyao Liang,Risto Miikkulainen,Ila Fiete*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies two distinct failure modes in language models—conflict (parametric vs. working memory disagreement) and hallucination (unlearned facts)—and shows they share a unified geometric account via attractor basins in hidden-state space. A geometric margin metric separates correct recall from hallucination better than output entropy, and confident hallucinations follow a scaling law.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To mechanistically understand why language models produce confident incorrect outputs regardless of whether the failure stems from conflicting information sources (PM vs. WM) or absence of learned knowledge, and to develop better detectability methods beyond output-based monitoring.

Method: The authors propose a unified geometric framework where learned facts form attractor basins in hidden-state space. Conflict is modeled as basin competition (WM disrupts convergence), hallucination as basin absence (drift without attractor). They use controlled synthetic tasks with LoRA adapters for causal isolation, and validate on natural language queries from pretrained models.

Result: Geometric margin (distance to nearest memorized basin) separates correct recall from hallucination with zero false refusals, outperforming entropy-based detection. Confident hallucinations follow scaling law C = exp(-c/Δ̄), growing with scale. Hidden states encode epistemic state but the frozen output head systematically erases it, worsening with scale.

Conclusion: Attractor basin geometry is a structural property of language models (not fine-tuning artifact) that explains both conflict and hallucination failures. Output-based monitoring is fundamentally blind; hidden-state geometry provides a more reliable epistemic signal. Scaling exacerbates the erasure of epistemic state by the output head, leading to more confident hallucinations.

Abstract: Language models draw on two knowledge sources: facts baked into weights (parametric memory, PM) and information in context (working memory, WM). We study two mechanistically distinct failure modes--conflict, when PM and WM disagree and interfere; and hallucination, when the queried fact was never learned. Both produce confident output regardless, making output-based monitoring blind by design. We show both failures share a unified geometric account. In the hidden-state space of autoregressive generation, learned facts form attractor basins. Conflict is basin competition: WM disrupts convergence to the correct basin without raising output entropy. Hallucination is basin absence: the hidden state drifts freely when no memorized basin exists. The frozen LM head, designed for next-token prediction, cannot distinguish these cases and fires confidently either way. We verify this account in a controlled synthetic task--entity identifiers mapped to unique codes with PM installed via LoRA adapters--where ground truth is exact and component roles can be causally isolated through targeted adapter placement. Geometric margin--the hidden state's distance to the nearest memorized basin--reads this geometry directly and separates correct recall from hallucination far more cleanly than output entropy, with zero false refusals where entropy-based detection cannot avoid rejecting the vast majority of correct outputs. The separation holds on natural-language factual queries from the pretrained model with no adaptation, confirming attractor geometry is structural rather than a fine-tuning artifact. The fraction of confident hallucinations follows a scaling law $C = \exp(-c/\barΔ)$, growing with scale even as overall error rates fall. Hidden states reliably encode epistemic state; the frozen output head systematically erases it--and this erasure worsens with scale.

</details>


### [62] [DataDignity: Training Data Attribution for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05687)
*Xiaomin Li,Andrzej Banburski-Fahey,Jaron Lanier*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces FakeWiki, a controlled benchmark for pinpoint provenance (identifying which source documents support a language model's response), and proposes two methods: SteerFuse (training-free activation-steering retrieval fusion) and ScoringModel (supervised contrastive provenance ranker). ScoringModel achieves best results, improving Recall@10 from 35.0 to 52.2 across nine LLMs and five query conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Auditing language-model outputs requires identifying which source documents support a response, but existing benchmarks allow models to rely on lexical shortcuts. The authors aim to study "pinpoint provenance" in a setting that separates true answer support from topical or lexical resemblance.

Method: The authors create FakeWiki (3,537 fabricated Wikipedia-style articles with ground-truth provenance), including QA probes, source-preserving paraphrases, retro-generated variants, hard anti-documents, and five query conditions (clean + four jailbreak-inspired). They evaluate seven retrieval baselines, a training-free activation-steering fusion method (SteerFuse), and a supervised contrastive provenance ranker (ScoringModel) trained with InfoNCE using in-batch, retrieval-mined, and anti-document negatives.

Result: ScoringModel improves mean Recall@10 from 35.0 (best retrieval baseline) to 52.2 across nine open-weight instruction-tuned LLMs and five query conditions, winning 41/45 model-by-condition cells. On jailbreak-inspired queries, ScoringModel improves Recall@10 by 15.7 points on average. SteerFuse is usually second-best without supervised training.

Conclusion: Robust training data attribution requires evaluation settings that separate true answer support from topical/lexical resemblance. ScoringModel demonstrates strong performance, while SteerFuse shows activation-space evidence can efficiently complement text retrieval without supervised training.

Abstract: Auditing language-model outputs often requires more than judging correctness: an auditor may need to identify which source document most likely supports the knowledge expressed in a response. We study this as pinpoint provenance: given a prompt, a target-model response, and a candidate corpus, rank the documents that best support the response. We introduce FakeWiki, a controlled benchmark of 3,537 fabricated Wikipedia-style articles designed to preserve ground-truth provenance while weakening lexical shortcuts. FakeWiki includes QA probes, source-preserving paraphrases, retro-generated variants, hard anti-documents that remain topically similar while removing answer-critical facts, and five query conditions: clean prompting plus four jailbreak-inspired transformations. We evaluate seven retrieval baselines, a training-free activation-steering retrieval-fusion method, SteerFuse, and a supervised contrastive provenance ranker, ScoringModel. ScoringModel maps response and document features into a shared space and is trained with InfoNCE using in-batch, retrieval-mined, and anti-document negatives. Across nine open-weight instruction-tuned LLMs and five query conditions, ScoringModel improves mean Recall@10 from 35.0 for the strongest retrieval baseline to 52.2, without inference-time fusion, and wins 41/45 model-by-condition cells. SteerFuse is usually second-best despite requiring no supervised training, showing that activation-space evidence can efficiently complement text retrieval. On jailbreak-inspired transformed queries, ScoringModel improves Recall@10 by 15.7 points on average over the best baseline. Overall, our work shows that robust training data attribution requires evaluation settings that separate true answer support from topical or lexical resemblance.

</details>


### [63] [GCCM: Enhancing Generative Graph Prediction via Contrastive Consistency Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05689)
*Shaozhen Ma,Wei Huang,Hanchen Wang,Dong Wen,Wenjie Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes GCCM, a graph contrastive consistency model that mitigates shortcut solutions in consistency training for diffusion-based graph prediction by introducing contrastive negative pairs and feature perturbation, achieving improved performance over deterministic predictors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion-based graph prediction methods suffer from expensive iterative denoising and unstable sampling. While consistency training reduces denoising steps, it can fall into a shortcut solution where the model ignores noisy targets and collapses into a deterministic predictor.

Method: GCCM introduces a contrastive consistency objective with negative pairs (beyond isolated pairwise matching) and applies feature perturbation to input node/edge features to break identical conditioning, preventing the model from trivially satisfying self-consistency constraints.

Result: Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets show GCCM mitigates the shortcut solution and yields consistent performance improvements over deterministic predictors for graph prediction tasks.

Conclusion: Adding contrastive negative pairs and feature perturbation effectively addresses the shortcut problem in consistency training for graph prediction, leading to better performance than deterministic baselines.

Abstract: Conditional generative models, particularly diffusion-based methods, have recently been applied to graph prediction by modeling the target as a conditional distribution given the input graph, yielding competitive results compared to deterministic predictor. However, existing diffusion-based prediction methods typically require expensive iterative denoising at inference and often suffer from unstable sampling, which motivates recent efforts to reduce inference denoising steps and enable stable sampling via techniques such as consistency training. Despite this progress, we find that existing consistency training methods for graph prediction could potentially fall into a shortcut solution: the model may attempt to satisfy the self-consistency constraint by ignoring the noisy target (i.e., assigning it negligible weight), ultimately collapsing into a purely deterministic predictor. To mitigate such shortcut solution, we propose GCCM, a graph contrastive consistency model that goes beyond isolated pairwise matching between the same target at different noise levels by introducing negative pairs into a contrastive consistency objective. This adds an additional separation requirement, making the shortcut solution no longer trivially sufficient to satisfy the proposed objective. Moreover, we apply feature perturbation to the input node/edge features to break identical conditioning on the input graph, so that the shortcut no longer yields the same predictions across noise levels and becomes less attractive. Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate that GCCM mitigates the shortcut solution and yields consistent performance improvements in graph prediction compared to deterministic predictors.

</details>


### [64] [Saliency-Aware Regularized Quantization Calibration for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05693)
*Yanlong Zhao,Xiaoyuan Cheng,Huihang Liu,Baihua He,Xinyu Zhang,Harrison Bo Hua Zhu,Wenlong Chen,Li Zeng,Zhuo Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SARQC introduces saliency-aware regularization to PTQ for LLMs, improving generalization by keeping quantized weights close to original weights, with consistent gains in perplexity and accuracy across dense and MoE models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing PTQ methods suffer from generalization risk because calibration based solely on empirical reconstruction error on limited/unrepresentative data can shift quantized weights away from original weights, degrading downstream performance.

Method: SARQC augments standard PTQ objectives with a saliency-aware regularization term that encourages quantized weights to remain close to original weights during calibration, integrated seamlessly into both scale search and Gram-based PTQ pipelines.

Result: Consistent improvements in perplexity and zero-shot accuracy on dense and Mixture-of-Experts LLMs, with no additional computational overhead during inference.

Conclusion: Saliency-aware regularization effectively mitigates generalization risk in PTQ for LLMs, offering a unified enhancement applicable to existing methods.

Abstract: Post-training quantization (PTQ) is an effective approach for deploying large language models (LLMs) under memory and latency constraints. Most existing PTQ methods determine quantization parameters by minimizing a layer-wise reconstruction error on a predetermined calibration dataset, usually optimized via either scale search or Gram-based methods. However, from the perspective of generalization risk, existing calibration objectives of PTQ based only on empirical reconstruction error on limited or unrepresentative calibration data could move the quantized weights away from the original weights. This may cause the generalization risk to diverge, potentially degrading downstream performance. To address this issue, we propose \emph{Saliency-Aware Regularized Quantization Calibration} (SARQC) a unified framework that augments the standard PTQ objective with a saliency-aware regularization term. This term encourages quantized weights to stay close to the original weights during calibration, leading to improved generalization during inference. SARQC integrates seamlessly into existing PTQ pipelines, enhancing both scale search and Gram-based methods under a unified formulation. Extensive experiments on dense and Mixture-of-Experts LLMs demonstrate consistent improvements in perplexity and zero-shot accuracy, without additional computational overhead during inference.

</details>


### [65] [Inference-Time Budget Control for LLM Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05701)
*Zhengru Fang,Senkang Forest Hu,Zhonghao Chang,Yu Guo,Yihang Tao,Hongyao Liu,Mengzhe Ruan,Jun Huang,Yuguang Fang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a two-stage inference-time budget control method for LLM search agents in multi-hop QA, using VOI scores for search actions and selective evidence-grounded finalization, achieving consistent gains across benchmarks and budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM search agents operate under hard dual budgets (token and tool call limits), making it essential to allocate budget efficiently across search actions and decide when to commit a final answer.

Method: Formulates two-stage budget control: (1) search-time controller assigns VOI scores to actions (retrieval, decomposition, answer commitment) to decide next budget allocation; (2) answer-time finalizer compares trajectory answer with refined candidate and rewrites only low-risk answer-form errors.

Result: Across four multi-hop QA benchmarks, three LLM backbones, four budget levels, the method yields positive gains over four baselines. Search-time budget control (especially budget-dependent penalty) drives main gains; answer-time control helps when retrieval is adequate.

Conclusion: Inference-time budget control for LLM search agents should govern both how budget is spent during search and how the final answer is committed.

Abstract: LLM search agents increasingly rely on tools at inference time, but their trajectories are often constrained by hard limits on both tool calls and generated tokens. Under such dual budgets, better answers require not only stronger models, but also explicit control over which search action should receive the next budget unit and when the accumulated evidence is sufficient to commit a final answer. We study this problem in multi-hop question answering (QA) and formulate it as two-stage inference-time budget control. At search time, our controller assigns each feasible action a task-level Value-of-Information (VOI) score, defined as an operational estimate of marginal task value per unit budget under the current search state and remaining dual budget, and uses this score to choose among retrieval, decomposition, and answer commitment. After search, a selective evidence-grounded finalizer compares the trajectory answer with a refined candidate and rewrites only when the residual error appears to be a low-risk answer-form error. Across four multi-hop QA benchmarks, three LLM backbones, and four budget levels, the method yields positive aggregate gains over four audited baselines under the same hard dual-budget protocol. Ablations show that search-time budget control, especially budget-dependent penalty, provides the main performance gain, while answer-time control helps mainly when the retrieval path is already adequate. These results suggest that inference-time budget control for LLM search agents should govern both how budget is spent during search and how the final answer is committed.

</details>


### [66] [Knowledge-Graph Paths as Intermediate Supervision for Self-Evolving Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05702)
*Huyu Wu,Jun Liu,Xiaochi Wei,Yan Gao,Yi Wu,Yao Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper improves Search Self-Play (SSP) for self-evolving search agents by using knowledge-graph paths as intermediate supervision. It grounds question construction in KG subgraphs for relational context and introduces Waypoint Coverage Reward (WCR) for graded partial credit based on entity coverage. Across 7 QA benchmarks and 9 model configurations, it outperforms standard SSP, especially on multi-hop tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SSP faces two bottlenecks: (1) the Proposer generates questions from isolated answer entities without relational context, yielding invalid/unverifiable questions; (2) the Solver receives only a binary outcome reward, discarding useful signal from partially correct trajectories.

Method: (1) Grounds question construction in LLM-guided knowledge-graph subgraphs to provide relational context for the Proposer. (2) Introduces Waypoint Coverage Reward (WCR), which grants graded partial credit to incorrect Solver trajectories based on coverage of entities from the construction path, while preserving full reward for correct answers. Reuses knowledge-graph paths as construction-derived intermediate supervision.

Result: Across seven QA benchmarks and nine model configurations, the approach improves average score over standard SSP in all configurations, with notable gains on multi-hop QA tasks.

Conclusion: Knowledge-graph paths can be reused as lightweight intermediate supervision, providing both relational guidance and process feedback without additional task-specific human annotations or manually labeled process steps.

Abstract: Self-evolving search agents reduce reliance on human-written training questions by generating and solving their own search tasks. We build on Search Self-Play (SSP), a representative Proposer and Solver framework in which questions are generated and answered via multi-step search and reasoning. In practice, however, SSP faces two bottlenecks: the Proposer constructs questions from isolated answer entities without relational context, yielding many invalid or unverifiable questions in early self-play training, while the Solver receives only a binary outcome reward that discards useful signal from partially on-track search trajectories. We address both bottlenecks by reusing knowledge-graph paths as construction-derived intermediate supervision for both question construction and reward shaping. First, we ground question construction in LLM-guided knowledge-graph subgraphs, providing relational context for the Proposer. Second, we observe that constructing and solving a multi-hop question can involve overlapping intermediate entities: the factual bridges used to formulate the question may provide approximate waypoints for answering it. Exploiting this overlap, we introduce Waypoint Coverage Reward (WCR), which grants graded partial credit to incorrect Solver trajectories according to their coverage of entities on the construction path, while preserving full reward for correct answers. Across seven QA benchmarks and nine model configurations, our approach improves the average score over standard SSP in all configurations, including notable gains on multi-hop QA tasks. These results suggest that knowledge-graph paths can be reused as lightweight intermediate supervision, providing both relational guidance and process feedback without additional task-specific human annotations or manually labeled process steps.

</details>


### [67] [Resolving the bias-precision paradox with stochastic causal representation learning for personalized medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05706)
*Peisong Zhang,Manqiang Peng,Yuxuan Wu,Pawit Phadungsaksawasdi,Wesley Yeung,Ye Zhang,Trang Nguyen,Qiang Zhang,Nan Liu,Meng Wang,Kee Yuan Ngiam,Yih-Chung Tham,Ching-Yu Cheng,Tianfan Fu,Qingyu Chen,Rosemary Ke,Chang Li,Wenzhuo Yang,Zhenghao Lu,Chunyou Lai,Yu Zhang,Sheng Zhong,Hao Deng,Dianbo Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies a bias-precision paradox in causal representation learning for estimating individualized treatment effects, and proposes sampling-based maximum mean discrepancy (sMMD) as a stochastic alignment strategy that replaces global adversarial balancing with subset-level matching. Tested on two large ICU cohorts (n=27,783), the framework reduces error by up to 11.5%, improves recall in high-risk tasks, and enhances clinician accuracy by 14.7% while reducing decision time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for estimating individualized treatment effects from longitudinal observational data face a fundamental tension: reducing confounding bias suppresses clinically informative heterogeneity, degrading patient-specific predictions. This bias-precision paradox limits the practical utility of causal representation learning in data-driven medicine.

Method: The authors introduce sampling-based maximum mean discrepancy (sMMD), a stochastic alignment strategy that replaces global adversarial balancing with subset-level matching. This is instantiated in a framework for counterfactual outcome prediction with attribution-grounded interpretability.

Result: Across two large-scale ICU cohorts (n=27,783), the framework improves accuracy under distribution shift, reducing error by up to 11.5% and substantially increasing recall in high-risk tasks. Mechanistic analyses show sMMD selectively preserves clinically decisive variables. In human-AI evaluation, the method outperforms clinicians-in-training and large language models, and improves clinician accuracy by 14.7% while reducing decision time.

Conclusion: The proposed sMMD framework resolves the bias-precision paradox in causal representation learning, enabling interpretable, real-time clinical decision support that improves both accuracy and efficiency of clinician decision-making.

Abstract: Estimating individualized treatment effects from longitudinal observational data is central to data-driven medicine, yet existing methods face a fundamental limitation: reducing confounding bias often suppresses clinically informative heterogeneity, degrading patient-specific predictions. Here, we identify this tension as a bias-precision paradox in causal representation learning and introduce sampling-based maximum mean discrepancy (sMMD), a stochastic alignment strategy that replaces global adversarial balancing with subset-level matching. We instantiate this approach in a framework for counterfactual outcome prediction with attribution-grounded interpretability. Across two large-scale ICU cohorts (n = 27,783), our framework improves accuracy under distribution shift, reducing error by up to 11.5% and substantially increasing recall in high-risk tasks. Mechanistic analyses show that sMMD selectively preserves clinically decisive variables. In human-AI evaluation, our method outperforms clinicians-in-training and large language models, and improves clinician accuracy by 14.7% while reducing decision time, enabling interpretable, real-time clinical decision support.

</details>


### [68] [Conceal, Reconstruct, Jailbreak: Exploiting the Reconstruction-Concealment Tradeoff in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05709)
*Md Farhamdur Reza,Richeng Jin,Tianfu Wu,Huaiyu Dai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Intent-obfuscation jailbreak attacks on MLLMs exploit a reconstruction–concealment tradeoff. Existing methods fail to balance this tradeoff, but character-removed variants achieve better balance. The proposed concealment-aware variant construction and keyword-related distractor images outperform baselines across closed- and open-source MLLMs, revealing that a model's own reconstruction ability can be turned against it.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically analyze the reconstruction–concealment tradeoff governing intent-obfuscation jailbreak attacks on MLLMs, and to expose an underexplored vulnerability where a model's own reconstruction ability can be exploited to recover hidden harmful intent.

Method: (1) Reconstruction analysis of three black-box intent-obfuscation methods revealing poor tradeoff balance; (2) proposing concealment-aware variant construction that greedily selects character-removed variants low in harmful-keyword alignment and mutually diverse; (3) instantiating these variants via five modality-aware prompting strategies; (4) introducing keyword-related distractor images providing more effective auxiliary visual context.

Result: Proposed strategies outperform strong baselines on both closed-source and open-source MLLMs, demonstrating that exploiting the model's own reconstruction capability yields more effective jailbreak attacks.

Conclusion: Intent-obfuscation attacks are governed by a reconstruction–concealment tradeoff; character-removed variants achieve a better balance than existing transformations. The proposed concealment-aware construction and keyword-related distractors reveal a new vulnerability where MLLMs' reconstruction ability can be weaponized against their own safety mechanisms.

Abstract: Intent-obfuscation-based jailbreak attacks on multimodal large language models (MLLMs) transform a harmful query into a concealed multimodal input to bypass safety mechanisms. We show that such attacks are governed by a \emph{reconstruction--concealment tradeoff}: the transformed input must hide harmful intent from safety filters while remaining recoverable enough for the victim model to reconstruct the original request. Through a reconstruction analysis of three representative black-box methods, we find that existing transformations struggle to balance this tradeoff, limiting their effectiveness. In contrast, we show that character-removed variants achieve a better balance. Building on this, we propose \emph{concealment-aware variant construction}, which greedily selects character-removed variants that are low in harmful-keyword alignment and mutually diverse, and instantiates them through five modality-aware prompting strategies. We further introduce \emph{keyword-related distractor images} that depict the harmful keyword in diverse contexts, providing more effective auxiliary visual context than generic distractor images. Experiments across closed-source and open-source MLLMs show the proposed strategies outperform strong baselines, revealing an underexplored vulnerability: a model's own reconstruction ability can be exploited to recover hidden harmful intent and produce unsafe responses.

</details>


### [69] [Decodable but Not Corrected by Fixed Residual-Stream Linear Steering: Evidence from Medical LLM Failure Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05715)
*Ming Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM hidden states contain linearly decodable signals for "overthinking" failures in chain-of-thought reasoning, but fixed linear steering cannot correct these failures due to representational entanglement with task-critical computations. However, the same probe enables selective abstention for post-generation reliability estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether linearly decodable failure signals in LLM hidden states can be leveraged to correct those failures, specifically examining the classification-correction gap.

Method: The authors study Overthinking (OT) in medical QA—a stable behavioral regime where models answer correctly under resampling but fail in extended chain-of-thought. They probe linear decodability of OT, test five families of fixed linear steering (29 configurations, n=1,273), measure representational overlap (specificity ratio), perform concept erasure (LEACE), and evaluate selective abstention via the same probe.

Result: OT is linearly decodable at 71.6% balanced accuracy (p<10^{-16}), but all fixed linear steering yields Δ≈0, with null results cross-architecture (Qwen2.5-7B) and cross-domain (MMLU-STEM). Representational entanglement is evidenced by: 85-88% overlap with task-critical computation (specificity ratio ≤0.152), non-targeted steering damaging accuracy (-12.1pp), LEACE erasure damaging accuracy (-3.6pp, p=0.01) vs. +0.3pp for random erasures. Per-instance probe-steering correlation is r=-0.002 (p=0.97). However, the probe enables selective abstention (AUROC=0.610, exceeding all five uncertainty baselines, p=0.009).

Conclusion: Decodable failure structure supports post-generation reliability estimation (selective abstention) even when fixed linear steering cannot exploit it for correction, highlighting a fundamental limitation of linear interventions on entangled representations.

Abstract: Can linearly decodable failure signals in LLM hidden states be leveraged to correct those failures? We investigate this classification-correction gap via Overthinking (OT)--a stable behavioral regime (Jaccard >= 0.81, 94% inter-annotator agreement) in medical QA where models answer correctly under resampling yet fail in extended chain-of-thought. OT is linearly decodable at 71.6% balanced accuracy (p < 10^{-16}). Yet five families of fixed linear steering (29 configurations, n=1,273) all yield Delta ~= 0, with identical null results cross-architecture (Qwen2.5-7B) and cross-domain (MMLU-STEM). Three convergent lines of evidence suggest representational entanglement: the OT direction has 85-88% overlap with task-critical computation (specificity ratio <= 0.152); non-targeted shared-direction steering damages accuracy (-12.1pp); and LEACE concept erasure damages accuracy (-3.6pp, p=0.01), while 10 random erasures produce Delta=+0.3pp. The per-instance probe-steering correlation is r=-0.002 (p=0.97). Positively, the same probe enables selective abstention (held-out AUROC=0.610, exceeding all five uncertainty baselines, p=0.009): decodable failure structure supports post-generation reliability estimation even when the fixed linear steering family cannot exploit it for correction.

</details>


### [70] [Detecting Time Series Anomalies Like an Expert: A Multi-Agent LLM Framework with Specialized Analyzers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05725)
*Hyeongwon Kang,Jeongseob Kim,Jinwoo Park,Pilsung Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAGE is a multi-agent framework that decomposes time-series anomaly detection into four specialized analyzers (point, structural, seasonal, pattern), uses evidence-grounded detection and generates diagnostic reports, achieving state-of-the-art results without requiring real anomaly segments for in-context learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based time-series anomaly detection methods use a single general-purpose model to directly infer anomalies, which lacks controllability, interpretability, and reliability for complex anomaly patterns.

Method: SAGE decomposes anomaly analysis into four specialized Analyzers (point, structural, seasonal, pattern), each using domain-specific tools and visualizations to generate evidence. An evidence-grounded Detector consolidates evidence into confidence-scored anomaly records. A Supervisor converts records into diagnostic reports. Synthetic in-context examples are constructed from normal-reference training segments without using real anomalies.

Result: SAGE achieves the best average performance across three benchmarks compared to strong ML/DL and language-model-based baselines. Ablation studies and human evaluation confirm improved detection reliability and practical usefulness.

Conclusion: The multi-agent decomposition approach with specialized analyzers and evidence-grounded detection significantly improves the controllability, interpretability, and reliability of LLM-based time-series anomaly detection.

Abstract: Recent studies have explored large language models for time-series anomaly detection, yet existing approaches often rely on a single general-purpose model to directly infer anomaly indices or intervals, limiting controllability, interpretability, and reliability for complex anomaly patterns. We propose SAGE (Specialized Analyzer Group for Expert-like Detection), a multi-agent framework for structured anomaly diagnosis in univariate time series. It decomposes anomaly analysis into four specialized Analyzers for point, structural, seasonal, and pattern anomalies. Each Analyzer applies family-specific numerical tools and diagnostic visualizations to generate evidence, while an evidence-grounded Detector consolidates the evidence into confidence-scored anomaly records with intervals and candidate types. A Supervisor then converts these structured records into analyst-facing diagnostic reports. SAGE further constructs synthetic in-context examples from normal-reference training segments, without using real anomalous segments or anomaly-type labels as in-context examples. Across three benchmarks, SAGE achieves the best average performance among strong ML/DL and language-model-based baselines. Ablation studies and human evaluation further show that the proposed framework improves detection reliability and the practical usefulness of diagnostic outputs.

</details>


### [71] [SkillRet: A Large-Scale Benchmark for Skill Retrieval in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05726)
*Hongcheol Cho,Ryangkyung Kang,Youngeun Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillRet is a large-scale benchmark (17,810 skills, 63,259 training samples, 4,997 evaluation queries) for skill retrieval in LLM agents. Current retrievers perform poorly on realistic large-scale skill libraries; task-specific fine-tuning on SkillRet improves NDCG@10 by +13.1 to +16.9 points over prior/off-the-shelf retrievers, with gains attributed to better focus on relevant signals in noisy queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Skill retrieval in LLM agent systems is underexplored despite its practical importance; existing benchmarks and understanding of retrieval behavior on realistic large-scale skill libraries are limited.

Method: Built SkillRet benchmark with 17,810 public agent skills, structured semantic tags, two-level taxonomy (6 major categories, 18 sub-categories), 63,259 training samples, and 4,997 evaluation queries with disjoint skill pools. Evaluated diverse retrievers and analyzed fine-tuning effects.

Result: Off-the-shelf models struggle on large-scale skill libraries; task-specific fine-tuning on SkillRet boosts NDCG@10 by +13.1 points over the best prior retriever and +16.9 points over the best off-the-shelf retriever. Fine-tuned models better focus on small skill-relevant signals in long noisy queries.

Conclusion: SkillRet serves as a strong benchmark and foundation for future research on retrieval in large-scale agent systems, with substantial headroom remaining for improvement.

Abstract: As LLM agents are increasingly deployed with large libraries of reusable skills, selecting the right skill for a user request has become a critical systems challenge. In small libraries, users may invoke skills explicitly by name, but this assumption breaks down as skill ecosystems grow under tight context and latency budgets. Despite its practical importance, skill retrieval remains underexplored, with limited benchmarks and little understanding of retrieval behavior on realistic skill libraries. To address this gap, we introduce SkillRet, a large-scale benchmark for skill retrieval in LLM agents. SkillRet contains 17,810 public agent skills, organized with structured semantic tags and a two-level taxonomy spanning 6 major categories and 18 sub-categories. It provides 63,259 training samples and 4,997 evaluation queries with disjoint skill pools, enabling both benchmarking and retrieval-oriented training. Across a diverse set of retrievers, we find that skill retrieval remains far from solved: off-the-shelf models struggle on realistic large-scale skill libraries, and prior skill-retrieval models still leave substantial headroom. Task-specific fine-tuning on SkillRet substantially improves performance, improving NDCG@10 by +13.1 points over the strongest prior retriever and by +16.9 points over the strongest off-the-shelf retriever. Our analysis further suggests that these gains arise because fine-tuned models better focus on the small skill-relevant signals within long and noisy queries. These results establish SkillRet as a strong benchmark and foundation for future research on retrieval in large-scale agent systems.

</details>


### [72] [Knee Osteoarthritis Severity Grading Using Optimized Deep Learning and LLM-Driven Intelligent AI on Computationally Limited Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05731)
*Dayam Nadeem,Neha,Safdar Mustafa,Adnan Alvi,Mohd Hussain*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes an automated diagnostic approach for knee osteoarthritis severity grading using a ResNet-18 CNN, achieving 94.48% test accuracy on KL-grade classification. The model is optimized and converted to TensorFlow Lite for on-device deployment without internet connectivity, with an auxiliary LLM (Gemini-2.0-flash) providing interpretable clinical findings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Knee osteoarthritis is a prevalent global health issue causing chronic pain and mobility restrictions. Current diagnostic practices suffer from subjectivity and inter-observer variability, highlighting the need for precise, timely, and automated severity assessment.

Method: Uses a ResNet-18 CNN with transfer learning to classify knee images into five Kellgren-Lawrence grades. The model is optimized and converted to TensorFlow Lite for lightweight deployment. An auxiliary LLM (Gemini-2.0-flash) generates structured interpretive findings independent of the classification pipeline.

Result: Achieved 94.48% test accuracy with stable convergence during training. The optimized model was successfully transformed into TensorFlow Lite format, enabling operation on resource-constrained devices without continuous internet connectivity.

Conclusion: The proposed model demonstrates the feasibility of an on-device, interpretable decision-support tool for early KOA diagnosis, improving accessibility to AI-assisted knee screening in environments with limited connectivity.

Abstract: Knee osteoarthritis (KOA) is among the musculoskeletal disorders that considerably restrict joint mobility, cause severe chronic pain and impact negatively on quality life. It is one of the persistent health issues worldwide. Generally, subjectivity and inter-observer variability undermine conventional practices and evaluation process that are adopted to address such health issues. Hence precise and timely diagnosis would be one of the effective ways for the assessment of its severity. This paper proposes an automated diagnostic approach for severity grading of KOA by blending a deep learning convolutional neural network (CNN) with a device-based inference platform powered by TensorFlow Lite. It proposes a model based on the ResNet-18 convolutional neural network. The designed model is trained on publicly available database. Through a transfer learning approach obtained knee images are first classified into five Kellgren-Lawrence (KL) grades. Further the developed model is optimised. During the training of the model test accuracy of 94.48% with stable convergence has been achieved. Subsequently the optimised model transformed into a lightweight TensorFlow Lite format, facilitating seamless deployment on resource-constrained devices. The designed model is capable enough to operate in the environment having no continuous internet connectivity. Also, an auxiliary Large Language Model (Gemini-2.0-flash) is applied to generate structured interpretive findings like potential symptoms, risk factors, and preventive majors etc. The LLM component functions as interface without influencing the classification process. The proposed model articulates the feasibility of an on-device, interpretable decision-support tools for early diagnosis and improve accessibility to Artificial Intelligence (AI)-assisted knee screening tool.

</details>


### [73] [SDFlow: Similarity-Driven Flow Matching for Time Series Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05736)
*Wei Li,Shibo Feng,Pengcheng Wu,Min Wu,Peilin Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SDFlow is a non-autoregressive flow matching framework for time-series generation that operates in the VQ latent space, eliminating exposure bias, reducing high-dimensionality via low-rank manifold decomposition, and incorporating discrete supervision into continuous dynamics, achieving SOTA performance with faster inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive VQ-based time-series generation suffers from exposure bias, where errors accumulate during sequential inference, causing quality degradation in long-horizon generation. The authors aim to overcome this fundamental limitation.

Method: (1) Replaces step-wise autoregressive token prediction with a global transport map via flow matching to eliminate exposure bias; (2) Uses low-rank manifold decomposition with a learned anchor prior to mitigate high-dimensional VQ token spaces; (3) Introduces a categorical posterior over codebook indices within a variational flow-matching formulation to incorporate discrete supervision into continuous transport dynamics.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance with improved Discriminative Score and substantially reduced Context-FID, especially for long-sequence generation. Also provides significant inference speedups over autoregressive baselines.

Conclusion: SDFlow effectively addresses exposure bias in time-series generation while maintaining high fidelity and computational efficiency, offering a promising non-autoregressive alternative to VQ-AR paradigms.

Abstract: Vector quantization (VQ) with autoregressive (AR) token modeling is a widely adopted and highly competitive paradigm for time-series generation. However, such models are fundamentally limited by exposure bias: during inference, errors can accumulate across sequential predictions, leading to pronounced quality degradation in long-horizon generation. To address this, we propose SDFlow ($\textbf{S}$imilarity-$\textbf{D}$riven $\textbf{Flow}$ Matching), a non-autoregressive framework that operates entirely in the frozen VQ latent space and enables parallel sequence generation via flow matching. We tackle three key challenges in making this transition: (1) eliminating exposure bias by replacing step-wise token prediction with a global transport map; (2) mitigating the high-dimensionality of VQ token spaces via a low-rank manifold decomposition with a learned anchor prior over the latent manifold; and (3) incorporating discrete supervision into continuous transport dynamics by introducing a categorical posterior over codebook indices within a variational flow-matching formulation. Extensive experiments show that SDFlow achieves state-of-the-art performance, improving Discriminative Score and substantially reducing Context-FID, particularly for challenging long-sequence generation. Moreover, SDFlow provides significant inference speedups over autoregressive baselines, offering both high fidelity and computational efficiency. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/SDFlow-D6F3/

</details>


### [74] [ReFlect: An Effective Harness System for Complex Long-Horizon LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05737)
*Fan Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReFlect is a deterministic harness system that wraps around LLMs to detect and recover from reasoning errors in long-horizon tasks, achieving per-model gains of +7 to +29 pp over Direct CoT and SWE-bench structural quality improvements from 0% to 82-87%, with gains inversely proportional to baseline success rates (slope=-1.69, r=-0.76).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current reasoning paradigms (CoT, ReAct, self-critique) fail on long-horizon, multi-stage tasks because errors accumulate silently across reasoning steps. The key open question is whether a reasoning system can effectively detect and recover from its own failures.

Method: ReFlect is a model-agnostic, training-free harness that creates standalone error detection and recovery logic as a deterministic wrapper around the LLM, operating entirely at inference time.

Result: Prompt-level self-critique fails: 90/100 reflection blocks flag no issues; models wrongly accept wrong answers in ≥76% cases. ReFlect achieves 41-56% task success rates across 6 models, with per-model gains over Direct CoT of +7 to +29 pp. SWE-bench structural quality rises from 0% to 82-87%. Harness gain is inversely proportional to baseline success (slope=-1.69, r=-0.76). Adding structured reasoning state/operators yields only 15-18.7% on smaller models due to unreliable state population.

Conclusion: ReFlect demonstrates that a deterministic wrapper for error detection and recovery can significantly improve LLM reasoning performance on long-horizon tasks, with the benefit being most pronounced for models with weaker baseline reasoning capabilities.

Abstract: Current reasoning paradigms for LLMs include chain-of-thought, ReAct, and post-hoc self-critique. These paradigms rely on two assumptions that fail on long-horizon, multi-stage tasks. As a result, errors accumulate silently across reasoning steps, leaving an open question: can a reasoning system effectively detect and recover from its own failures? We present ReFlect, a \emph{harness} system for LLM reasoning that creates standalone error detection and recovery logic as a deterministic wrapper around the model. Controlled experiments across 6 reasoning domains show that prompt-level self-critique produces formulaic templates that flag no issues in 90 of 100 audited reflection blocks, and the investigated LLMs wrongly accept a wrong answer in at least 76\% of cases. Our ReFlect harness achieves task success rates ranging from 41\% on gpt-4o-mini to 56\% on Claude Sonnet 4.5 across six models spanning small and frontier scale, with per-model gains over Direct CoT ranging from +7 pp on Qwen2.5-72B to +29 pp on Claude Sonnet 4.5, and additionally raises SWE-bench patch-structural quality from 0\% (Direct CoT) to between 82\% (Qwen2.5-72B) and 87\% (GPT-4o). Notably, the harness gain is inversely proportional to the model's Direct CoT task success rate (the fitted slope is -1.69 with r=-0.76): each pp lost in baseline success rate is mechanically recovered by 1.69 pp of harness gain. We spot that adding structured reasoning state and operators yields only 15.0--18.7\% pair-mean on Llama-3.3-70B and Qwen2.5-72B because models at this scale cannot reliably populate the state its operators require. ReFlect is model-agnostic, training-free, and operates entirely at inference time.

</details>


### [75] [HyperLens: Quantifying Cognitive Effort in LLMs with Fine-grained Confidence Trajectory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05741)
*Chengda Lu,Xiaoyu Fan,Wei Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HyperLens is a high-resolution probe that traces confidence trajectories across transformer layers, revealing that complex tasks require higher cognitive effort. It diagnoses that standard SFT can reduce cognitive effort and degrade in-domain performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM inference dynamics are poorly understood due to limited resolution of existing analysis tools; the paper aims to uncover fine-grained confidence patterns during inference.

Method: Identifies an intrinsic magnification mechanism in transformers where deeper layers amplify small changes in layer-wise confidence. Introduces HyperLens, a high-resolution probe to trace confidence trajectories and quantify cognitive effort.

Result: HyperLens reveals a consistent divergence in confidence trajectories separating complex from simple tasks. Complex tasks require higher cognitive effort. Standard SFT reduces cognitive effort, degrading in-domain performance.

Conclusion: Confidence trajectories provide a principled metric for cognitive effort; SFT's side effect of reducing cognitive effort explains performance degradation on in-domain tasks.

Abstract: While Large Language Models (LLMs) achieve strong performance across diverse tasks, their inference dynamics remain poorly understood because of the limited resolution of existing analysis tools. In this work, we identify an intrinsic magnification mechanism in transformer architectures: deeper layers inherently magnify the small changes of layer-wise confidence, providing a fine-grained confidence trajectory. Building on this insight, we introduce HyperLens, a high-resolution probe designed to trace confidence trajectories and quantify the cognitive effort during inference. Across LLMs and datasets, HyperLens reveals a consistent divergence in confidence trajectories that separates complex from simple tasks. We abstract this pattern into a quantitative cognitive effort metric. Our analysis reveals a fundamental principle: complex tasks consistently require higher cognitive effort. Finally, we provide a mechanistic diagnosis of a common side effect of standard Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT): it can reduce cognitive effort and consequently degrade performance on in-domain tasks.

</details>


### [76] [Best Arm Identification in Generalized Linear Bandits via Hybrid Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05745)
*Qirun Zeng,Xuchuang Wang,Jiayi Shen,Xutong Liu,Fang Kong,Jinhang Zuo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a hybrid Track-and-Stop algorithm for fixed-confidence best arm identification in generalized linear bandits under a hybrid feedback model (absolute and relative/dueling feedback), using a likelihood-ratio-based confidence sequence with self-concordance, and extends to cost-aware settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable efficient best arm identification when the learner can query both absolute reward feedback from a single arm and relative (dueling) feedback from an arm pair, which are governed by generalized linear models, addressing the lack of unified algorithms for hybrid feedback modalities.

Method: Introduces a likelihood-ratio-based confidence sequence that unifies heterogeneous generalized linear observations into an explicit ellipsoidal confidence set under self-concordance. Proposes a hybrid Track-and-Stop algorithm that adaptively allocates queries by tracking a minimax-optimal design over a joint action space of arms and pairs. Extends to cost-aware settings with heterogeneous acquisition costs.

Result: Establishes δ-correctness and provides high-probability upper bounds on stopping time. Empirical experiments show significant improvements in sample efficiency over baseline methods.

Conclusion: The hybrid feedback model and the proposed algorithm effectively unify different feedback types for best arm identification, offering theoretical guarantees and practical efficiency gains.

Abstract: We study fixed-confidence best arm identification in generalized linear bandits under a hybrid feedback model: at each round, the learner may query either (i) absolute reward feedback from a single arm or (ii) relative (dueling) feedback from an arm pair, both governed by generalized linear models. We introduce a likelihood-ratio--based confidence sequence that unifies heterogeneous generalized linear observations and yields an explicit ellipsoidal confidence set under a self-concordance assumption. Building on this confidence set, we propose a hybrid Track-and-Stop algorithm that adaptively allocates queries by tracking a minimax-optimal design over a joint action space of arms and pairs. We establish $δ$-correctness and provide high-probability upper bounds on the stopping time. We further extend the framework to a cost-aware setting that accounts for heterogeneous acquisition costs across feedback modalities. Empirical experiments demonstrate that the proposed algorithms significantly improve sample efficiency over baseline methods.

</details>


### [77] [Evaluating Explainability in Safety-Critical ATR Systems: Limitations of Post-Hoc Methods and Paths Toward Robust XAI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05748)
*Vanessa Buhrmester,David Muench,Dimitri Bulatov,Michael Arens*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper evaluates explainability methods for safety-critical Automatic Target Recognition (ATR) systems, identifying limitations like spurious explanations, instability, and overtrust, and calling for more robust, causally grounded approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High predictive performance alone is insufficient for deploying machine learning in safety-critical ATR systems; decisions must also be interpretable, reliable, and suitable for validation.

Method: The authors identify major XAI paradigms (saliency-based, attention-based, surrogate approaches, detection-aware extensions), formalize explainability as an assurance-oriented assessment problem, introduce a taxonomy, and evaluate methods across four dimensions: interpretability, robustness, vulnerability to manipulation, and suitability for validation/verification.

Result: Systematic limitations of post-hoc explanation methods are identified, including spurious explanations, instability under perturbations, and overtrust induced by visually convincing outputs. Widely used XAI techniques may be insufficient for safety-critical deployment.

Conclusion: The field must move beyond visually plausible explanations toward more robust, causally grounded, and physically informed methods that support reliable decision-making and system-level assurance in ATR systems.

Abstract: Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI) is increasingly rec ognized as essential for deploying machine learning systems in safety critical environments. In Automatic Target Recognition (ATR), where models operate on image, video, radar, and multisensor data, high pre dictive performance alone is insufficient. Model decisions must also be interpretable, reliable, and suitable for validation. This paper presents a structured evaluation of explainability methods in the context of safety-critical ATR systems: We identify major XAI paradigms, including saliency-based, attention-based, and surrogate ap proaches, as well as recent detection-aware extensions.
