Based on this, we formalize explainability as an assurance-oriented assessment problem, introduce a taxonomy, and assess these methods with respect to four key dimensions: interpretability, robustness, vulnerability to manipula tion, and suitability for validation and verification. The analysis identifies systematic limitations of current post-hoc explanation methods. In par ticular, we derive critical failure modes such as spurious explanations, instability under perturbations, and overtrust induced by visually con vincing outputs. These findings indicate that widely used XAI techniques may be insufficient for safety-critical deployment. Finally, we discuss implications for ATR systems and outline directions toward more robust, causally grounded, and physically informed explain ability methods. Our results emphasize the need to move beyond visually plausible explanations toward approaches that support reliable decision making and system-level assurance.

</details>


### [78] [Confidence is the key: how conformal prediction enhances the generative design of permeable peptides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05770)
*Laura van Weesep,Sunay Chankeshwara,Leonardo De Maria,Florian David,Ola Engkvist,Gökçe Geylan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents an RL-guided generative framework for designing permeable cyclic peptides, integrating conformal prediction (CP) as an uncertainty quantification method to address predictive model reliability outside its domain of applicability, improving both reliability and efficiency of peptide optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative models with RL can suggest molecules outside the predictor's domain of applicability, leading to unreliable predictions and suboptimal designs. This is especially problematic for cyclic peptides, which are understudied yet therapeutically promising for intracellular targeting due to challenges in identifying optimal permeable designs.

Method: RL-guided generative framework (e.g., REINVENT/PepINVENT style) using an uncertainty-aware permeability predictor with conformal prediction (CP) as the uncertainty quantification method, operating under a user-defined confidence level to assess and reward generated peptides.

Result: Rewarding generated peptides with CP-informed predictions improves both reliability and efficiency of the peptide optimization process, and discourages exploration outside the predictor's applicability domain.

Conclusion: This is the first work combining generative modeling and conformal prediction for molecular design, bridging the gap between predictive uncertainty and RL-guided exploration.

Abstract: Generative models coupled with reinforcement learning (RL), such as REINVENT and PepINVENT, have emerged as a powerful framework for de novo molecular design. During the ideation process these generative frameworks utilize various predictive models as part of the optimization objectives. However, the utility of the predictive models can be limited by their domain of applicability. When RL is used to explore the chemical space with predictive models, it can suggest molecules that lie outside the predictor's domain of applicability. As a result, the predictions may become less reliable, potentially steering designs into high reward but also high uncertainty chemical spaces. This is particularly pronounced for cyclic peptides which show therapeutic promise due to their modifiability and large interaction surfaces but are understudied compared to small molecules. While passive membrane permeation in cyclic peptides has attracted interest, identifying optimal permeable designs remains challenging yet crucial for targeting intracellular sites. We present an RL-guided generative framework that designs permeable cyclic peptides using an uncertainty-aware permeability predictor as the scoring component. To address predictive uncertainty, especially impacted by novel chemistry, we integrate conformal prediction (CP) as our uncertainty quantification method. CP assesses designs based on the calibrated model under a user-defined confidence level. We demonstrate that rewarding generated peptides with CP-informed predictions improves both reliability and efficiency of peptide optimization process. This also discourages exploration outside the predictor's applicability domain. This approach bridges the gap between predictive uncertainty and RL-guided exploration, showing how generative modelling and conformal prediction can be combined for the first time.

</details>


### [79] [CircuitFormer: A Circuit Language Model for Analog Topology Design from Natural Language Prompt](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05773)
*Md Touhidul Islam,Sujan Kumar Saha,Farimah Farahmandi,Mark Tehranipoor*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces CircuitFormer, a 511M-parameter transformer for analog circuit netlist generation, addressing data scarcity and tokenization inefficiency. It curates the largest analog circuit dataset (31,341 pairs) and proposes CKT tokenizer that mines frequent subcircuits with O(1) vocabulary complexity. CKT reduces sequence length by 57% with 2.3x better compression. CircuitFormer achieves 100% syntactic correctness and 83% functional success, outperforming open-source LLMs by 10-14% with 240x fewer parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Analog circuit design automation remains a longstanding EDA challenge. Existing LLMs struggle due to (i) scarcity of annotated analog design datasets and (ii) inefficiency of general-purpose tokenizers like BPE in capturing circuit graph structures.

Method: (1) Curated the largest annotated analog circuit dataset (31,341 netlist-description pairs). (2) Proposed Circuit Tokenizer (CKT), a graph tokenizer that mines frequent subcircuits, achieving O(1) vocabulary complexity vs prior O(n_max). (3) Trained CircuitFormer, a 511M encoder-decoder transformer using CKT tokenization.

Result: CKT reduces sequence length by 57% and achieves 2.3x compression ratio with vocabulary size 512. CircuitFormer achieves 100% syntactic correctness and 83% functional success rate, outperforming open-source LLMs by 10% and 14% respectively, with 240x fewer parameters.

Conclusion: Circuit-specific tokenization (CKT) and curated datasets enable highly efficient and accurate analog circuit generation, demonstrating that domain-adapted transformers can outperform much larger general-purpose LLMs in specialized EDA tasks.

Abstract: Automating analog circuit design remains a longstanding challenge in Electronic Design Automation (EDA). While Transformer-based Large Language Models (LLMs) have revolutionized software code generation, their application to analog hardware design is hindered by two critical limitations: (i) the scarcity of analog design datasets containing natural language description of a design and its corresponding netlist, and (ii) the inefficiency of general-purpose tokenizers (e.g., Byte Pair Encoding (BPE)) in capturing the inherent graph structure of circuits. To bridge this gap, first, we curate the largest annotated dataset of analog circuit netlists to date, comprising 31,341 netlist-natural language description pairs across all major circuit classes. Furthermore, we propose Circuit Tokenizer (CKT), a novel circuit graph tokenizer designed to encode netlist connectivity by explicitly mining frequent subcircuits. In terms of scalability, CKT overcomes the bottleneck of prior circuit graph serialization methods where vocabulary size scales linearly with maximum number of components in the dataset, n_max, (O(n_max)); instead, CKT decouples vocabulary growth from circuit complexity, achieving a constant O(1) complexity. Empirically, CKT outperforms standard BPE on circuit topology representation, reducing sequence length by 57% and achieving a 2.3x superior compression ratio using a compact, fixed vocabulary of size 512. Leveraging this optimized tokenization, we train a circuit-specific language model, CircuitFormer, a 511M parameter encoder-decoder transformer. Our model achieves 100% syntactic correctness and an 83% functional success rate across all major analog circuit categories, outperforming state-of-the-art open-source LLMs by 10% and 14%, respectively, while requiring 240x fewer parameters. The dataset is publicly available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/touhid314/cktformer-dataset.

</details>


### [80] [HEDP: A Hybrid Energy-Distance Prompt-based Framework for Domain Incremental Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05776)
*Yu Feng,Zhen Tian,Haoran Luo,Xie Yu,Diancheng Cheng,Haoyue Zheng,Shuai Lyu,Ping Zong,Lianyuan Li,Xin Ge,Yifan Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HEDP is a domain-incremental learning framework using Helmholtz free energy-inspired hybrid energy-distance prompts to improve domain representation separability and selection, achieving 2.57% accuracy gain on benchmarks like CORe50.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Domain shifts in incremental learning cause severe performance degradation and catastrophic forgetting; existing methods lack effective mechanisms to maintain separability across evolving domains and generalize to unseen domains without retraining.

Method: Proposes Hybrid Energy-Distance Prompt (HEDP) with (1) an energy regularization loss inspired by Helmholtz free energy to enhance domain representation separability, and (2) a hybrid energy-distance weighted mechanism that fuses energy-based and distance-based cues for improved domain selection and generalization.

Result: Achieves 2.57% accuracy gain on multiple benchmarks including CORe50, effectively mitigating catastrophic forgetting and enhancing open-world adaptability on unseen domains.

Conclusion: HEDP demonstrates that energy-based regularization and hybrid weighting effectively address domain incremental learning challenges, offering superior generalization to unseen domains.

Abstract: Domain Incremental Learning is a critical scenario that requires models to continuously adapt to new data domains without retraining. However, domain shifts often cause severe performance degradation. To address this, we propose Hybrid Energy-Distance Prompt, a domain-incremental framework inspired by Helmholtz free energy. HEDP introduces an energy regularization loss to enhance the separability of domain representations and a hybrid energy-distance weighted mechanism that fuses energy-based and distance-based cues to improve domain selection and generalization. Experiments on multiple benchmarks, including CORe50, show that HEDP achieves superior performance on unseen domains with a 2.57\% accuracy gain, effectively mitigating catastrophic forgetting and enhancing open-world adaptability. Our code is \href{https://github.com/dannis97500/HEDP/}{available here}.

</details>


### [81] [Von Neumann Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05780)
*Shekhar S. Chandra*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Von Neumann Neuron, a differentiable artificial neuron based on John von Neumann's cellular array model, and constructs Von Neumann Networks (VNNs)—self-engineering neural networks whose architecture is determined by input/output locations on a cellular grid. VNNs leverage neural operators, Green's functions, and diffusion processes, are proven computationally universal via Cellular Machines, and empirically outperform standard MLPs on basic tasks with greater parameter efficiency while enabling new task paradigms and extending von Neumann architecture concepts to cellular computing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To revive and develop John von Neumann's mid-20th-century cellular array model of the brain within a modern deep learning framework, enabling learnable specialized neuron roles and architecturally self-engineering neural networks.

Method: The Von Neumann neuron is defined on a cellular array with finite states learned via gradient descent. VNNs are constructed by arranging such neurons based on input/output spatial locations; the mathematical framework extends neural operators and learns Green's functions via convolutions on a cellular topology with a diffusion signature. Computational universality is proved by embedding VNNs into a more general Cellular Machines formalism.

Result: VNN-based multi-layered perceptrons outperform standard deep learning MLPs on basic tasks, are more parameter-efficient, and can learn new task types. They also enable construction of a cellular extension of the Von Neumann hardware architecture.

Conclusion: Von Neumann Networks represent a novel class of self-engineering neural networks with theoretical grounding in diffusion processes and Green's functions, offering improved efficiency and expanded capability over conventional architectures, with promising directions for cellular computing.

Abstract: In the mid-twentieth century, mathematician and polymath John von Neumann created a computational system on an array of cells as a simple model of the human brain, where each cell had one of a finite set of roles or states that he predicted would be modelled by a diffusion process. In this work, we show that such a system, when developed in a modern deep learning setting, enables the construction of an artificial neuron having specialized roles that can be learnt. We refer to this neuron as the Von Neumann neuron, and the resulting neural network from such neurons result in a self-engineered design whose architecture is only dependent on the structure and locations of its inputs and outputs on this cellular array. The mathematical framework for these Von Neumann Networks (VNNs) is also constructed and shows that they are based on the extension of neural operators and the learning of Green's functions with convolutions on a cellular topology having a diffusion signature. We also prove that these VNNs are part of a more general computational system called Cellular Machines that are computationally universal. Initial experiments show that VNN based multi-layered perceptrons outperform their equivalent deep learning variant on basic tasks, while being more parameter efficient and are capable of learning new types of tasks. This includes the ability to solve for and construct an extension of the Von Neumann (hardware) architecture common to all modern computers to cells and suggests new opportunities that could be explored.

</details>


### [82] [Sheet as Token: A Graph-Enhanced Representation for Multi-Sheet Spreadsheet Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05811)
*Yiming Lei,Yiqi Wang,Yujia Zhang,Bo Guan,Depei Zhu,Chunhui Wang,Zhuonan Hao,Tianyu Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes "Sheet as Token," a graph-enhanced framework that treats each worksheet as a unified semantic unit (encoded as a dense token) for multi-sheet spreadsheet retrieval, using a Graph Retriever with multi-stage graph transformer to compose semantic, query-conditioned, schema-consistency, and shape-compatibility relations for supporting sheet set retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Workbook-scale spreadsheet understanding is challenging because information is distributed across multiple sheets with heterogeneous schemas, layouts, and implicit relationships, and existing chunk-centric retrieval approaches fragment worksheets into isolated text spans, weakening global sheet-level semantics.

Method: The method extracts schema-aware records (sheet names, column headers, representative values, layout features), encodes each worksheet into a compact dense token, and uses a Graph Retriever to construct a query-specific candidate graph over sheet tokens using semantic, query-conditioned, schema-consistency, and shape-compatibility relations, composed via a multi-stage graph transformer.

Result: Experiments on a constructed multi-sheet spreadsheet corpus show that sheet-level tokenization learns stable representations, and graph-enhanced cross-sheet reasoning improves listwise retrieval over a shallow graph baseline with limited additional graph-side computation.

Conclusion: Sheet-level tokenization is a promising abstraction for scalable multi-sheet spreadsheet understanding.

Abstract: Workbook-scale spreadsheet understanding is increasingly important for language-model-based data analysis agents, but remains challenging because relevant information is often distributed across multiple sheets with heterogeneous schemas, layouts, and implicit relationships. Existing retrieval-augmented approaches typically decompose spreadsheets into rows, columns, or blocks to improve scalability; however, such chunk-centric representations can fragment worksheets into isolated text spans and weaken global sheet-level semantics. We propose Sheet as Token, a graph-enhanced framework that treats each worksheet as a unified semantic unit for multi-sheet spreadsheet retrieval. Our method extracts schema-aware records from sheet names, column headers, representative values, and layout features, and encodes each worksheet into a compact dense token. Given a natural-language query, a Graph Retriever constructs a query-specific candidate graph over sheet tokens using semantic, query-conditioned, schema-consistency, and shape-compatibility relations, and composes these channels through a multi-stage graph transformer to retrieve supporting sheet sets. Experiments on a constructed multi-sheet spreadsheet corpus show that sheet-level tokenization learns stable representations, and that graph-enhanced cross-sheet reasoning improves listwise retrieval over a shallow graph baseline with limited additional graph-side computation. These results suggest that sheet-level tokenization is a promising abstraction for scalable multi-sheet spreadsheet understanding.

</details>


### [83] [Long-Horizon Q-Learning: Accurate Value Learning via n-Step Inequalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05812)
*Armaan A. Abraham,Lucy Xiaoyang Shi,Chelsea Finn*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LQL introduces a principled backstop against compounding error in off-policy Q-learning by using a hinge loss to penalize violations of optimality bounds derived from action sequences, consistently outperforming standard TD methods across benchmarks without requiring extra computational overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Off-policy value-based RL (e.g., Q-learning) suffers from brittle long-horizon learning due to bootstrapping: estimation errors at later states propagate backward through TD updates and compound over time.

Method: LQL leverages a prior optimality tightening observation (action sequences lower-bound optimal policy performance) and turns it into a practical stabilization mechanism by using a hinge loss to penalize bound violations, computed from network outputs already produced for TD error (no auxiliary networks or extra forward passes).

Result: Combined with multiple state-of-the-art methods across online and offline-to-online benchmarks, LQL consistently outperforms both 1-step TD and n-step TD learning at similar runtime.

Conclusion: LQL provides an effective, computationally lightweight mechanism to mitigate compounding errors in Q-learning, advancing long-horizon off-policy RL.

Abstract: Off-policy, value-based reinforcement learning methods such as Q-learning are appealing because they can learn from arbitrary experience, including data collected by older policies or other agents. In practice, however, bootstrapping makes long-horizon learning brittle: estimation errors at later states propagate backward through temporal-difference (TD) updates and can compound over time. We propose long-horizon Q-learning (LQL), which introduces a principled backstop against compounding error when learning the optimal action-value function. LQL builds on a prior optimality tightening observation: any realized action sequence lower-bounds what the optimal policy can achieve in expectation, so acting optimally earlier should not be worse than following the observed actions for several steps before switching to optimal behavior. Our contribution is to turn this inequality into a practical stabilization mechanism for Q-learning by using a hinge loss to penalize violations of these bounds. Importantly, LQL computes these penalties using network outputs already produced for the TD error, requiring no auxiliary networks and no additional forward passes relative to Q-learning. When combined with multiple state-of-the-art methods on a range of online and offline-to-online benchmarks, LQL consistently outperforms both 1-step TD and n-step TD learning at similar runtime.

</details>


### [84] [AGPO: Asymmetric Group Policy Optimization for Verifiable Reasoning and Search Ads Relevance at JD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05826)
*Yang Xu,Kun Yao,Yiming Deng,Zheng Fang,Kai Ming Ting,Ming Pang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AGPO is a new RLVR method that uses negative-dominant reinforcement and group advantage mechanism to prevent reasoning capability shrinkage in LLMs, achieving SOTA accuracy on math benchmarks and improving real-world search ad relevance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RLVR methods improve sampling efficiency but narrow the reasoning capability boundary of trained models compared to base models, especially at large sample sizes (lower pass@k coverage).

Method: Asymmetric Group Policy Optimization (AGPO): (1) Negative-dominant reinforcement strategy to suppress incorrect reasoning paths while preserving exploration capacity; (2) Group advantage mechanism that scales positive updates based on intra-group variance, focusing on rare correct paths and suppressing trivial paths.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art accuracy on five mathematical benchmarks, consistently improves pass@k performance at scale, and enhances search ads relevance in a large-scale industrial application, boosting downstream student model performance.

Conclusion: AGPO effectively counteracts boundary shrinkage in RLVR training, maintaining exploration capacity while improving accuracy, with demonstrated success in both mathematical reasoning and practical industrial applications.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has demonstrated notable success in enhancing the reasoning performance of large language models (LLMs). However, recent studies reveal that while current RLVR methods improve sampling efficiency towards correct paths, they do not elicit fundamentally new reasoning patterns. Instead, the reasoning capability boundary of trained models often narrows compared to their base models, with base models achieving higher coverage at large sample sizes. In this work, we propose Asymmetric Group Policy Optimization (AGPO) to counteract this boundary shrinkage. AGPO adopts a negative-dominant reinforcement strategy to suppress incorrect reasoning paths, maintaining the base model's exploration capacity. For positive reinforcement, AGPO adopts a group advantage mechanism, which scales positive updates based on intra-group variance, allowing the model to focus on rare correct paths while suppressing updates from trivial paths. Our experiments on five mathematical benchmarks demonstrate that AGPO achieves state-of-the-art accuracy while consistently improving pass@$k$ performance at scale. In a large-scale industrial application for search ads relevance optimization, AGPO effectively enhances the quality of the data annotation, leading to substantial performance gains in downstream student models.

</details>


### [85] [On the Role of Language Representations in Auto-Bidding: Findings and Implications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05833)
*Guanyu Zhu,Jining Luan,Hanwen Du,Xinyu Fang,Sibo Xu,Ersheng Ni,Hongji Li,Jincheng Fang,Ronghao Chen,Huacan Wang,Xuanqi Lan,Yongxin Ni,Yiqi Sun,Youhua Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SemBid integrates LLM-encoded semantic information (Task, History, Strategy) into offline auto-bidding trajectories via token-level injection and self-attention, outperforming competitive baselines in performance, constraint satisfaction, and robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing auto-bidding methods use compact numerical state representations that implicitly capture delivery dynamics but lack support for explicitly encoding high-level intent, evolving feedback, and operator-style strategic guidance. LLMs offer semantic encoding capabilities, but it is unclear when they help and how to integrate them without sacrificing numerical precision.

Method: SemBid injects LLM-encoded semantic inputs (Task, History, Strategy) as tokens alongside numerical trajectory tokens, using self-attention to integrate semantic and numeric information at the token level for offline auto-bidding.

Result: Across diverse scenarios and budget regimes, SemBid outperforms competitive baselines from offline RL and generative sequence modeling, with more consistent gains in overall performance, constraint satisfaction, and robustness.

Conclusion: Semantic--numeric integration via token-level injection improves controllability and generalization across objectives in auto-bidding, outperforming both purely numerical and naive concatenation approaches.

Abstract: Auto-bidding is a crucial task in real-time advertising markets, where policies must optimize long-horizon value under delivery constraints (e.g., budget and CPA). Existing methods for auto-bidding rely on compact numerical state representations: while they can implicitly capture delivery dynamics, they offer limited support for explicitly representing and controlling high-level intent, evolving feedback, and operator-style strategic guidance in real campaigns. Meanwhile, Large Language Models (LLMs) offer a powerful method for encoding semantic information, it remains unclear when LLMs help and how to integrate them without sacrificing numerical precision. Through systematic preliminary studies, we find that (1) LLM embeddings contain bidding-relevant cues yet cannot replace numerical features, and (2) gains emerge only with careful semantic--numeric integration rather than naive concatenation. Motivated by these findings, we propose \textit{SemBid}, a novel auto-bidding framework that injects LLM-encoded semantics into offline bidding trajectories at the token level. SemBid introduces three semantic inputs: \textit{Task}, \textit{History}, and \textit{Strategy}. It injects these semantics as tokens alongside numerical trajectory tokens and uses self-attention to integrate them, improving controllability and generalization across objectives. Across diverse scenarios and budget regimes, SemBid outperforms competitive baselines from offline RL and generative sequence modeling, with more consistent gains in overall performance, constraint satisfaction, and robustness. Our code is available at: \href{https://github.com/AlanYu04/SemBid-KDD2026}{\textcolor{blue}{here}}.

</details>


### [86] [Taklif.AI: LLM-Powered Platform for Interest-Based Personalized College Assignments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05842)
*Zaki Kurdya,Mohammed Zuqlam,Salem Amassi,Shady Telbany,Motaz Saad*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Taklif.AI is a platform using LLMs (Llama 3.3 70B) to automatically generate personalized assignments based on students' extracurricular interests and cultural contexts, incorporating structured prompt engineering with guardrails and a serverless AWS architecture. Preliminary testing with 68 participants showed 84% found personalization beneficial.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Educators struggle to create engaging, personalized assignments for diverse students; traditional one-size-fits-all approaches reduce engagement and increase plagiarism.

Method: Taklif.AI uses Llama 3.3 70B via LiteLLM for multi-provider load balancing, LangChain for prompt orchestration, and a structured prompt engineering pipeline with input/output guardrails on a serverless AWS architecture with Next.js.

Result: User acceptance testing with 68 participants (65 students, 3 educators) found 84% rated the personalization feature as beneficial.

Conclusion: The platform shows positive initial reception for personalized assignment generation, with plans for rigorous empirical evaluation of learning outcomes.

Abstract: Educators face significant challenges in creating engaging, personalized assignments that accommodate students' diverse interests and cognitive abilities. Traditional one-size-fits-all assignments frequently lead to decreased student engagement and increased reliance on unethical practices such as plagiarism. To address these challenges, we present Taklif.AI, a platform that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to automatically generate personalized assignments tailored to individual student interests. Unlike existing AI-powered educational platforms that personalize based on academic performance metrics alone, Taklif.AI incorporates students' extracurricular interests and cultural contexts into the assignment generation process through a structured prompt engineering pipeline with input and output guardrails. The platform employs a serverless architecture on AWS with Next.js, using Llama 3.3 70B as the primary LLM via LiteLLM for multi-provider load balancing and LangChain for prompt orchestration. We describe the system architecture, the prompt design methodology, and the guardrails framework that ensures output quality. Preliminary user acceptance testing with 68 participants (65 students and 3 educators) indicates positive reception, with 84% of participants rating the personalization feature as beneficial. We discuss the platform's current capabilities and limitations, and outline directions for rigorous empirical evaluation of learning outcomes.

</details>


### [87] [AirQualityBench: A Realistic Evaluation Benchmark for Global Air Quality Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05854)
*Xing Xu,Xu Wang,Yudong Zhang,Huilin Zhao,Zhengyang Zhou,Yang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AirQualityBench is a global multi-pollutant benchmark for evaluating air-quality forecasting models under realistic monitoring conditions, using 3,720 stations across 2021-2025, preserving missingness as part of the forecasting problem rather than imputing data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing air-quality forecasting evaluations use sanitized datasets (removed/completed missing observations) that hide real-world challenges: uneven global coverage, structured missingness, heterogeneous pollutant scales, and deployment cost. The gap between sanitized evaluation and real monitoring networks motivates a more realistic benchmark.

Method: The benchmark collects hourly data from 3,720 monitoring stations globally (2021-2025) covering six pollutants, preserves provider-native observation masks without imputation, reports errors on valid future observations after inverse transformation to physical concentration scales, and uses a unified evaluation protocol.

Result: Strong performance on sanitized datasets does not reliably transfer to global, fragmented monitoring streams when evaluated under the realistic AirQualityBench protocol.

Conclusion: AirQualityBench serves as a realistic testbed for scalable, mask-aware, and physically interpretable air-quality forecasting. It exposes missingness as part of the forecasting problem rather than preprocessing it away.

Abstract: Air-quality forecasting models are commonly evaluated on regional, preprocessed, and normalized datasets, where missing observations are removed or artificially completed. Such protocols simplify comparison but hide the conditions that dominate real monitoring networks: uneven global coverage, structured missingness, heterogeneous pollutant scales, and deployment cost. We introduce \textbf{AirQualityBench}, a global multi-pollutant benchmark designed to evaluate forecasting models under these realistic conditions. The benchmark contains hourly observations from 3,720 monitoring stations over 2021--2025, covers six major pollutants, and preserves provider-native observation masks. Rather than imputing a dense data tensor, AirQualityBench exposes missingness as part of the forecasting problem and reports errors on valid future observations after inverse transformation to physical concentration scales. Evaluating representative spatio-temporal models under this unified protocol shows that strong performance on sanitized datasets does not reliably transfer to global, fragmented monitoring streams. AirQualityBench therefore serves as a realistic testbed for scalable, mask-aware, and physically interpretable air-quality forecasting. All benchmark data, code, evaluation scripts, and baseline implementations are available at \href{https://github.com/Star-Learning/AirQualityBench}{GitHub}.

</details>


### [88] [SANEmerg: An Emergent Communication Framework for Semantic-aware Agentic AI Networking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05861)
*Yong Xiao,Haoran Zhou,Yujie Zhou,Marwan Krunz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SANEmerg is a multi-agent emergent communication framework for semantic-aware AgentNet systems that enables bandwidth-adaptive, computationally bounded signaling protocols to emerge among collaborative AI agents, achieving superior task accuracy while reducing bandwidth and computational overhead compared to state-of-the-art solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional networking paradigms suffer from rigid decoupling of communication and computation, causing inefficiencies in large-scale agentic AI networking (AgentNet) systems. Emergent communication offers a solution by enabling autonomous agents to develop task-specific signaling protocols, but existing approaches lack bandwidth and computational constraints tailored for real-time agentic ecosystems.

Method: The paper proposes SANEmerg, a multi-agent emergent communication framework with two key components: (1) a bandwidth-adaptable importance-filter that dynamically prioritizes higher-contribution message dimensions under bandwidth limits, and (2) a complexity-regularizer based on the Minimum Description Length (MDL) principle to enforce computationally bounded signaling. The framework operates in a semantic-aware setting where user intent is detected, inferred, and decomposed into sub-tasks for agent assignment.

Result: Evaluated via an AgentNet prototype and extensive experimentation, SANEmerg demonstrates significant performance improvements over state-of-the-art solutions, achieving superior task accuracy while substantially reducing bandwidth and computational overhead.

Conclusion: SANEmerg effectively enables emergent communication for collaborative task fulfillment in bandwidth-constrained and computationally bounded AgentNet environments, paving the way for efficient agentic AI-native networking ecosystems.

Abstract: Future networking systems are envisioned to become part of an agentic AI-native ecosystem in which a vast number of heterogeneous and specialized AI agents cooperate seamlessly to fulfill complex user requirements in real time. However, traditional networking paradigms are characterized by a rigid decoupling of communication and computation, which often leads to significant inefficiencies in large-scale agentic AI networking (AgentNet) systems. Emergent communication offers a novel solution by enabling autonomous agents that support task-specific signaling protocols for information exchange and collaborative coordination. In this paper, we consider a multi-agent emergent communication framework, tailored for semantic-aware AgentNet systems in which the user's semantic intent can be automatically detected, inferred, and linked to a set of sub-tasks to be assigned to a set of agents. We investigate how communication and signaling protocols can emerge among collaborative agents with computationally bounded intelligence under stringent bandwidth constraints. Our proposed framework, called SANEmerg, is designed to facilitate the emergence of communication for collaborative task fulfillment while adhering to the physical limits of AgentNet. SANEmerg incorporates a bandwidth-adaptable importance-filter that dynamically prioritizes the transmission of higher-contribution message dimensions, ensuring robust performance in bandwidth-limited environments. Furthermore, SANEmerg integrates a complexity-regularizer grounded in the Minimum Description Length (MDL) principle to facilitate the emergence of computationally bounded signaling. Evaluated via an AgentNet prototype and extensive experimentation, SANEmerg demonstrates significant performance improvements over state-of-the-art solutions, achieving superior task accuracy while significantly reducing bandwidth and computational overhead.

</details>


### [89] [XDecomposer: Learning Prior-Free Set Decomposition for Multiphase X-ray Diffraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05866)
*Hanyu Gao,Bin Cao,Yunyue Su,Tong-Yi Zhang,Qiang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: XDecomposer is a deep learning framework that jointly decomposes and identifies multiple crystalline phases from powder X-ray diffraction patterns without requiring prior knowledge of phase candidates or phase numbers, treating the problem as a set prediction task with phase-query mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multiphase PXRD analysis remains a bottleneck because real-world synthesis produces complex mixtures that cannot be reliably disentangled. Existing representation-based approaches assume single-phase inputs and fail in multiphase settings.

Method: Formulates multiphase diffraction analysis as a set prediction problem using a phase-query-driven decomposition mechanism and diffraction-consistent physical reconstruction within a unified architecture to infer unordered sets of phase-resolved components, their proportions, and structural representations.

Result: Substantially improves reconstruction accuracy and phase identification across diverse chemical systems on both simulated and experimental datasets, with strong generalization to unseen mixtures.

Conclusion: Provides a practical data-driven route toward source-resolved multiphase XRD analysis, reducing the long-standing dependence on prior-guided iterative phase matching.

Abstract: Multiphase powder X-ray diffraction (PXRD) analysis remains a fundamental bottleneck in structure identification, as real-world synthesis often produces complex mixtures whose constituent phases (components) cannot be reliably disentangled. While recent advances in representation-based crystal retrieval and generation suggest the possibility of inferring structures directly from PXRD, existing approaches largely assume single-phase inputs and break down in multiphase settings. Here, we present XDecomposer, a prior-free framework for joint decomposition and identification of multiphase XRD patterns without requiring candidate phase lists, structural templates, or prior knowledge of phase number. We formulate multiphase diffraction analysis as a set prediction problem, where the model infers an unordered set of phase-resolved components, their mixture proportions, and corresponding structural representations within a unified architecture. A phase-query-driven decomposition mechanism, together with diffraction-consistent physical reconstruction, enables accurate source separation while preserving crystallographic fidelity. Extensive experiments on both simulated and experimental datasets show that XDecomposer substantially improves reconstruction accuracy and phase identification across diverse chemical systems, while maintaining strong generalization to unseen mixtures. These results provide a practical route toward data-driven, source-resolved multiphase XRD analysis and reduce long-standing dependence on prior-guided iteratively phase matching. The code is openly available at https://github.com/Licht0812/XDecomposer

</details>


### [90] [Agentic, Context-Aware Risk Intelligence in the Internet of Value](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05878)
*Basel Magableh,OmniRisk Research*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a risk primitive for the Internet of Value (IoV) composed of five engines (prediction, verification, sentiment fusion, agentic action, and API-risk/scenario) to handle composite marginal risk across heterogeneous, partially-trusted networks, validated through empirical experiments on Solana.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The IoV's dominant marginal risk is composite (route, sentiment, liquidity, policy) rather than a property of any single chain, requiring a new risk primitive adequate for this heterogeneous, partially-trusted regime.

Method: The architecture combines five engines: (1) prediction over price, liquidity, volatility, and route health; (2) Bittensor verification subnet for decentralized scoring; (3) sentiment-fusion over text, on-chain flow, and grey-literature; (4) agentic engine under constitutional role-bound constraints; (5) API-risk/scenario engine for Monte-Carlo action programs. Validated via 27-hour Solana stress-response and 168-hour prediction-router calibration arc.

Result: The case study supports deployability; the validator-loss decomposition is stated formally and is falsifiable. Two empirical artefacts were produced: a stress-response experiment and a calibration arc with explicit class-imbalance honesty.

Conclusion: The proposed five-engine risk primitive is a viable approach for managing composite risk in IoV, with formal falsifiable claims and empirical evidence supporting real-world deployability.

Abstract: The Internet of Value (IoV) is a heterogeneous, partially-trusted network in which the dominant marginal risk is composite (route, sentiment, liquidity, and the policy a system is willing to commit to) rather than a property of any single chain. We argue that a risk primitive adequate for this regime is a composition of five engines: a prediction engine over price, liquidity, volatility, and route health; a Bittensor verification subnet that decentralises and economically scores prediction outputs; a sentiment-fusion engine over text, on-chain flow, and grey-literature feeds; an agentic engine under constitutional, role-bound action constraints; and an API-risk and scenario engine that converts forecasts into pre-committed action programs in the sense of Monte-Carlo scenario generation. We anchor the architecture in two empirical artefacts: a 27-hour policy-constrained liquidity stress-response experiment on Solana, and a 168-hour prediction-router calibration arc reported with explicit class-imbalance honesty. The case study supports deployability; the validator-loss decomposition is stated formally and is falsifiable.

</details>


### [91] [Null Space Constrained Contrastive Visual Forgetting for MLLM Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05909)
*Yuhang Wang,Zhenxing Niu,Haoxuan Ji,Guangyu He,Linlin Zhang,Haichang Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a machine unlearning method for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) that forgets target visual knowledge while preserving non-target visual and all textual knowledge. It uses Contrastive Visual Forgetting (CVF) to separate target/retained visual knowledge, constrains unlearning in the null space of retained knowledge, and extends to continual unlearning scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core challenge in machine unlearning is balancing target knowledge removal with non-target knowledge retention, which is especially difficult in MLLMs due to tightly intertwined visual and textual modalities.

Method: (1) Freezes the LLM backbone and fine-tunes only the visual module. (2) Proposes Contrastive Visual Forgetting (CVF) to separate target visual knowledge from retained visual knowledge in feature space. (3) Identifies the null space of retained knowledge to constrain unlearning and reduce retention degradation. (4) Extends the approach to continual unlearning with sequential forgetting requests.

Result: Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks demonstrate a strong balance between effective forgetting and robust knowledge retention.

Conclusion: The proposed approach achieves effective target visual forgetting while preserving non-target visual and textual knowledge, and generalizes to continual unlearning scenarios.

Abstract: The core challenge of machine unlearning is to strike a balance between target knowledge removal and non-target knowledge retention. In the context of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), this challenge becomes even more pronounced, as knowledge is further divided into visual and textual modalities that are tightly intertwined. In this paper, we introduce an MLLM unlearning approach that aims to forget target visual knowledge while preserving non-target visual knowledge and all textual knowledge. Specifically, we freeze the LLM backbone and achieve unlearning by fine-tuning the visual module. First, we propose a Contrastive Visual Forgetting (CVF) mechanism to separate target visual knowledge from retained visual knowledge, guiding the representations of target visual concepts toward appropriate regions in the feature space. Second, we identify the null space associated with retained knowledge and constrain the unlearning process within this space, thereby significantly mitigating degradation in knowledge retention. Third, beyond static unlearning scenarios, we extend our approach to continual unlearning, where forgetting requests arrive sequentially. Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks demonstrate that our approach achieves a strong balance between effective forgetting and robust knowledge retention.

</details>


### [92] [PREFER: Personalized Review Summarization with Online Preference Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05911)
*Millend Roy,Agostino Capponi,Vineet Goyal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes an online learning framework for personalized product review summaries that iteratively adapts to users' unknown latent preferences through feedback from generated summaries, demonstrating improved preference alignment on Amazon Reviews'23 data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generic static summaries from e-commerce platforms fail to account for heterogeneous and evolving user preferences regarding different product characteristics, overwhelming users with irrelevant information.

Method: An online learning framework that generates personalized summaries by iteratively refining understanding of user preferences through feedback directly from the summaries, addressing unknown latent preference challenges.

Result: Controlled simulations on Amazon Reviews'23 dataset show online preference learning improves alignment with target user interests while maintaining summary quality.

Conclusion: Personalized, adaptive summarization via online learning better aligns with diverse user preferences without sacrificing summary quality, offering a promising direction for e-commerce systems.

Abstract: Product reviews significantly influence purchasing decisions on e-commerce platforms. However, the sheer volume of reviews can overwhelm users, obscuring the information most relevant to their specific needs. Current e-commerce summarization systems typically produce generic, static summaries that fail to account for the fact that (i) different users care about different product characteristics, and (ii) these preferences may evolve with interactions. To address the challenge of unknown latent preferences, we propose an online learning framework that generates personalized summaries for each user. Our system iteratively refines its understanding of user preferences by incorporating feedback directly from the generated summaries over time. We provide a case study using the Amazon Reviews'23 dataset, showing in controlled simulations that online preference learning improves alignment with target user interests while maintaining summary quality.

</details>


### [93] [Wisteria: A Unified Multi-Scale Feature Learning Framework for DNA Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05913)
*Weihua Wang,Haoji Li,Feilong Bao,Lei Yang,Guanglai Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Wisteria is a genomic language model that integrates local motif capture (via gated dilated convolutions) and global dependency refinement (via gated MLPs) within a Mamba-based architecture, further enhanced by a Fourier-based attention mechanism for frequency-domain modeling. It outperforms competitive DNA LMs on benchmarks across short- and long-range dependency settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DNA language models focus on long-range token interactions but neglect the interplay between local motifs and global dependencies, limiting their ability to capture multi-scale regulatory grammar in genomes.

Method: Wisteria augments Mamba with (1) gated dilated convolutions for local motif capture, (2) gated MLPs for global dependency refinement, and (3) a Fourier-based attention mechanism for frequency-domain modeling, periodic extension, and length generalization.

Result: Wisteria achieves strong performance on downstream benchmarks across four settings involving both short- and long-range dependencies, outperforming competitive DNA language model baselines.

Conclusion: Wisteria effectively unifies local and global dependency modeling for multi-scale genomic sequence analysis.

Abstract: DNA language model aims to decipher the regulatory grammar and semantic of genomes by capturing long range dependencies in DNA sequences. Existing methods emphasize long range token interactions but often ignore the interplay between local motifs and global dependencies. In this paper, we propose Wisteria, a genomic language model that integrates multi scale feature learning within a unified framework for DNA sequence. Specifically, Wisteria augments the Mamba based architecture with gated dilated convolutions to capture local motifs and regulatory patterns, while gated multilayer perceptrons refine global dependencies. We further introduce a Fourier based attention mechanism to support frequency domain modeling, periodic extension and length generalization. Across four experimental settings with both short and long range dependencies, Wisteria demonstrates strong performance on downstream benchmarks against competitive DNA language model baselines. These results indicate that Wisteria effectively unifies local and global dependency modeling for multi scale genomic sequence analysis.

</details>


### [94] [Intentmaking and Sensemaking: Human Interaction with AI-Guided Mathematical Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05921)
*Alex Bäuerle,Adam Connors,Alexander Novikov,Adam Zsolt Wagner,Ngân Vũ,Fernanda Viegas,Martin Wattenberg,Lucas Dixon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents findings from a user study with 11 expert mathematicians using AlphaEvolve, an evolutionary coding agent. It identifies and characterizes a workflow called "intentmaking" — the iterative process of discovering, defining, and refining experimental goals through active system interaction — framed as a natural extension of sensemaking. The study suggests users cycle between intentmaking and sensemaking during investigations, advocating for AI tools designed as collaborative instruments rather than black-box question/answer systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The interaction paradigms required to effectively harness AI systems for scientific discovery remain underexplored, motivating the need to study how experts actually use these tools in practice.

Method: A formative user study with 11 expert mathematicians who used AlphaEvolve, an evolutionary coding agent, to solve advanced problems in their fields of expertise.

Result: Identification and characterization of "intentmaking" — the iterative process of discovering, defining, and refining experimental goals through active system interaction. Documentation of a cyclical workflow between intentmaking (defining/updating experiments) and sensemaking (interpreting results) that repeats throughout investigations.

Conclusion: The findings suggest designing AI tools for scientific discovery as collaborative instruments rather than opaque black-box assistants, moving beyond the current question/answer interaction model.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence offers powerful new tools for scientific discovery, but the interaction paradigms required to effectively harness these systems remain underexplored. In this paper, we present findings from a formative user study with 11 expert mathematicians who used AlphaEvolve, an evolutionary coding agent, to tackle advanced problems in their fields of expertise. We identify and characterize a distinct workflow we term intentmaking, the iterative process of discovering, defining, and refining one's experimental goals through active system interaction. We frame this as a natural extension to sensemaking, the cognitive process of building an understanding of complex or novel data. We suggest that users enter a cycle of intentmaking (defining and updating their experiment) and sensemaking (interpreting the results) which repeats many times during the course of an investigation. Our documentation of these themes suggests an approach to designing AI tools for scientific discovery that goes beyond the existing question/answer model of many current systems, treating them as collaborative instruments rather than opaque black-box assistants.

</details>


### [95] [Which Are the Low-Resource Languages of the Semantic Web?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05929)
*Ndeye-Emilie Mbengue,Pierre Monnin,Miguel Couceiro,Fabien Gandon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a multi-level language categorization for Linked Open Data Knowledge Graphs (DBpedia, BabelNet, Wikidata) to formally define low-, medium-, and high-resource languages, aiming to address the digital divide in Open Access Data between high- and low-resource languages and support cross-lingual transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Digital technologies are widening the Open Access Data gap between high- and low-resource languages, excluding communities from digital transformation. No clear quantitative definition of low-resource languages exists in the context of LOD KGs, hindering cross-lingual transfer efforts.

Method: The authors analyze language distribution across LOD KGs (DBpedia, BabelNet, Wikidata) and propose a preliminary multi-level categorization to formally define low-, medium-, and high-resource languages.

Result: A multi-level language categorization based on three major LOD KGs, providing formal definitions for low-, medium-, and high-resource language categories.

Conclusion: This categorization can be leveraged to select cross-lingual transfer candidates, potentially mitigating the OAD divide for low-resource languages.

Abstract: Emerging digital technologies are exacerbating the existing divide in Open Access Data (OAD) between high-and low-resource languages, excluding many communities from the global digital transformation. Multilingual Linked Open Data Knowledge Graphs (LOD KGs) could contribute to mitigating this divide through cross-lingual transfer; however, no clear quantitative definition of low-resource languages has yet been established in the context of LOD KGs. In this poster, we present a methodology to analyze the distribution of languages across LOD KGs and propose a preliminary multi-level categorization based on DBpedia, BabelNet, and Wikidata. This categorization is leveraged to bring a formal definition of low-, high-, and medium-resource languages that could be later leveraged to select cross-lingual transfer candidates.

</details>


### [96] [In Data or Invisible: Toward a Better Digital Representation of Low-Resource Languages with Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05931)
*Ndeye-Emilie Mbengue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This PhD proposal addresses the digital divide in Open Access Data between high- and low-resource languages by analyzing language coverage in Linked Open Data knowledge graphs (DBpedia, BabelNet, Wikidata) and proposes cross-lingual transfer learning and analogical reasoning methods to improve multilingual knowledge graph completion and language coverage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Emerging digital technologies are worsening the Open Access Data divide between high- and low-resource languages, excluding many communities from digital transformation. The paper aims to address this gap in language coverage within Linked Open Data knowledge graphs.

Method: First, identify key variables characterizing language distribution in LOD (e.g., Wikipedia articles per language, language-tagged entities in LOD KGs) and analyze them across DBpedia, BabelNet, and Wikidata. Then, study cross-lingual transfer candidate selection for multilingual KG completion, investigating strategies based on linguistic proximity and curated annotated alignments. Finally, explore analogical reasoning leveraging (dis)similarities to identify cross-lingual correspondences.

Result: Not yet available; this is a PhD proposal describing planned research rather than completed results. The expected outcome is improved multilingual KG completion performance and enhanced language coverage in LOD.

Conclusion: The proposed research aims to mitigate the language divide in Open Access Data by improving language coverage in Linked Open Data knowledge graphs through cross-lingual transfer and analogical reasoning methods.

Abstract: Emerging digital technologies are exacerbating the existing divide in Open Access Data (OAD) between high-and low-resource languages, excluding many communities from participating in the global digital transformation. In this PhD proposal, we aim to address this gap, focusing on the language coverage of Linked Open Data knowledge graphs (LOD KGs). First, we identify key variables that characterize language distribution in LOD, including the number of Wikipedia articles per language edition and the number of language-tagged entities in LOD KGs. These variables are analyzed across three major multilingual LOD KGs, DBpedia, BabelNet, and Wikidata, providing insights into the representation and distribution of languages within LOD. Building on this analysis, we intend to study the impact of cross-lingual transfer candidate selection on the task of multilingual KG completion. In particular, we plan to investigate strategies based on linguistic proximity and the availability of curated annotated alignments between languages. Language proximity also motivates us to explore the benefits of analogical reasoning that relies on (dis)similarities and has not yet been investigated to identify correspondences across languages to improve KG completion performance and enhance language coverage in LOD.

</details>


### [97] [ICU-Bench:Benchmarking Continual Unlearning in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05938)
*Yuhang Wang,Wenjie Mei,Junkai Zhang,Guangyu He,Zhenxing Niu,Haichang Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ICU-Bench is a continual multimodal unlearning benchmark with 1,000 privacy-sensitive profiles, 9,500 images, 16,000 QA pairs, and 100 forget tasks across medical reports and labor contracts, introduced to evaluate continual privacy deletion requests in MLLMs. Experiments show existing methods struggle in continual settings, revealing limitations in forgetting quality, utility preservation, and scalability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MLLM unlearning benchmarks focus on static or short-sequence settings, lacking support for evaluating continual privacy deletion requests in realistic deployments.

Method: ICU-Bench is constructed with 1,000 privacy-sensitive profiles from two document domains (medical reports and labor contracts), comprising 9,500 images, 16,000 QA pairs, and 100 forget tasks. New continual unlearning metrics are introduced for evaluating forgetting effectiveness, historical forgetting preservation, retained utility, and stability.

Result: Existing unlearning methods generally struggle in continual settings, exhibiting clear limitations in balancing forgetting quality, utility preservation, and scalability over long task sequences.

Conclusion: There is a need for multimodal unlearning methods explicitly designed for continual privacy deletion.

Abstract: Although Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved remarkable progress across many domains, their training on large-scale multimodal datasets raises serious privacy concerns, making effective machine unlearning increasingly necessary. However, existing benchmarks mainly focus on static or short-sequence settings, offering limited support for evaluating continual privacy deletion requests in realistic deployments. To bridge this gap, we introduce ICU-Bench, a continual multimodal unlearning benchmark built on privacy-critical document data. ICU-Bench contains 1,000 privacy-sensitive profiles from two document domains, medical reports and labor contracts, with 9,500 images, 16,000 question-answer pairs, and 100 forget tasks. Additionally, new continual unlearning metrics are introduced, facilitating a comprehensive analysis of forgetting effectiveness, historical forgetting preservation, retained utility, and stability throughout the continual unlearning process. Through extensive experiments with representative unlearning methods on ICU-Bench, we show that existing methods generally struggle in continual settings and exhibit clear limitations in balancing forgetting quality, utility preservation, and scalability over long task sequences. These findings highlight the need for multimodal unlearning methods explicitly designed for continual privacy deletion.

</details>


### [98] [MAS-Algorithm: A Workflow for Solving Algorithmic Programming Problems with a Multi-Agent System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05949)
*Yuliang Xu,Xiang Xu,Yao Wan,Hu Wei,Tong Jia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAS-Algorithm is a multi-agent workflow for algorithmic problem solving that decomposes the process into modular stages, achieving 6.48% average acceptance rate gain across Qwen models, significantly outperforming parameter-efficient fine-tuning (0.89% gain), with individual agents contributing up to 27.7% improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches for algorithmic problem solving rely on costly model-centric strategies (architectural modifications, data scaling) with limited interpretability, while alternative methods using external tools or prompting techniques are fragmented and lack a unified framework.

Method: Proposes MAS-Algorithm, a systematic multi-agent workflow inspired by competitive programmers and algorithm engineers, which decomposes end-to-end solving into modular stages with structured reasoning, tool integration, and flexible agent coordination.

Result: 6.48% average acceptance rate improvement across multiple Qwen series models (vs. 0.89% for fine-tuning), 4.72% gain on LiveCodeBench-Pro, with individual agents contributing up to 27.7% improvement in ablation studies.

Conclusion: The multi-agent workflow approach demonstrates strong potential for advancing AI-driven algorithmic reasoning, offering a modular, interpretable, and extensible alternative to model-centric methods.

Abstract: Algorithmic problem solving serves as a rigorous testbed for evaluating structured reasoning in AI coding systems, as it directly reflects a model's ability to perform structured reasoning in complex scenarios.Existing approaches predominantly rely on model-centric strategies, such as architectural modifications and data scaling, which are costly and offer limited interpretability. Alternative methods leveraging external tools or prompting techniques (e.g., chain-of-thought) are often fragmented and lack a unified framework. In this paper, we propose MAS-Algorithm, a systematic multi-agent workflow for algorithmic problem solving inspired by the practices of competitive programmers and algorithm engineers. Our framework decomposes the end-to-end solving process into modular stages, enabling structured reasoning, tool integration, and flexible coordination among agents. The design emphasizes both rigor and extensibility, allowing it to generalize across diverse problem types.Experimental results on a self-constructed benchmark demonstrate consistent improvements across multiple Qwen series models, achieving an average gain of 6.48% in acceptance rate. In contrast, parameter-efficient fine-tuning on the same data yields only a marginal improvement of 0.89%. We further observe a 4.72% gain on LiveCodeBench-Pro, along with consistent improvements across additional accuracy and efficiency metrics.Beyond performance gains, we conduct comprehensive analyses to better understand the reasoning process within the workflow, including error patterns and cross-scenario behaviors. We further perform customized replacement and ablation studies to explore the upper bound of the framework, showing that individual agents can contribute improvements of up to 27.7%. These results highlight the strong potential of MAS-Algorithm for advancing AI-driven algorithmic reasoning.

</details>


### [99] [HaM-World: Soft-Hamiltonian World Models with Selective Memory for Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05951)
*Haoyun Tang,Haodong Cui,Keyao Xu,Kun Wang,Zhandong Mei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HaM-World (HMW) is a structured world model that decomposes latent states into a canonical (q,p) subspace governed by Soft-Hamiltonian dynamics and a context subspace c for semantic factors, using Mamba-based selective state-space memory for history conditioning. It achieves state-of-the-art results on DeepMind Control tasks, reducing long-horizon rollout error and improving OOD robustness significantly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World models suffer from unstable imagined rollouts as planning horizon grows or dynamics distribution shifts, due to missing history-conditioned memory for Markov completeness and geometric organization separating configuration, momentum, and task semantics.

Method: Proposes HaM-World (HMW) which decomposes latent state into (q,p) subspace (energy-derived Hamiltonian vector field plus learnable residual/control dynamics) and context subspace c (semantic, dissipative, non-conservative factors). Uses Mamba selective state-space memory as history-conditioned input. Single latent state shared across dynamics prediction, reward/value estimation, imagined rollouts, and CEM action search.

Result: Highest Avg. AUC (117.9, +9.5%) on 4 DeepMind Control tasks; long-horizon rollout error reduced to 45% of strong baseline; wins 11/12 k in {3,5,7} MSE cells; highest return under all 12 OOD perturbations; average OOD-return gains of 10.2% (Finger Spin) and 13.6% (Reacher Easy).

Conclusion: The structured Soft-Hamiltonian dynamics design with Mamba memory successfully stabilizes long-horizon planning and generalizes robustly to OOD conditions, as evidenced by bounded energy drift, structured energy variation, and coherent control-induced energy transfer.

Abstract: World models enable model-based planning through learned latent dynamics, but imagined rollouts become unstable as the planning horizon grows or the dynamics distribution shifts. We argue that this instability reflects two missing structures in planner-facing latents: history-conditioned memory for approximate Markov completeness, and geometric organization that separates configuration, momentum, and task semantics. We propose HaM-World (HMW), a structured world model that decomposes the latent state into a canonical (q, p) subspace and a context subspace c, while using Mamba selective state-space memory as the history-conditioned input to the same latent dynamics. Within this interface, (q, p) evolves through an energy-derived Hamiltonian vector field plus learnable residual/control dynamics, while c captures semantic, dissipative, and non-conservative factors. This gives the planner a single latent state shared by dynamics prediction, reward/value estimation, imagined rollouts, and CEM action search. On four DeepMind Control Suite tasks, HaM-World reaches the highest Avg. AUC (117.9, +9.5%), reduces long-horizon rollout error to 45% of a strong baseline model, and wins 11/12 k in {3,5,7} MSE cells. Under 12 OOD perturbations spanning dynamics shifts, action delay, and observation masking, HaM-World achieves the highest return in every condition, with average OOD-return gains of 10.2% on Finger Spin and 13.6% on Reacher Easy. Mechanism diagnostics further show bounded action-free Hamiltonian-energy drift, structured energy variation under policy rollouts, and coherent control-induced energy transfer, supporting the intended Soft-Hamiltonian dynamics design.

</details>


### [100] [Temporal Smoothness Doubly Robust Learning for Debiased Knowledge Tracing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05958)
*Peilin Zhan,Wei Chen,Weilin Chen,Shuyi Pan,Ruichu Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes TSDR, a doubly robust framework for Knowledge Tracing that corrects selection bias by combining propensity and imputation models with a temporal smoothness regularizer to reduce variance, achieving unbiased estimation and improved performance across various KT backbones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing knowledge tracing methods neglect selection bias caused by non-random exercise recommendations and student choices, leading to biased mastery estimates and error accumulation in recommendations.

Method: TSDR integrates a doubly robust formulation (propensity model + error imputation model) with a temporal smoothness regularizer that controls estimator variance while preserving unbiasedness guarantees, supported by derived generalization bounds.

Result: Consistent improvements over multiple state-of-the-art KT backbones on real-world benchmarks, validating the effectiveness of bias correction.

Conclusion: Principled bias correction through doubly robust estimation with variance reduction is vital for knowledge tracing, and TSDR provides a theoretically grounded framework that generalizes across KT models.

Abstract: Knowledge Tracing (KT) is fundamental to intelligent education systems, yet relies on educational logs that are selectively observed. The non-random nature of exercise recommendations and student choices inevitably induces severe selection bias. Most existing KT methods neglect this issue, training on observed logs using standard empirical risk, which yields biased mastery estimates and accumulates errors in subsequent recommendations. To address this, we introduce a doubly robust (DR) formulation for KT that integrates a propensity model with an error imputation model, theoretically guaranteeing unbiasedness if either model is accurate. Beyond unbiasedness, in the sequential setting of KT, we identify that the estimator's performance is compromised by variance-dependent stochastic deviations that accumulate over time, thereby causing training instability and limiting performance. To mitigate this, we derive a generalization bound that explicitly characterizes the impact of estimator variance and identifies temporal smoothness as a key factor in controlling it. Building on these theoretical insights, we propose the Temporal Smoothness Doubly Robust (TSDR) framework. TSDR jointly optimizes the KT predictor and the imputation model with a smoothness regularizer, effectively reducing variance while preserving the unbiasedness guarantee of DR. Experiments on multiple real-world benchmarks demonstrate that TSDR consistently enhances various state-of-the-art KT backbones, underscoring the vital role of principled bias correction in KT.

</details>


### [101] [From Coordinate Matching to Structural Alignment: Rethinking Prototype Alignment in Heterogeneous Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05959)
*Xinghao Wu,Jianwei Niu,Guogang Zhu,Xuefeng Liu,Shaojie Tang,Jiayuan Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FedSAF introduces structural alignment of inter-class relationships (rather than coordinate alignment of prototypes) for heterogeneous federated learning, outperforming existing prototype-based HtFL methods by up to 3.52%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing prototype-based HtFL methods use coordinate alignment (MSE/cosine) that forces heterogeneous clients into a shared global feature subspace, which suppresses client-specific learning capacity under model heterogeneity. The authors identify that coordinate alignment couples two distinct objectives: aligning semantic structure (beneficial) and enforcing a shared basis (harmful in HtFL).

Method: FedSAF shifts alignment from absolute prototype coordinates to inter-class relational structure. Instead of matching client representations to global prototypes element-wise, it preserves the relational geometry among class prototypes, decoupling semantic structure alignment from feature subspace enforcement.

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art prototype-based HtFL methods by up to 3.52% on multiple benchmarks.

Conclusion: Structural alignment is consistently superior to coordinate alignment in heterogeneous FL settings, validating the insight that enforcing a shared feature basis is unnecessary and harmful under model heterogeneity.

Abstract: Heterogeneous federated learning (HtFL) aims to enable collaboration among clients that differ in both data distributions and model architectures. Prototype-based methods, which communicate class-level feature centers (prototypes) instead of full model parameters, have recently shown strong potential for HtFL. Existing prototype-based HtFL methods typically reuse the MSE-based or cosine-based alignment mechanism developed for homogeneous FL when aligning client-specific representations with global prototypes. These approaches are essentially coordinate alignment, where representations of clients are forced to match the global prototypes in the embedding space in an element-wise manner. Such alignment implicitly assumes that all clients should map their representations into the feature subspace defined by the global prototypes. This assumption is reasonable in homogeneous FL, where all clients share the same feature extractor. However, it becomes problematic in HtFL, since heterogeneous feature extractors naturally induce client-specific feature subspaces, and forcing all clients to optimize within a single global subspace unnecessarily suppresses their learning capacity. We observe that coordinate alignment implicitly couples two distinct objectives: aligning inter-class semantic structure, which is directly beneficial for classification, and enforcing a shared feature basis, which is unnecessary and even harmful under model heterogeneity. Building on this insight, we design FedSAF, which shifts the alignment objective from absolute coordinates to inter-class relational structure. We demonstrate that structural alignment consistently outperforms coordinate alignment in heterogeneous settings. Experiments on multiple benchmarks show that our structural alignment outperforms state-of-the-art prototype-based HtFL methods by up to 3.52\%.

</details>


### [102] [TheraAgent: Self-Improving Therapeutic Agent for Precise and Comprehensive Treatment Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05963)
*Junkai Li,Yunghwei Lai,Tianyi Zhu,Zheng Long Lee,Weizhi Ma,Yang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TheraAgent replaces one-shot LLM treatment plan generation with an iterative generate-judge-refine pipeline that mirrors human expert reasoning, achieving state-of-the-art results on HealthBench and an 86% win rate against physicians in expert evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLMs rely on one-shot output without explicit verification, producing rough, incomplete, and potentially unsafe treatment plans. Treatment planning is inherently a complex reasoning and refinement task, not a simple generation problem.

Method: TheraAgent is an agentic framework with an iterative generate-judge-refine pipeline, incorporating TheraJudge—a treatment-specific evaluation module—into the inference loop to enforce clinical standards.

Result: State-of-the-art results on HealthBench (Accuracy and Completeness), 86% win rate against physicians in expert evaluations with superior Targeting and Harm Control, and high agreement between TheraJudge and HealthBench evaluations.

Conclusion: Replacing one-shot generation with iterative refinement and integrated verification improves treatment plan quality, and TheraJudge reliably enforces clinical standards within the inference loop.

Abstract: Formulating a treatment plan is inherently a complex reasoning and refinement task rather than a simple generation problem. However, existing large language models (LLMs) mainly rely on one-shot output without explicit verification, which may result in rough, incomplete, and potentially unsafe treatment plans. To address these limitations, we propose TheraAgent, an agentic framework that replaces one-shot generation with an iterative generate-judge-refine pipeline. By mirroring the actual reasoning process of human experts who iteratively revise treatment plans, our framework progressively transforms coarse and incomplete drafts into precise, comprehensive, and safer therapeutic regimens. To facilitate the critical judge component, we introduce TheraJudge, a treatment-specific evaluation module integrated into the inference loop to enforce clinical standards. Experiments show TheraAgent achieves state-of-the-art results on HealthBench, leading in Accuracy and Completeness. In expert evaluations, it attains an 86% win rate against physicians, with superior Targeting and Harm Control. Moreover, the highly agreement between TheraJudge and HealthBench evaluations confirms the reliability of our framework.

</details>


### [103] [BehaviorGuard: Online Backdoor Defense for Deep Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05977)
*Yinbo Yu,Xueyu Yin,Jiadai Wang,Chunwei Tian,Sai Xu,Qi Zhu,Daoqiang Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BehaviorGuard is an online, trigger-agnostic backdoor defense for DRL that detects and mitigates attacks by monitoring behavioral drift in action distributions, outperforming prior methods across single- and multi-agent settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing backdoor defenses for DRL rely on reward anomaly detection and trigger reverse-engineering, which are brittle against complex triggers, and model fine-tuning, which is computationally expensive. The authors aim to develop a more robust and efficient trigger-agnostic defense.

Method: BehaviorGuard detects backdoor attacks by analyzing action distribution shifts (behavioral drift) in high-quantile regions and distribution tails, without needing to know the trigger. It uses a novel metric to identify and suppress anomalous actions at runtime in an online manner.

Result: Across diverse benchmarks and multiple backdoor attacks, BehaviorGuard consistently outperforms prior defense methods in both efficacy (detection/ mitigation accuracy) and efficiency (computational cost). It is the first online defense effective against attacks in both single- and multi-agent DRL.
