Conclusion: BehaviorGuard demonstrates that focusing on backdoor output behaviors (action distribution shifts) rather than trigger patterns enables a more robust and practical online defense for DRL systems, applicable to both single- and multi-agent scenarios.

Abstract: Backdoor attacks pose a serious threat to deep reinforcement learning (DRL). Current defenses typically rely on reward anomalies to reverse-engineer triggers and model finetuning to remove backdoors. However, complex trigger patterns undermine their robustness, and fine-tuning entails high costs, limiting practical utility. Therefore, we shift defense concerns to trigger-agnostic backdoor output behaviors and propose BehaviorGuard, an online behavior-based backdoor detection and mitigation framework for DRL. Specifically, we find that regardless of attacks, backdoored policies induce consistent shifts in action distributions to ensure reliable activation, leaving detectable traces in high-quantile regions and distribution tails, even in the absence of triggers. Based on this, we design a novel metric that captures behavioral drift in action distributions to identify and suppress backdoor actions at runtime. To our knowledge, this is the first online backdoor defense that counters attacks both in single- and multi-agent DRL. Evaluated across diverse benchmarks with different backdoor attacks, BehaviorGuard consistently surpasses prior methods in both efficacy and efficiency.

</details>


### [104] [TACT: Mitigating Overthinking and Overacting in Coding Agents via Activation Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05980)
*Yuan Sui,Yulin Chen,Yibo Li,Xue Jiang,Yufei He,Yihong Dong,Xiaoxin He,Tianyu Gao,Bryan Hooi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TACT detects and mitigates agent drift (overthinking and overacting) in language model agents by steering hidden state activations along discovered drift axes, improving resolve rates by up to +5.8 pp and reducing steps-to-resolve by up to 26% on software engineering benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language model agents degrade over long trajectories (agent drift), specifically suffering from overthinking (repeated reasoning on known information) and overacting (issuing tool calls without integrating recent observations), which limits reliable long-horizon task completion.

Method: TACT identifies two linear drift axes (overthinking and overacting) in the residual stream with ~0.9 AUC separation, then at test time projects step activations onto these axes and pulls drifted ones back toward the calibrated region via activation steering.

Result: TACT outperforms unsteered baselines on SWE-bench Verified, Terminal-Bench 2.0, and CLAW-Eval, achieving +5.8 pp resolve rate on Qwen3.5-27B and +4.8 pp on Gemma-4-26B-A4B-it, with up to 26% reduction in steps-to-resolve.

Conclusion: Agent drift is a steerable direction in the residual stream, and TACT provides a viable mechanism for building more reliable long-horizon agents.

Abstract: When language model agents tackle complex software engineering tasks, they often degrade over long trajectories, which we define as *agent drift*. We focus on two recurring failure modes *overthinking* and *overacting*, i.e., where the agent repeatedly reasons over information it already has, and where it issues tool calls without integrating recent observations or acquiring new evidence. In this paper, we introduce TACT (Think-Act Calibration via activation Steering), to detect and mitigate agent drift in the residual stream before it surfaces as a behavioral failure. In specific, we label trajectory steps as overthinking, overacting, or calibrated, and find that their hidden states can separate linearly along two *drift axes*, pointing from calibrated behavior toward each failure mode (AUC $\approx$ 0.9). To mitigate agent drift, we project each step's activation onto these axes at test time and pull drifted ones back toward the calibrated region. Experiments show that TACT outperforms unsteered baselines across SWE-bench Verified, Terminal-Bench 2.0, and CLAW-Eval, lifting average resolve rate by $+5.8$ pp on Qwen3.5-27B and $+4.8$ pp on Gemma-4-26B-A4B-it while cutting steps-to-resolve by up to $26\%$. These gains frame agent drift as a steerable direction in the residual stream, and position TACT as a viable handle for reliable long-horizon agents.

</details>


### [105] [BioResearcher: Scenario-Guided Multi-Agent for Translational Medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05985)
*Remigiusz Kinas,Joanna Krawczyk,Rafał Powalski,Przemysław Pietrzak,Agnieszka Kowalewska,Krzysztof Kolmus,Maciej Sypetkowski,Łukasz Smoliński,Tomasz Jetka*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Ingenix BioResearcher is a scenario-guided multi-agent system for translational medicine that maps queries to versioned research playbooks, delegates to specialized subagents with 30+ tools, and applies multi-model reconciliation. It achieves strong results across unit-level (83.49% pass rate), open-ended biomedical reasoning (89.33% on BixBench), and end-to-end clinical discovery (74.7% positive hit rate) benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Translational medicine requires evidence synthesis from heterogeneous sources (literature, trials, patents, multi-omics) with provenance and uncertainty, but existing foundation models, tool-augmented, and multi-agent systems fail to support auditable, scenario-specific workflows.

Method: Ingenix BioResearcher uses a scenario-guided multi-agent architecture that: (1) maps queries to versioned research playbooks, (2) delegates tasks to specialized subagents over 30+ tools and ML endpoints, (3) integrates structured database access with sandboxed code for genome-scale analyses, and (4) applies claim-level multi-model reconciliation before editorial assembly.

Result: BioResearcher achieves 83.49% pass rate (0.892 avg score) on 109 single-step tests, 89.33% on BixBench-Verified-50, top 0.758 mean score on BaisBench Scientific Discovery, and 74.7%±3.3% positive hit rate with 96.8%±0.2% negative clear rate on 30-query clinical end-to-end benchmark.

Conclusion: BioResearcher demonstrates broad, competitive performance across unit-level, open-ended biomedical reasoning, and end-to-end clinical discovery evaluations, outperforming baselines in translational medicine evidence synthesis.

Abstract: Translational medicine turns underspecified development goals into evidence synthesis that must combine literature, trials, patents, and quantitative multi-omics analysis while preserving identifiers, uncertainty, and retrievable provenance. General-purpose foundation models and off-the-shelf tool-augmented or multi-agent systems are not built for this: they tend to produce single-shot answers or run open-endedly, and fall short on the auditable, scenario-specific workflows that heterogeneous biomedical sources demand.
  This paper introduces Ingenix BioResearcher, a scenario-guided multi-agent system that maps queries to versioned research playbooks, delegates to specialized subagents over 30+ tools and machine-learning endpoints, mixes structured database access with sandboxed code for genome-scale analyses, and applies claim-level multi-model reconciliation before editorial assembly.
  We evaluate BioResearcher across unit-level capabilities, open-ended biomedical reasoning, and end-to-end clinical discovery. It leads evaluated baselines on 109 single-step tests (83.49% pass rate; 0.892 average score), achieves strong biomedical benchmark performance (89.33% on BixBench-Verified-50 and the top 0.758 mean score on BaisBench Scientific Discovery), and leads on a 30-query clinical end-to-end benchmark with the highest positive hit rate (74.7% $\pm$ 3.3%) and negative clear rate (96.8% $\pm$ 0.2%). These results show broad, competitive performance across unit-level, open-ended, and end-to-end clinical evaluations.

</details>


### [106] [Strat-LLM: Stratified Strategy Alignment for LLM-based Stock Trading with Real-time Multi-Source Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06024)
*Wenliang Huang,Zengyi Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Strat-LLM is a framework for evaluating LLMs as autonomous trading agents using Stratified Strategy Alignment, tested on A-share and U.S. markets in a live-forward 2025 setting. It reveals that reasoning-heavy models excel in Free Mode, standard models need Strict Mode, alignment modes are regime-dependent, mid-scale models (35B) offer best fidelity, ultra-large models (122B) face an alignment tax in rigid modes, and standard LLMs suffer a high win-rate trap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks overlook the interplay between architectural reasoning and strategy consistency in LLM-based trading agents.

Method: Strat-LLM introduces Stratified Strategy Alignment with four modes (Free, Guided, Strict, etc.) applied in a live-forward 2025 setting, integrating sequential prices, real-time news, and annual reports to eliminate look-ahead bias.

Result: (1) Reasoning-heavy models peak in Free Mode; standard models need Strict Mode. (2) Alignment utility is regime-dependent: Free/Guided capture momentum in uptrends, Strict mitigates drawdowns in downtrends. (3) Mid-scale models (35B) show optimal fidelity under strict constraints; ultra-large models (122B) suffer alignment tax under rigid rules but perform better in Guided Mode. (4) Standard LLMs fall into a high win-rate trap, optimized via deep reasoning or strict guardrails.

Conclusion: Strat-LLM provides a systematic framework to evaluate and align LLM trading agents, revealing critical trade-offs between reasoning depth, model scale, and regime-dependent strategy consistency for trustworthy deployment.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are evolving into autonomous trading agents, yet existing benchmarks often overlook the interplay between architectural reasoning and strategy consistency. We propose Strat-LLM, a framework grounded in Stratified Strategy Alignment. Operating in a live-forward setting throughout 2025, it integrates heterogeneous data including sequential prices, real-time news, and annual reports to eliminate look-ahead bias. Extensive stress tests on A-share and U.S. markets reveal: (1) reasoning-heavy models achieve peak utility in Free Mode via internal logic, whereas standard models require Strict Mode as a vital risk anchor; (2) alignment utility is regime-dependent, with Free and Guided modes capturing momentum in uptrending markets, while Strict Mode mitigates drawdowns in downtrends; (3) mid-scale models (35B) show optimal fidelity under strict constraints, whereas ultra-large models (122B) suffer an alignment tax under rigid rules but gain a performance premium in Guided Mode; (4) standard LLMs often fall into a high win-rate trap, optimizing for small gains at the expense of total returns, which can only be mitigated through deep reasoning or strict external guardrails. Project details are available at https://Strat-LLM.github.io.

</details>


### [107] [Pathways to AGI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06029)
*Gordon Fletcher,Saomai Vu Khan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper critically examines the development pathways of generative AI from a software studies perspective, questioning assumptions about inevitability. It explores five key questions about historical pathways, leverage points, trajectory differences across model types, alternative projects, and plausible socio-technical programs toward AGI-adjacent capabilities that prioritize transparency, wellbeing, and sustainability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to avoid assumptions about the inevitability of current AI development trajectories and instead critically examine how social, political, and economic circumstances have shaped commercial AI. The authors aim to surface alternative possibilities that were lost and question the conceptual validity of AGI.

Method: The paper adopts a critical software studies perspective to analyze five structured questions: (1) critical pathways leading to current generative AI; (2) leverage nodes and dead ends; (3) differences across proprietary, open-weight, and sovereign model trajectories; (4) status of alternative projects branching from key leverage points; (5) plausible socio-technical programs toward AGI-adjacent capabilities under constraints of transparency, wellbeing, and sustainability.

Result: The analysis identifies specific critical pathways, decision points acting as leverage nodes, and divergent trajectories across frontier proprietary, open-weight, and domain-specific/sovereign models. It documents alternative projects that branched from key junctures, assessing their success, stalling, failure, or absorption.

Conclusion: The paper concludes with recommendations for socio-technical development programs that could move toward AGI-adjacent capability while meeting requirements for transparency, moderation, wellbeing, and sustainable business models, emphasizing that current AI trajectories are contingent rather than inevitable.

Abstract: Our focus are five related questions that stem from a critical software studies perspective. Underpinning this view is the acknowledged need to avoid assumptions regarding the inevitability of the current situation relating to AI. What we need to see is the closeness of the linkage between current commercial AI development and our prevailing social, political and economic circumstances. This does mean that the perspectives presented here are done so critically and conditionally. Most importantly, Artificial General Intelligence (AGI) is seen as being problematic both conceptually and definitionally. This conditioning of any view regarding AGI does lead the discussion in specific directions and to certain conclusions regarding the future. However, adopting this perspective enables the work to offer some final recommendations. We set out to ask the following questions, 1. What are the critical pathways that produced the current dominant generative AI tools (capabilities, product forms, adoption patterns)? 2. Which decision points acted as leverage nodes (small changes that had large downstream effects), and which dead ends reveal alternative possibilities that did not become dominant? 3. How do pathways differ across three foundational-model trajectories such as the frontier proprietary models, open-weight models or specific domain and sovereign models? 4. Which alternative projects branched from key leverage nodes, what is their current state, and why did some succeed, stall, fail or become absorbed? 5. Based on this analysis, what socio-technical development programmes could plausibly move toward AGI-adjacent capability while meeting requirements for transparency, moderation, wellbeing and sustainable business models?

</details>


### [108] [Novelty-based Tree-of-Thought Search for LLM Reasoning and Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06040)
*Leon Hamm,Zlatan Ajanovic*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a novelty-based pruning method for tree-of-thought reasoning in LLMs, inspired by width-based search in planning. Novelty measures uniqueness of new thoughts compared to previously seen nodes, reducing tree size and token costs while maintaining or improving reasoning performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM reasoning methods (CoT, ToT, RL) are brittle, have not achieved human-level performance, and suffer from high time and token costs. The authors aim to improve tree-of-thought reasoning by transferring the concept of novelty from width-based search planning to language domains.

Method: The authors propose a measurable concept of novelty that describes uniqueness of a new node compared to previously seen nodes in the search tree. Novelty is estimated by prompting an LLM and leveraging its embedded general knowledge from pre-training. This metric is used to prune branches and reduce search scope, decreasing overall tree size despite more prompts per state.

Result: The method was tested on several benchmarks in language-based planning and general reasoning. The novelty-based pruning introduces more prompts per state but reduces overall token cost through pruning and reduced tree size.

Conclusion: Novelty estimation from LLM pre-trained knowledge can effectively prune tree-of-thought search, reducing token costs while maintaining reasoning quality across language-based planning and reasoning benchmarks.

Abstract: Although advances such as chain-of-thought, tree-of-thought or reinforcement learning have improved the performance of LLMs in reasoning and planning tasks, they are still brittle and have not achieved human-level performance in many domains, and often suffer from high time and token costs. Inspired by the success of width-based search in planning, we explore how the concept of novelty can be transferred to language domains and how it can improve tree-of-thought reasoning. A tree of thoughts relies on building possible "paths" of consecutive ideas or thoughts. These are generated by repeatedly prompting an LLM. In our paper, a measurable concept of novelty is proposed that describes the uniqueness of a new node (thought) in comparison to nodes previously seen in the search tree. Novelty is estimated by prompting an LLM and making use of embedded general knowledge from pre-training. This metric can then be used to prune branches and reduce the scope of the search. Although this method introduces more prompts per state, the overall token cost can be reduced by pruning and reducing the overall tree size. This procedure is tested and compared using several benchmarks in language-based planning and general reasoning.

</details>


### [109] [Visual Fingerprints for LLM Generation Comparison](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06054)
*Amal Alnouri,Andreas Hinterreiter,Christina Humer,Furui Cheng,Marc Streit*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "visual fingerprints" — distribution-level visualizations of linguistic choices (content, expression, structure) extracted from LLM outputs — to compare how different generation conditions (prompts, instructions, parameters) shape model behavior, revealing patterns not observable through individual responses or aggregate metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how different generation conditions (prompts, system instructions, model parameters, architecture) shape LLM behaviors is essential for prompt design and model evaluation, but remains challenging due to the stochastic and open-ended nature of text generation.

Method: The approach models LLM responses as collections of linguistic choices (content, expression, structure), extracts these choices via NLP pipelines, represents their distributions across repeated samples, and visualizes them as "visual fingerprints" for distribution-level comparison of condition-specific tendencies.

Result: Through four usage scenarios, visual fingerprints reveal consistent patterns in LLM behavior that are difficult to observe through individual responses or aggregate metrics.

Conclusion: Visual fingerprints enable direct, distribution-level comparison of how different generation conditions bias LLM outputs, providing a novel tool for analyzing and comparing model behaviors across conditions.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) outputs arise from complex interactions among prompts, system instructions, model parameters, and architecture. We refer to specific configurations of these factors as generation conditions, each of which can bias outputs in various ways. Understanding how different generation conditions shape model behaviors is essential for tasks such as prompt design and model evaluation, yet it remains challenging due to the stochastic and open-ended nature of text generation. We present an approach to visually compare LLM outputs across generation conditions by modeling responses as collections of linguistic choices, including content, expression, and structure. We extract these choices using natural language processing pipelines and represent their distributions across repeated samples. We then visualize these distributions as visual fingerprints, enabling direct, distribution-level comparison of condition-specific tendencies. Through four usage scenarios, we demonstrate how visual fingerprints reveal consistent patterns in LLM behavior that are difficult to observe through individual responses or aggregate metrics.

</details>


### [110] [VibeServe: Can AI Agents Build Bespoke LLM Serving Systems?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06068)
*Keisuke Kamahori,Shihang Li,Simon Peter,Baris Kasikci*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VibeServe is an agentic system that automatically synthesizes specialized LLM serving stacks for different usage scenarios, outperforming generic systems like vLLM in non-standard scenarios while remaining competitive in standard deployments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM serving systems use a single general-purpose stack hand-tuned for all models and workloads, which misses optimization opportunities in non-standard scenarios. The authors propose reversing this approach by specializing serving systems at generation time rather than relying on runtime generality.

Method: VibeServe uses a multi-agent loop with an outer loop for planning and searching over system designs, and an inner loop for implementing candidates, checking correctness, and measuring performance on target benchmarks. It automatically synthesizes entire LLM serving stacks end-to-end.

Result: In standard deployment, VibeServe is competitive with vLLM. In non-standard scenarios (involving novel model architectures, workload knowledge, or hardware-specific optimizations), VibeServe outperforms existing systems by exploiting opportunities that generic systems miss.

Conclusion: Generation-time specialization is a promising alternative to runtime generality for infrastructure software design, suggesting a different point in the design space for building serving systems.

Abstract: For years, we have built LLM serving systems like any other critical infrastructure: a single general-purpose stack, hand-tuned over many engineer-years, meant to support every model and workload. In this paper, we take the opposite bet: a multi-agent loop that automatically synthesizes bespoke serving systems for different usage scenarios. We propose VibeServe, the first agentic loop that generates entire LLM serving stacks end-to-end. VibeServe uses an outer loop to plan and track the search over system designs, and an inner loop to implement candidates, check correctness, and measure performance on the target benchmark. In the standard deployment setting, where existing stacks are highly optimized, VibeServe remains competitive with vLLM, showing that generation-time specialization need not come at the cost of performance. More interestingly, in non-standard scenarios, VibeServe outperforms existing systems by exploiting opportunities that generic systems miss in six scenarios involving non-standard model architectures, workload knowledge, and hardware-specific optimizations. Together, these results suggest a different point in the design space for infrastructure software: generation-time specialization rather than runtime generality. Code is available at https://github.com/uw-syfi/vibe-serve.

</details>


### [111] [Safety Certification is Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06087)
*Oliver Schön,Licio Romao,Sadegh Soudjani*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a kernel embedding framework for certifying safety of dynamical systems under uncertainty. Instead of recursively computing safety probabilities via dynamic programming (DP), it treats safety certification as a classification problem on trajectory data to directly estimate T-step safety probability, which avoids compounding errors and works for non-Markovian systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DP-based safety certification methods suffer from compounding errors across time horizons, leading to vacuous lower bounds for large T. Additionally, they require Markovian dynamics, limiting applicability.

Method: A kernel embedding framework that reformulates safety certification as a classification problem on trajectory data, enabling direct estimation of T-step safety probability without recursive dynamic programming.

Result: The framework subsumes existing approaches (barrier certificates, robust Markov models) as special cases. Direct estimators remain stable regardless of horizon length and in non-Markovian settings, while DP-based certificates silently produce unsound results. Validated on a neural-controlled quadrotor simulation.

Conclusion: The proposed kernel embedding approach bypasses compounding errors inherent in DP-based methods, enabling reliable safety certification for longer horizons and non-Markovian dynamics where traditional methods fail.

Abstract: The goal of this paper is certifying safety of dynamical systems subject to uncertainty. Existing approaches use trajectory data to estimate transition probabilities, and compute safety probabilities recursively via dynamic programming (DP). This recursion may lead to compounding errors in the certified safety probability, thus collapsing to a vacuous lower bound for growing horizons $T$. We propose a kernel embedding framework that treats safety certification as a classification problem on trajectory data, directly estimating the $T$-step safety probability without recursion. We show that the framework subsumes well-established approaches from the literature (e.g., barrier certificates, robust Markov models) as special cases, and allows us to go beyond their limitations. As the main consequence, it bypasses compounding error across the horizon and enables certification for systems with non-Markovian dynamics. We demonstrate that direct estimators remain stable independent of the certification horizon and in the non-Markovian setting, whilst DP-based certificates silently go unsound -- confirmed in simulation on a neural-controlled quadrotor.

</details>


### [112] [Shallow Prefill, Deep Decoding: Efficient Long-Context Inference via Layer-Asymmetric KV Visibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06105)
*Jungsuk Oh,Hyeseo Jeon,Hyunjune Ji,Kyongmin Kong,Jay-Yoon Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SPEED introduces a phase-asymmetric KV-visibility policy where prefill tokens only maintain KV states in lower layers, while decode tokens retain full-depth access. This achieves 33% TTFT reduction, 22% TPOT improvement, and 25% KV memory savings at 128K context with minimal quality loss (51.2 vs 51.4 average score).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context inference in decoder-only LMs is expensive due to full-depth KV caching for prompt tokens during both prefill and decode phases, leading to high memory and latency costs.

Method: Phase-asymmetric KV-visibility policy: non-anchor prompt-token KV states are materialized only in lower layers during prefill, while decode-phase tokens maintain full-depth access. Only a minimal Beginning-of-Sequence (BoS) anchor token retains full-depth KV visibility.

Result: With Llama-3.1-8B at 128K context: 33% TTFT reduction, 22% TPOT improvement, 25% active KV memory reduction, while average OLMES score drops only from 51.4 to 51.2.

Conclusion: Long-context prompt tokens do not need to persist as full-depth KV-cache objects when decode-phase tokens remain full-depth. The lower layers capture essential prompt-selection and representation-stabilization functions, making upper-layer prompt KV states unnecessary for decode.

Abstract: Long-context inference in decoder-only language models is costly because long prompts are processed during Prefill, cached at every layer, and repeatedly attended to during autoregressive Decode. We introduce \emph{Shallow Prefill, dEEp Decode} (SPEED), a phase-asymmetric KV-visibility policy that materializes non-anchor prompt-token KV states only in lower layers while keeping Decode-phase tokens full-depth. Unlike previous approaches that make upper-layer prompt KV states cheaper to store or construct, SPEED removes prefill tokens from the upper-layer Decode visibility set altogether. With a minimal BoS anchor, this simple change preserves broad benchmark quality while reducing long-context cost. In a controlled Llama-3.1-8B instruction-tuning study, SPEED using only 75\% of layers for prefill tokens reaches 51.2 average score on OLMES-style benchmarks, compared with 51.4 for the full-depth baseline, while improving TTFT by 33\%, TPOT by 22\%, and reducing active KV memory by 25.0\% at 128K context. Layer-wise diagnostics suggest that this cutoff retains the main prompt-selection and representation-stabilization regions of the full-depth model. These results show that long-context prompt tokens need not always persist as full-depth KV-cache objects when Decode-phase tokens remain full-depth.

</details>


### [113] [On Time, Within Budget: Constraint-Driven Online Resource Allocation for Agentic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06110)
*Xinglin Wang,Zishen Liu,Shaoxiong Feng,Peiwen Yuan,Yiwei Li,Jiayi Shi,Yueqi Zhang,Chuyi Tan,Ji Zhang,Boyuan Pan,Yao Hu,Kan Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies constraint-driven online resource allocation for agentic workflows, proposing Monte Carlo Portfolio Planning (MCPP), a lightweight closed-loop planner that maximizes the probability of completing workflows under explicit budget and deadline constraints. Experiments show MCPP consistently outperforms baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real deployments of agentic systems often impose concrete budget and deadline constraints, shifting the goal from average efficiency optimization to maximizing the probability of completing the entire workflow under these explicit constraints.

Method: The authors formulate the setting as a finite-horizon stochastic online allocation problem and propose Monte Carlo Portfolio Planning (MCPP), a closed-loop planner that estimates constrained completion probability via simulated workflow executions and replans after observed outcomes.

Result: Experiments on CodeFlow and ProofFlow demonstrate that MCPP consistently improves constrained completion probability over strong baselines across a wide range of budget-deadline constraints.

Conclusion: MCPP effectively addresses the real-world requirement of completing agentic workflows under explicit budget and deadline constraints by using lightweight Monte Carlo simulation and replanning.

Abstract: Agentic systems increasingly solve complex user requests by executing orchestrated workflows, where subtasks are assigned to specialized models or tools and coordinated according to their dependencies. While recent work improves agent efficiency by optimizing the performance--cost--latency frontier, real deployments often impose concrete requirements: a workflow must be completed within a specified budget and before a specified deadline. This shifts the goal from average efficiency optimization to maximizing the probability that the entire workflow completes successfully under explicit budget and deadline constraints. We study \emph{constraint-driven online resource allocation for agentic workflows}. Given a dependency-structured workflow and estimates of success rates and generation lengths for each subtask--model pair, the executor allocates models and parallel samples across simultaneously executable subtasks while managing the remaining budget and time. We formulate this setting as a finite-horizon stochastic online allocation problem and propose \emph{Monte Carlo Portfolio Planning} (MCPP), a lightweight closed-loop planner that directly estimates constrained completion probability through simulated workflow executions and replans after observed outcomes. Experiments on CodeFlow and ProofFlow demonstrate that MCPP consistently improves constrained completion probability over strong baselines across a wide range of budget--deadline constraints.

</details>


### [114] [CrossCult-KIBench: A Benchmark for Cross-Cultural Knowledge Insertion in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06115)
*Zhen Zeng,Leijiang Gu,Feng Li,Jing Yu,Zenglin Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the task of cross-cultural knowledge insertion for MLLMs, presents the CrossCult-KIBench benchmark (9,800 image-grounded cases across English, Chinese, Arabic), proposes Memory-Conditioned Knowledge Insertion (MCKI) as a baseline, and shows that current methods struggle to balance cultural adaptation with behavioral preservation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLLMs trained on English-centric data often produce culturally inappropriate responses in cross-cultural settings, highlighting the need for methods that can adapt models to specific cultural contexts without disrupting their behavior in non-target cultures.

Method: The authors propose Memory-Conditioned Knowledge Insertion (MCKI), which retrieves relevant cultural knowledge from an external memory using frozen MLLM representations and prepends matched entries as conditional prompts. They also introduce CrossCult-KIBench, a benchmark with 9,800 cases across 49 visual scenarios in English, Chinese, and Arabic, supporting single-insert and sequential-insert evaluation settings.

Result: Experiments on CrossCult-KIBench show that existing approaches fail to effectively balance cultural adaptation with behavioral preservation, underscoring a significant challenge in developing culturally-aware MLLMs.

Conclusion: The work highlights the importance of cross-cultural knowledge insertion as a research direction and demonstrates the need for methods that can achieve both effective cultural adaptation and minimal side effects on non-target cultures.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), trained primarily on English-centric data, frequently generate culturally inappropriate or misaligned responses in cross-cultural settings. To mitigate this, we introduce the task of cross-cultural knowledge insertion, which focuses on adapting models to specific cultural contexts while preserving their original behavior in other cultures. To facilitate research in this area, we introduce CrossCult-KIBench, a comprehensive evaluation benchmark for assessing both the effectiveness of knowledge insertion and its unintended side effects on non-target cultures. The benchmark includes 9,800 image-grounded cases covering 49 culturally relevant visual scenarios across English, Chinese, and Arabic language-culture groups. It supports evaluation in both single-insert and sequential-insert settings. We also propose Memory-Conditioned Knowledge Insertion (MCKI) as a baseline method. MCKI retrieves relevant cultural knowledge from an external memory using frozen MLLM representations, prepending matched entries as conditional prompts when applicable. Extensive experiments on CrossCult-KIBench reveal that current approaches struggle to balance effective cultural adaptation with behavioral preservation, highlighting a key challenge in developing culturally-aware MLLMs. Our work thus underscores an important research direction for developing more culturally adaptive and responsible MLLMs.

</details>


### [115] [Back to the Beginning of Heuristic Design: Bridging Code and Knowledge with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06123)
*Nguyen Viet Tuan Kiet,Bui Dinh Pham,Dao Van Tung,Tran Cong Dao,Huynh Thi Thanh Binh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a top-down, knowledge-first paradigm for automatic heuristic design (AHD) in combinatorial optimization, contrasting with existing bottom-up code-centric approaches. It formalizes a distortion-compression trade-off, instantiates knowledge-first search in population-based and tree-based frameworks, and demonstrates improved efficiency, transfer, and generalization across CO tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AHD methods follow a bottom-up paradigm that searches over executable programs and extracts insights from execution feedback, moving from low-level implementations to high-level principles. The authors argue this view is incomplete and introduce a complementary top-down perspective where knowledge is the primary search object and code merely instantiates it, making learned knowledge explicit and reusable across problems and trajectories.

Method: The paper formalizes the top-down knowledge-first shift through a statistical-learning view exposing a distortion-compression trade-off. The approach is instantiated in both population-based and tree-based AHD frameworks, where interpretable hypotheses are iteratively constructed and evolved.

Result: Knowledge-first search improves discovery efficiency, transferability, and generalization compared to code-centric pipelines. Combining both top-down knowledge-first and bottom-up code-centric strategies yields further performance gains across CO and non-CO tasks.

Conclusion: Progress in automatic heuristic design depends on iteratively constructing and evolving interpretable hypotheses that retain value beyond a single search trajectory, suggesting that knowledge should be the primary search object rather than code.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have recently advanced automatic heuristic design (AHD) for combinatorial optimization (CO), where candidate heuristics are iteratively proposed, evaluated, and refined. Most existing approaches search over executable programs and distill insights from execution feedback to guide later iterations. Because this process moves from low-level implementations to high-level principles, we refer to it as a bottom-up paradigm. We argue that this view is incomplete and introduce a complementary top-down perspective: knowledge becomes the primary search object and code merely instantiates and tests it, making what is learned explicit and reusable across problems and trajectories. We formalize this shift through a statistical-learning view that exposes a distortion--compression trade-off, and instantiate it in both population-based and tree-based AHD frameworks. Across CO and tasks beyond it, knowledge-first search improves discovery efficiency, transfer, and generalization, often outperforming code-centric pipelines, while combining both strategies yields further gains. Our results suggest that progress in AHD depends on iteratively constructing and evolving interpretable hypotheses that retain value beyond a single search trajectory.

</details>


### [116] [P-Guide: Parameter-Efficient Prior Steering for Single-Pass CFG Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06124)
*Xin Peng,Ang Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: P-Guide reduces CFG's computational overhead in flow matching by achieving single-pass guidance through initial latent modulation, cutting inference latency by ~50% while maintaining quality comparable to dual-pass CFG.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG) in flow matching requires dual forward passes per sampling step, creating a significant computational bottleneck. The paper aims to eliminate this overhead while preserving guidance quality.

Method: P-Guide modulates only the initial latent state to achieve guidance in a single inference pass. It is shown to be equivalent to CFG under a first-order approximation without requiring explicit velocity field extrapolation. The framework supports both homoscedastic and heteroscedastic priors, jointly modeling mean and variance for adaptive loss attenuation.

Result: P-Guide reduces inference latency by approximately 50% while maintaining fidelity and prompt alignment competitive with standard dual-pass CFG baselines.

Conclusion: Single-pass guidance via initial latent modulation is a viable alternative to dual-pass CFG, offering substantial computational savings. Heteroscedastic priors with joint mean-variance modeling improve robustness to data uncertainty.

Abstract: Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG) is essential for high-fidelity conditional generation in flow matching, yet it imposes significant computational overhead by requiring dual forward passes at each sampling step. In this work, we address this bottleneck by introducing \textbf{P-Guide}, a framework that achieves high-quality guidance through a single inference pass by modulating only the initial latent state. We further show that, under a first-order approximation, P-Guide is equivalent to CFG in the sense that it steers generation from the prior space, without requiring explicit velocity field extrapolation during sampling. We consider both homoscedastic and \textbf{heteroscedastic} priors, and find that jointly modeling the mean and variance enables adaptive loss attenuation and improved robustness to data uncertainty. Extensive experiments demonstrate that P-Guide reduces inference latency by approximately 50\% while maintaining fidelity and prompt alignment competitive with standard dual-pass CFG baselines.

</details>


### [117] [Skill1: Unified Evolution of Skill-Augmented Agents via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06130)
*Yaorui Shi,Yuxin Chen,Zhengxi Lu,Yuchun Miao,Shugui Liu,Qi GU,Xunliang Cai,Xiang Wang,An Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Skill1 is a framework that uses a single policy to co-evolve skill selection, utilization, and distillation from a unified task-outcome reward signal, outperforming prior methods on ALFWorld and WebShop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods optimize skill selection, utilization, and distillation in isolation or with separate reward sources, resulting in partial and conflicting evolution of these coupled capabilities.

Method: A single policy generates a query to search the skill library, re-ranks candidates, solves the task conditioned on the selected skill, and distills a new skill from the trajectory. Learning uses one task-outcome signal where low-frequency trends credit selection and high-frequency variations credit distillation.

Result: Skill1 outperforms prior skill-based and reinforcement learning baselines on ALFWorld and WebShop. Training dynamics confirm co-evolution of the three capabilities, and ablations show removing any credit signal degrades evolution.

Conclusion: A unified reward decomposition approach enables effective co-evolution of all three skill management capabilities, producing superior performance compared to isolated optimization.

Abstract: A persistent skill library allows language model agents to reuse successful strategies across tasks. Maintaining such a library requires three coupled capabilities. The agent selects a relevant skill, utilizes it during execution, and distills new skills from experience. Existing methods optimize these capabilities in isolation or with separate reward sources, resulting in partial and conflicting evolution. We propose Skill1, a framework that trains a single policy to co-evolve skill selection, utilization, and distillation toward a shared task-outcome objective. The policy generates a query to search the skill library, re-ranks candidates to select one, solves the task conditioned on it, and distills a new skill from the trajectory. All learning derives from a single task-outcome signal. Its low-frequency trend credits selection and its high-frequency variation credits distillation. Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop show that Skill1 outperforms prior skill-based and reinforcement learning baselines. Training dynamics confirm the co-evolution of the three capabilities, and ablations show that removing any credit signal degrades the evolution.

</details>


### [118] [Graphlets as Building Blocks for Structural Vocabulary in Knowledge Graph Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06154)
*Kossi Amouzouvi,Robert Wardenga,Jens Lehmann,Sahar Vahdati*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes using graphlets (small connected graphs) as structural tokens for Knowledge Graph Foundation Models (KGFMs), enabling zero-shot transfer across heterogeneous KGs by mining relational patterns via closed/open 2- and 3-path and star graphlets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing KGFMs struggle to transfer representations across unseen KGs because KGs lack a universal token set and fixed grid geometry, unlike language or vision. The paper aims to close this transferability gap by identifying structural invariances that recur across different KGs.

Method: A model-agnostic framework that treats graphlets (closed/open 2- and 3-path, star) as structural tokens, mining a KG between relations via pattern matching to obtain robust structural invariances for representation learning.

Result: Evaluated on 51 KGs across diverse domains for zero-shot inductive and transductive link prediction; adding simple graphlets to the vocabulary yields models that outperform prior KGFMs.

Conclusion: Graphlets serve as effective structural tokens that enable transferable representations across heterogeneous KGs without requiring a universal token set, outperforming existing KGFM approaches.

Abstract: Foundation models excel at language, where sentences become tokens, and vision, where images become pixels, because both reduce to discrete symbols on a shared, fixed grid. Knowledge Graphs share the discreteness, but not the geometry. Their entities and relations are discrete symbols, yet their arrangement is relational and lacks a common, fixed grid. Knowledge Graphs (KGs) share the discreteness, but not the geometry. They form irregular, non-Euclidean topologies whose local neighborhoods differ from graph to graph. Therefore, Knowledge Graph Foundation Models (KGFMs) rely on identifying structural invariances to produce transferable representations. Without a universal token set, KGFMs are limited in their ability to transfer representations across unseen KGs. We close this gap by treating graphlets, small connected graphs, as structural tokens that recur in heterogeneous KGs. In this paper, We introduce a model-agnostic framework based on a vocabulary of graphlets that mines a KG between relations via pattern matching. In particular, we considered closed and open 2- and 3-path, and star graphlets, to obtain robust invariances. The framework is evaluated on 51 KGs from a wide range of domains, for zero-shot inductive and transductive link prediction. Experiments show that adding simple graphlets to the vocabulary yields models that outperform prior KGFMs.

</details>


### [119] [Beyond Accuracy: Policy Invariance as a Reliability Test for LLM Safety Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06161)
*Shihao Weng,Yang Feng,Xiaofei Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-as-a-Judge pipelines lack policy invariance: their verdicts conflate agent behavior with evaluation wording. Content-preserving rewrites flip up to 9.1% of verdicts, and 18-43% of flips occur on unambiguous cases. The paper proposes the Policy Invariance Score and Judge Card protocol to expose this unreliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks treat LLM judge verdicts as ground-truth proxies without verifying whether they depend on agent behavior or merely on how the evaluation policy is worded, making safety scores potentially unreliable.

Method: The authors define policy invariance with three principles: rubric-semantics invariance (under certified-equivalent rewrites), rubric-threshold invariance (under strict-to-lenient shifts), and ambiguity-aware calibration. They instantiate these as a stress-test protocol with four agent-class judges on trajectories from ASSEBench and R-Judge.

Result: Content-preserving policy rewrites flip up to 9.1% of verdicts above baseline jitter; 18-43% of observed flips occur on unambiguous cases. Judges respond to meaningful normative shifts and meaningless structural rewrites with comparable strength and cannot distinguish them.

Conclusion: Existing safety scores conflate agent behavior with evaluator prompting. The proposed Policy Invariance Score and Judge Card protocol expose an order-of-magnitude spread in judge reliability invisible to accuracy-only leaderboards. The protocol is released for auditing future evaluators.

Abstract: LLM-as-a-Judge pipelines have become the de facto evaluator for agent safety, yet existing benchmarks treat their verdicts as ground-truth proxies without checking whether the verdicts depend on the agent's behavior or merely on how the evaluation policy happens to be worded. We argue that any trustworthy safety judge must satisfy a basic property we call policy invariance, and we operationalize it as three testable principles: rubric-semantics invariance under certified-equivalent rewrites, rubric-threshold invariance under intentional strict-to-lenient shifts, and ambiguity-aware calibration so that verdict instability concentrates on genuinely ambiguous cases. Instantiating these principles as a stress-test protocol with four agent-class judges on trajectories drawn from ASSEBench and R-Judge, we surface a previously unmeasured failure mode: today's judges respond to meaningful normative shifts and to meaningless structural rewrites with comparable strength, and cannot tell the two apart. Content-preserving policy rewrites flip up to 9.1% of verdicts above baseline jitter, and 18-43% of all observed flips occur on unambiguous cases under such rewrites, so existing safety scores conflate what the agent did with how the evaluator was prompted. Beyond the diagnosis, we contribute the Policy Invariance Score and the Judge Card reporting protocol, which expose an order-of-magnitude spread in judge reliability that is invisible to accuracy-only leaderboards. We release the protocol and code so that future agent-safety benchmarks can audit their own evaluators rather than trust them by default.

</details>


### [120] [BioMedArena: An Open-source Toolkit for Building and Evaluating Biomedical Deep Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06177)
*Jinge Wu,Hongjian Zhou,Mingde Zeng,Jiayuan Zhu,Junde Wu,Jiazhen Pan,Sean Wu,Honghan Wu,Fenglin Liu,David A. Clifton*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BioMedArena is an open-source toolkit that decouples biomedical agent evaluation into six layers, providing 147 benchmarks and 75 tools across 9 families, enabling fair comparison of foundation models as deep-research agents while eliminating the per-paper engineering tax. It achieves SOTA on 8 benchmarks with +15.03 average improvement over prior SOTA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep research agent evaluation suffers from a "per-paper engineering tax" — inconsistent harnesses, tool registries, and model-specific integration effort make fair comparison across papers and models extremely costly and unreliable.

Method: Decouples evaluation into six layers (benchmark loading, tool exposure, tool selection, execution mode, context management, scoring); provides 147 benchmarks, 75 tools (9 families), 6 agent harnesses with 6 context-management strategies, and a few-line provider adapter pattern for integrating new models/benchmarks/tools.

Result: Achieves SOTA results on 8 representative biomedical benchmarks with an average improvement of +15.03 percentage points over prior SOTA; 12 backbones demonstrate competitive deep-research capabilities.

Conclusion: BioMedArena effectively eliminates the per-paper engineering tax and provides a standardized, extensible arena for fair comparison of foundation models as biomedical deep-research agents.

Abstract: Building a deep research agent today is an exercise in glue code: the same backbone evaluated on the same benchmark can report different accuracies in different papers because harness and tool registry all differ, and integrating a new foundation model into a comparable evaluation surface costs weeks of model-specific engineering. We call this the per-paper engineering tax and release BioMedArena, an open-source toolkit that not only alleviates it but also provides an arena for fair comparison of different foundation models when evaluating them as deep-research agents. BioMedArena decouples six layers of biomedical agent evaluation -- benchmark loading, tool exposure, tool selection, execution mode, context management, and scoring -- and exposes 147 biomedical benchmarks and 75 biomedical tools across 9 functional families. Adding a new model, benchmark, or tool reduces to registering a few-line provider adapter. We further provide 6 agent harnesses with 6 context-management strategies, which provide 12 backbones with competitive research capabilities and significantly improved performance, achieving state-of-the-art (SOTA) results on 8 representative biomedical benchmarks, with an average lift of +15.03 percentage points over prior SOTA. The toolkit, configurations, and per-task traces are available at https://github.com/AI-in-Health/BioMedArena

</details>


### [121] [Rethinking Adapter Placement: A Dominant Adaptation Module Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06183)
*Suoxin Zhang,Run He,Di Fang,Xiang Tan,Kaixuan Chen,Huiping Zhuang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PAGE is a gradient-based probe that identifies a single dominant adaptation module (a shallow FFN down-projection) where placing a LoRA adapter achieves strong performance with only ~0.7% of vanilla LoRA's trainable parameters, outperforming standard LoRA across multiple tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LoRA methods distribute adapters broadly, leaving the question of where to place a limited number of adapters to maximize performance largely unexplored.

Method: Introduces PAGE (Projected Adapter Gradient Energy), a gradient-based sensitivity probe to estimate trainable gradient energy per candidate LoRA adapter; proposes DomLoRA that places a single adapter at the identified dominant adaptation module.

Result: PAGE reveals high concentration on a single shallow FFN down-projection (dominant adaptation module) across two model families and four tasks. DomLoRA, with ~0.7% of vanilla LoRA's trainable parameters, outperforms vanilla LoRA on instruction following, mathematical reasoning, code generation, and multi-turn conversation, and also improves other LoRA variants.

Conclusion: The dominant adaptation module perspective serves as a practical placement guideline for LoRA, showing that focused adapter placement can dramatically improve parameter efficiency and performance.

Abstract: Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) is a widely used parameter-efficient fine-tuning method that places trainable low-rank adapters into frozen pre-trained models. Recent studies show that using fewer LoRA adapters may still maintain or even improve performance, but existing methods still distribute adapters broadly, leaving where to place a limited number of adapters to maximize performance largely open. To investigate this, we introduce PAGE (Projected Adapter Gradient Energy), a gradient-based sensitivity probe that estimates the initial trainable gradient energy available to each candidate LoRA adapter. Surprisingly, we find that PAGE is highly concentrated on a single shallow FFN down-projection across two model families and four downstream tasks. We term this module the dominant adaptation module and show that its layer index is architecture-dependent but task-stable. Motivated by this finding, we propose DomLoRA, a placement method that places a single adapter at the dominant adaptation module. With only ~0.7% of vanilla LoRA's trainable parameters, DomLoRA outperforms it on average across various downstream tasks, including instruction following, mathematical reasoning, code generation, and multi-turn conversation. This method also improves other LoRA variants, supporting the dominant adaptation module perspective as a practical placement guideline.

</details>


### [122] [Event-Causal RAG: A Retrieval-Augmented Generation Framework for Long Video Reasoning in Complex Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06185)
*Peizheng Yan,Yu Zhao,Liang Xie,Juntong Qi,Mingming Wang,Erwei Yin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Event-Causal RAG is a lightweight retrieval-augmented framework that segments streaming videos into semantically coherent events, models them as structured State-Event-State (SES) graphs, stores them in a dual-store memory, and uses bidirectional retrieval to identify causal event chains for long-video reasoning. It outperforms clip-based baselines on long-video benchmarks, especially for multi-event integration and causal inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing large vision-language models fail at ultra-long/infinite video reasoning due to O(n²) self-attention complexity in end-to-end methods and fragmented clip-level memory with weak temporal-causal modeling in RAG approaches.

Method: (1) Segment streaming videos into semantically coherent events; (2) Represent each event as a State-Event-State (SES) graph capturing state transitions; (3) Merge SES graphs into a global Event Knowledge Graph stored in dual-store memory (semantic matching + causal-topological retrieval); (4) Use bidirectional retrieval to find relevant event causal chains and feed them to a backbone video foundation model for answer generation.

Result: Outperforms clip-based retrieval baselines and long-context video models on long-video understanding benchmarks, particularly on questions requiring multi-event integration and causal inference across long temporal gaps. Also achieves improved memory efficiency and robust streaming performance.

Conclusion: Event-Causal RAG effectively addresses infinite long-video reasoning by introducing event-level semantic segmentation and causal graph structures, enabling coherent memory and efficient causal retrieval over extended video durations.

Abstract: Recent large vision-language models have achieved strong performance on short- and medium-length video understanding, yet they remain inadequate for ultra-long or even infinite video reasoning, where models must preserve coherent memory over extended durations and infer causal dependencies across temporally distant events. Existing end-to-end video understanding methods are fundamentally limited by the $O(n^2)$ complexity of self-attention, while recent retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) approaches still suffer from fragmented clip-level memory, weak modeling of temporal and causal structure, and high storage and online inference costs. We present Event-Causal RAG, a lightweight retrieval-augmented framework for infinite long-video reasoning. Instead of indexing fixed-length clips, our method segments streaming videos into semantically coherent events and represents each event as a structured State-Event-State (SES) graph, capturing the event together with its surrounding state transitions. These graphs are merged into a global Event Knowledge Graph and stored in a dual-store memory that supports both semantic matching and causal-topological retrieval. On top of this memory, we design a bidirectional retrieval strategy to efficiently identify the most relevant event causal chains and provide them, together with the associated video evidence, to a backbone video foundation model for answer generation. Experiments on long-video understanding benchmarks demonstrate that Event-Causal RAG consistently outperforms strong clip-based retrieval baselines and long-context video models, particularly on questions requiring multi-event integration and causal inference across long temporal gaps, while also achieving improved memory efficiency and robust streaming performance.

</details>


### [123] [OPSD Compresses What RLVR Teaches: A Post-RL Compaction Stage for Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06188)
*Jaehoon Kim,Dongha Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OPSD primarily acts as a compression mechanism (not correction) in thinking-enabled mathematical reasoning, shortening responses while preserving accuracy when applied to correct rollouts, but harming accuracy when applied to incorrect ones. A revised pipeline SFT→RLVR→OPSD is proposed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why OPSD's reported accuracy gains shrink or turn negative in thinking-enabled mathematical reasoning, and to investigate whether OPSD functions as a compression or correction mechanism in this setting.

Method: Apply OPSD separately to correct and incorrect rollout groups to isolate compression and correction effects in thinking-enabled mathematical reasoning.

Result: OPSD is a compression mechanism rather than a correction mechanism: training on correct rollouts preserves accuracy while shortening responses; training on incorrect rollouts damages accuracy.

Conclusion: Proposes a revised post-training pipeline: SFT → RLVR → OPSD for thinking-enabled mathematical reasoning.

Abstract: On-Policy Self-Distillation (OPSD) has recently emerged as an alternative to Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR), promising higher accuracy and shorter responses through token-level credit assignment from a self-teacher conditioned on privileged context. However, this promise does not carry over to thinking-enabled mathematical reasoning, where reported accuracy gains shrink and sometimes turn negative. We hypothesize that hindsight supervision can specify better token-level alternatives in short thinking-disabled outputs, but in long thinking-enabled traces it more readily identifies redundancy than supplies better replacements. To test this, we applied OPSD separately to correct and incorrect rollout groups, so that compression and correction can be observed in isolation. Our results show that in thinking-enabled mathematical reasoning, OPSD behaves most reliably as a compression mechanism rather than a correction mechanism: training only on correct rollouts preserves accuracy while substantially shortening responses, whereas training only on incorrect rollouts damages accuracy. In light of these findings, we propose a revised post-training pipeline for thinking-enabled mathematical reasoning: SFT then RLVR then OPSD.

</details>


### [124] [Systematic Evaluation of Large Language Models for Post-Discharge Clinical Action Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06191)
*Shivali Dalmia,Ananya Mantravadi,Prasanna Desikan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper evaluates LLMs for clinical action extraction from discharge notes using a two-stage framework, finding they match supervised models on binary tasks but lag on fine-grained classification, with errors often due to annotation conventions rather than true reasoning failures, highlighting the need for rationale-annotated datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess whether generative LLMs can reliably extract clinical actions from discharge notes in safety-critical, privacy-constrained healthcare settings, and to identify limitations in current evaluation methods due to annotation inconsistencies.

Method: Zero-shot and few-shot LLMs (general-purpose) vs. task-specific supervised BERT-based models are compared on the CLIP discharge-note dataset using a two-stage extraction framework with staged prompting to decompose narrative clinical text into actionable tasks.

Result: LLMs match or exceed supervised models on binary actionability detection, but supervised baselines outperform on fine-grained multi-label category classification. Errors often stem from misalignment between model reasoning and rigid annotation conventions, especially for implicit clinical actions.

Conclusion: Current datasets without rationales conflate clinical reasoning failures with annotation mismatches; advancing clinical NLP requires reasoning-annotated datasets that document why spans are actionable, not just which spans were labeled.

Abstract: The work in this paper evaluates zero-shot and few-shot large language models (LLMs) for safety-critical clinical action extraction using the CLIP discharge-note dataset, with particular emphasis on transitions of care and post-discharge patient safety. To manage the complexity of clinical documentation, we introduce a two-stage extraction framework that decomposes discharge notes, that are written in narrative form, into fine-grained, explicitly actionable clinical tasks through a staged prompting strategy. Our contributions include a systematic assessment of generative LLMs for clinical action extraction, a detailed comparison between general-purpose LLMs and task-specific supervised BERT-based models, and an analysis of annotation inconsistencies across different action categories. We show that contemporary LLMs achieve performance comparable to or exceeding supervised models on binary actionability detection, while supervised baselines retain a meaningful advantage on fine-grained multi-label category classification, despite the absence of task-specific fine-tuning and under strict data-privacy constraints. Qualitative error analysis reveals that many failures stem from misalignment between model reasoning and dataset annotation conventions, particularly in cases involving implicit clinical actions and rigid structural labeling rules. These results indicate that reported performance reflects model limitations due to lack of clinical reasoning, that is not captured by plain annotations. Labels without rationales make it impossible to distinguish clinical reasoning failures from annotation convention mismatches. Advancing clinical NLP requires reasoning-annotated datasets that document why specific spans are actionable, not merely which spans were labeled, enabling proper evaluation of model clinical understanding.

</details>


### [125] [The Granularity Axis: A Micro-to-Macro Latent Direction for Social Roles in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06196)
*Chonghan Qin,Xiachong Feng,Ziyun Song,Xiaocheng Feng,Jing Xiong,Lingpeng Kong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs encode social role granularity (from individual to institutional) as a dominant, causally manipulable latent direction in their representation space, accounting for 52.6% of variance in Qwen3-8B and transferable to Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether LLM internal representations encode the granularity of social roles (micro-level individual experience to macro-level organizational/institutional reasoning), beyond surface-level stylistic features.

Method: Defined a contrast-based Granularity Axis (difference between mean macro- and micro-role hidden states), constructed 75 social roles across five granularity levels, collected 91,200 role-conditioned responses, extracted role-level hidden states, and performed activation steering along the axis.

Result: The Granularity Axis aligns with PC1 (cosine 0.972) explaining 52.6% variance. Role projections increase monotonically across five levels, are stable across layers/prompt variants/splits, transfer to Llama-3.1-8B, and steering causally shifts response granularity (Llama moved from 2.00 to 3.17 on a five-point macro scale).

Conclusion: Social role granularity is a structured, ordered, and causally manipulable latent direction in role-conditioned LLM behavior, not merely a stylistic surface feature.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are routinely prompted to take on social roles ranging from individuals to institutions, yet it remains unclear whether their internal representations encode the granularity of such roles, from micro-level individual experience to macro-level organizational, institutional, or national reasoning. We show that they do. We define a contrast-based Granularity Axis as the difference between mean macro- and micro-role hidden states. In Qwen3-8B, this axis aligns with the principal axis (PC1) of the role representation space at cosine 0.972 and accounts for 52.6% of its variance, indicating that granularity is the dominant geometric axis organizing prompted social roles. We construct 75 social roles across five granularity levels and collect 91,200 role-conditioned responses over shared questions and prompt variants, then extract role-level hidden states and project them onto the axis. Role projections increase monotonically across all five levels, remain stable across layers, prompt variants, endpoint definitions, held-out splits, and score-filtered subsets, and transfer to Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. The axis is also causally relevant: activation steering along it shifts response granularity in the predicted direction, with Llama moving from 2.00 to 3.17 on a five-point macro scale under positive steering on prompts that admit local responses. The two models differ in controllability, suggesting that steering depends on each model's default operating regime. Overall, our findings suggest that social role granularity is not merely a stylistic surface feature, but a structured, ordered, and causally manipulable latent direction in role-conditioned language model behavior.

</details>


### [126] [Towards Annotation-Free Validation of MLLMs: A Vision-Language Logical Consistency Metric](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06201)
*Ying Gu,Mei Chee Leong,Hui Li Tan,Shangbo Mao,Liyuan Li,Nancy Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes VL-LCM, a ground-truth-free evaluation metric for measuring vision-language logical consistency in MLLMs based on sufficient and necessary cause-effect relations, revealing that logical consistency lags behind accuracy improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accuracy-based evaluation rewards unwarranted guessing in Large Language Models and is not applicable to novel tasks lacking ground-truth annotations, motivating the need for alternative validation metrics.

Method: The authors propose VL-LCM (Vision-Language Logical Consistency Metric) grounded in basic logic principles, evaluating sufficient and necessary cause-effect relations on MC-VQA tests and NaturalBench without requiring ground-truth annotations.

Result: Evaluation of 11 open-source MLLMs across 4 families on MMMU and NaturalBench shows that while accuracy has improved, logical consistency significantly lags behind. VL-LCM correlates well with ground-truth metrics and demonstrates reliability for model selection and validation.

Conclusion: Logical consistency can complement accuracy for evaluating both model accuracy and reliability, and VL-LCM is valid for MLLM selection, validation, and answer justification in novel tasks without ground-truth annotation.

Abstract: Dominant accuracy evaluation might reward unwarranted guessing of Large Language Models, and it might not be applicable to novel tasks for model validation without ground-truth (gt) annotation. Based on basic logic principle, we propose a novel framework to evaluate the vision-language logical consistency of MLLMs on both sufficient and necessary cause-effect relations. We define Vision-Language Logical Consistency Metric (VL-LCM) on traditional MC-VQA tests, and recent NaturalBench tests without the need for gt annotation. Through systematic experiments on representative VL benchmark MMMU and recent VL challenges like NaturalBench, we evaluated 11 recent open-source MLLMs from 4 frontier families. Our findings reveal that, despite significant progress of recent MLLMs on accuracy, logical consistency lags behind significantly. Extensive evaluations on the correlations of VL-LCM with metrics on gt, the reliability of LCM, and the relation of VL-LCM with response distribution justify the validity and applicability of VL-LCM even without gt annotation. Our findings suggest that, beyond accuracy, logical consistency could be employed for both accuracy and reliability. VL-LCM can also be employed for MLLM selection, validation, and reliable answer justification in novel tasks without gt annotation.

</details>


### [127] [Beyond Fixed Benchmarks and Worst-Case Attacks: Dynamic Boundary Evaluation for Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06213)
*Haoxiang Wang,Da Yu,Huishuai Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DBE actively locates each LLM's decision boundary (where pass probability ≈ 0.5) to produce a calibrated, globally comparable difficulty scale, avoiding ceiling/floor effects of fixed benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed benchmarks cause ceiling/floor effects that mask LLM capability gaps; the most informative evaluation lies at the boundary where per-prompt pass probability is near 0.5.

Method: (i) Calibrated item bank with per-item difficulty validated across 9 reference LLMs; (ii) Skill-Guided Boundary Search (SGBS) to find boundary items using only API queries; (iii) Adaptive evaluation protocol that places new LLMs on a unified ability scale and grows the item set if under-covered.

Result: Instantiated on safety (harmful request refusal, over-refusal), capability (instruction following), and truthfulness (sycophancy resistance). Covers a broader model spectrum without saturation while remaining compatible with existing datasets.

Conclusion: Dynamic Boundary Evaluation provides a more informative and saturation-resistant LLM evaluation framework by focusing on each model's capability boundary and placing results on a globally comparable difficulty scale.

Abstract: Evaluating large language models (LLMs) today rests on fixed benchmarks that apply the same set of items to any model, producing ceiling and floor effects that mask capability gaps. We argue that the most informative evaluation signal lies at the boundary, where the per-prompt pass probability is near $0.5$ under random-sampling decoding, and propose Dynamic Boundary Evaluation (DBE), which actively locates each model's boundary and places it on a globally comparable difficulty scale. DBE delivers three artifacts: (i) a calibrated item bank covering safety, capability, and truthfulness, with per-item difficulty labels validated across $9$ reference LLMs; (ii) Skill-Guided Boundary Search (SGBS), a search algorithm that finds boundary items for a given target LLM using only API-level query access; and (iii) an evaluation protocol that places a new LLM on a unified ability scale and grows the evaluation set adaptively when the target falls outside the bank's coverage. We instantiate DBE on four categories spanning safety (harmful request refusal and over-refusal), capability (constrained instruction following), and truthfulness (multi-turn sycophancy resistance). The resulting evaluation covers a broader model spectrum without saturation while remaining compatible with existing datasets.

</details>


### [128] [Joint Consistency: A Unified Test-Time Aggregation Framework via Energy Minimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06219)
*Yunzhen Yao,Hongye Wang,Yahong Wang,Michael C. Gastpar,Bo Jiang,Lie He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Joint Consistency (JC), a constrained Ising-type energy minimization framework for test-time aggregation that integrates independent evaluation signals (external fields) with pairwise comparisons (interactions) among candidate reasoning traces, outperforming existing voting/weighted methods on math and code benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing test-time aggregation methods rely on isolated evaluation signals or answer frequencies, ignoring comparative interactions among candidate traces, which limits aggregation quality.

Method: Formulates test-time aggregation as a constrained Ising-type energy minimization problem, where independent evaluation signals serve as external fields and pairwise LLM-as-a-judge comparisons serve as interactions; includes an efficient approximation strategy for large-scale scenarios.

Result: JC consistently outperforms existing baselines across multiple tasks, judge models, trace budgets, and trace-generation settings on math and code reasoning benchmarks.

Conclusion: Joint Consistency provides a unified framework subsuming voting and weighted aggregation methods, with theoretical interpretability under answer-level homogeneity assumptions and practical efficiency via approximation.

Abstract: This paper studies test-time aggregation, an approach that generates multiple reasoning traces and aggregates them into a final answer. Most existing methods rely on evaluation signals collected from candidate traces in isolation or answer frequencies, while ignoring comparative interactions among candidates. We propose Joint Consistency (JC), formulated as a constrained Ising-type energy minimization problem, where independent evaluation signals act as external fields and pairwise comparisons act as interactions. JC provides a unified framework for test-time aggregation that subsumes existing voting and weighted aggregation methods as special cases. Our construction of the interaction matrix leverages LLM-as-a-judge comparisons, and admits a theoretical interpretation under answer-level homogeneity assumptions. Moreover, we develop an efficient approximation strategy that makes interaction modeling practical for large-scale test-time aggregation. Experiments on math and code reasoning benchmarks show that JC consistently outperforms existing baselines across tasks, judge models, trace budgets, and trace-generation settings.

</details>


### [129] [A Versatile AI Agent for Rare Disease Diagnosis and Risk Gene Prioritization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06226)
*Tianyu Liu,Wangjie Zheng,Rui Yang,Benny Kai Guo Loo,Hui Zhang,Jeffries Lauran,Jianlei Gu,Botao Yu,Weihao Xuan,Kexin Huang,Nan Liu,James Zou,Yonghui Jiang,Hua Xu,Hongyu Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Hygieia is a multi-modal AI agent that integrates phenotypic, genetic, and clinical data to improve rare disease diagnosis, outperforming physicians by 12-60% and reducing clinician workload.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current diagnostic workflows for rare diseases suffer from prolonged assessment times and low accuracy, necessitating a more efficient and precise diagnostic approach.

Method: Hygieia employs a router-based, knowledge-enhanced multi-modal AI framework that integrates phenotypic features, genetic profiles, and clinical records, prioritizing risk-related genomic factors for rare diseases and providing confidence scores.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance across multiple diagnostic benchmarks; validated with Yale and Duke-NUS showing 12%-60% improvement over physicians and effective clinician assistance on real-world cases.

Conclusion: Hygieia enhances diagnostic accuracy and interpretability while significantly reducing clinician workload, demonstrating strong potential as a clinical decision support tool.

Abstract: Accurate and timely diagnosis is essential for effective treatment, particularly in the context of rare diseases. However, current diagnostic workflows often lead to prolonged assessment times and low accuracy. To address these limitations, we introduce Hygieia, a multi-modal AI agent system designed to support precision disease diagnosis by integrating diverse data sources, including phenotypic features, genetic profiles, and clinical records. Hygieia features a router-based and knowledge-enhanced framework that mitigates hallucination and tailors diagnostic strategies to different disease categories. Notably, it prioritizes risk-related genomic factors for rare diseases and provides confidence scores to assist clinical decision-making.
